# Caprock — UI

The screens and the visual direction. Every screen answers "what's happening / what does it cost / where do I need to act" ([01-product.md § Product principles](01-product.md#product-principles)). Data comes from the API in [03-contracts.md](03-contracts.md); which screen ships in which phase is in [09-execution-plan.md](09-execution-plan.md).

## Home (default, "the useful waiting screen")

The screen you leave open while Claude works. It was called **Now** until
2026-10-14 (owner, translated: "'Now' as the name of the main button is
depressing — it's not clear that it's the main one, and you're always trying
to get back to the main one"): the nav, the palette, the back links and the
feedback title say *Home*; the route is `#/` (`#/home` for the full screen on
a phone, and `#/now` still opens it); in code the route and the screen keep
the name `now` (`NowScreen`). Elsewhere in these docs "Now" is this screen. In the desktop app's Dashboard
tab (`NowScreen inApp`) it keeps its figures — Today, plan limits, the pulse,
the repos, all time, at a glance, the activity feed, full width — and hides the session
cards, their *show ended* row and the Projects panel: the sidebar beside it
lists the same projects and sessions, and the first tester found the two
"90% the same" (2026-10-09, translated). Hidden, not removed: the browser and
the phone show everything below. Per agent card:

- **Human-readable narration** of current activity ("editing `auth/middleware.go`", "running tests — 2nd attempt"), derived from tool events, not raw logs. The tool-event → human-phrase map is unit-tested (T7).
- **Plan progress** — parsed from TodoWrite/task-list tool events when present; else "N tool calls, M files touched".
- **Live burn**: tokens + $ this task, session context fill %, time since last event.
- **Health badges**: `working / idle / waiting-on-you / looping? / error`.
- One-click actions: open detail, send message, pause/kill.
- **Loop detector**: same tool + similar input ≥ K times in T minutes → banner + optional auto-pause. This is the "session in a loop drains your budget" killer feature. Defaults and rule: [02-architecture.md § Loop detector](02-architecture.md#loop-detector).

At the top of Now, an **attention** strip reports only what deserves interrupting someone: a live loop (with the evidence and what that session has spent so far — read-only tools are excluded, see below), a session that hit an error, and a session that has been waiting for you long enough to be costing you time (default 15 minutes — a question asked two minutes ago is not a problem). **That row carries no detail line**, unlike the others: they show the activity phrase because it is the evidence — an error's phrase names what broke — while a waiting session's phrase is the fixed string "waiting for you", so the row read `Waiting on you · caprock · waiting for you`, the same sentence twice in two prepositions with the repeat greyed as though it were adding something. The title says it once, and "what did it ask?" beside it is the part that tells you anything. **The waiting row is one big way back into that session** (owner, 2026-10-16, translated: "from here I want to go straight to the right terminal with one big button"): a click anywhere on it, Enter on it, or its amber **Open terminal →** at the right goes there. In the desktop app that is the session's tab, brought forward or opened when it has none — a terminal for a session Caprock started, a read-only tab for one another terminal holds (rule 7) — the same `onOpenSession` Home's live rows use (`lib/pipelineHost.ts`). In a browser it is the session's terminal view when Caprock holds its terminal, and otherwise its timeline at the moment it stopped, the button then reading **Open session →**; either way the prompt waiting is at the bottom. *what did it ask?* stays a quiet secondary that opens the answer in place and never jumps, and the project is plain text there rather than a second link. **The row names the session, not only the project** — `caprock-web  Fix the pricing page` — with `sessionTitle` (the user's name for it, else the agent's title, else words from its first prompt), because two rows both reading `caprock-web` did not say which one to answer. Other attention rows keep their small `open`. Cost alone is never a rule: spending is the job. **A "many turns, almost no files touched" rule was removed** — `files_touched` counts only Edit/Write/MultiEdit/NotebookEdit, so every file changed through Bash (`sed -i`, a heredoc, a python one-liner) was invisible to it, and an agent under bypassed permissions is told to prefer exactly those. On the owner's machine it had fired three times ever, on sessions making 636, 418 and 288 Bash calls: all three false, one of them shipping three releases while the banner read "no files touched". It had already been reworded once when the owner could not judge his own flagged session, which was the first sign; a rule whose own comment concedes the product cannot tell investigation from waste is arguing not to interrupt anyone. Counting files out of Bash command text would be guesswork about shell syntax ([rule 6](../CLAUDE.md) prefers no number to an invented one); if it returns it should be built on `internal/gitdiff`, which already knows what a session really changed. Each row carries the measured fact behind it, a one-click way into the session, and a cost — **labelled `session total`, because that is what it is**. The figure is the session's whole spend, and bare beside "Stuck in a loop" it read as the price of the repetition: a $58.85 the loop had not cost. Charging the loop's own window instead does not fix it — measured on a real two-hour loop, its window held $47 of which essentially all was the useful work happening alongside it, so that number would be wrong too and harder to argue with (rule 6). The honest move is to keep the number what it is and say so in the open rather than only on hover. **A loop is a card of its own** (`components/LoopAlertCard.tsx`, words and mute in `lib/loopalert.ts`), the same on Now, in the agent cockpit and on the session page. The row it replaced read "Stuck in a loop · caprock · ran Bash: python3 /private/tmp/…/p2e.py 5× in 3 min · $0.88 in context · $1,886.75 session total · 58m ago · open · this is what a cap stops · a cap that stops this · × · dismiss", and the owner's verdict (2026-10-14, translated) was "it's unclear what to do — many buttons, zero meaning". The card says one sentence — *caprock ran the same command 5 times in 3 minutes (python3 …/p2e.py)*, an absolute path cut to its last segment, the whole input on hover, the span measured from the first to the last call rather than the detector's window — with the context those calls re-read and the session total on a quiet line under it, each named for what it is. Then three actions in this order. **Tell the agent** (primary) opens the message it would send, editable, with **Send to the agent** focused, and sends it through the session's own message queue (`POST /v1/agents/{id}/queue`, ADR-053): typed in at once when the agent is idle, otherwise when its turn ends. The card then says which: **Sent to the agent**, or **Will send when it finishes this turn** (*…once the permission prompt is answered*, *…once its question is answered* when that is what holds it). The text is one line (a newline would submit early), opens with `[Caprock]` and ends "(Sent by Caprock's loop alert — not by the user.)", so the agent does not take a heads-up for an instruction from the person; it carries the exact input, which the agent will recognise, not the shortened one. It is offered only when the session is known to be Caprock's (rule 7) — unknown counts as not owned; on someone's own terminal session **Open session** takes its place with the reason on hover, and inside the session, where there is nowhere to open, the card says it in a line. **Open session** goes to the timeline at the first repeated call. **Dismiss** puts the card away until the detector raises a new alert and never sends anything — a draft not sent is dropped with the card; its ▾ menu holds *Don't warn about this command again in this session* (kept per viewer in `caprock.loop.mute`, keyed by session and input) and **one** quiet *Set a spending cap…*, which lands on the cap on Cost (`#/cost?section=cap`). The two near-identical cap links ("this is what a cap stops", "a cap that stops this") are gone from the card: they were most of its noise, and the cap pauses only sessions Caprock started — 122 of the 127 sessions that did real work on the owner's machine were started by hand. **There is no "all clear" state**: the strip renders nothing when nothing is wrong, because an always-on banner trains people to stop reading the space where the real warning will appear. That leaves one genuine gap — a quiet dashboard and a dead one look identical, since nothing moves in either — and it is answered by the header's connection state (`ConnectionState`, WP-13): `Live` only while something came from the daemon in the last 24 s (its pings arrive every 10), otherwise `Not answering — reconnecting`, `Catching up…`, `Reconnecting (n) · next try in s` or `Offline since hh:mm`. Liveness is a fact about the connection; "all clear" would be a verdict about the machine, and only the first is ours to state. It re-renders on a one-second tick rather than only when a frame arrives: on an idle machine no frame arrives *by definition*, so the old dot froze at `live · now` and kept claiming it for hours, and it said `live` for as long as the socket object was open, which a phone whose network dropped silently kept for minutes — a stale liveness indicator being worse than none, because it is the one people trust. Being expensive is never on its own a reason to fire — spending is the job. Rules are pure and unit-tested in `ui/src/lib/attention.ts`.

Beside the Projects roll-up sits **Live activity** — one column of what every session on the machine is doing, newest first, fed by the same `/v1/live` WebSocket and seeded from recent history so it is never empty on open. Each line is a short past-tense phrase with the repo, an optional monospace detail (a file name, a shortened command), and how long ago it happened; a click opens that session. Only events a human would want to read become lines — successful tool results, assistant turns, cost ticks and mail are dropped, because a feed of raw event kinds is noise and noise is what makes people stop looking. Absolute paths in shell commands collapse to their last two segments so the verb stays visible. A pause control freezes the stream for reading. Phrasing lives in `ui/src/lib/feed.ts` and is unit-tested.

**Panels refetch on live frames, never on top of themselves.** Every panel asks again when a frame arrives (at most every 400ms) and on its own interval; a refetch asked for while the last is still running is sent once, when it lands (`ui/src/lib/useApi.ts`). Without that, a busy session on a large database stacked dozens of the same seconds-long aggregate in the daemon: measured 2026-09-30 on a copy of the owner's database with one tab open, today's summary took 11s and rising to 22s, against 2-5s with the guard.

Below the cards, a **Projects** roll-up answers the question a session list cannot: what each repo costs and who is working in it. One row per project with its measured spend, token total, session count, a **sparkline** of when that spend happened, and a green dot when a session is live in that repo. The range selector (`today` / `7d` / `30d` / `all`) defaults to **30d** — "today" is empty most mornings, and an empty panel on first open reads as a broken feature rather than an honest zero. Data comes from `projects` in `/v1/stats/summary` ([03-contracts.md](03-contracts.md)); every figure is measured at API list price, never modelled.

**Both figures are always shown**, and both are legible: tokens lead the row (17px accent) with cost directly beneath at reading size (13px `text-fg-muted`, the tone used for text meant to be read — `text-fg-faint` is reserved for chrome). Tokens lead because on a flat plan the dollar figure is not money owed but a proxy for consumption, and because the panel header already carries the dollar total. The header states both in the same relationship (`10.00M · $1,000.00 total`), and an expanded directory row states both on one line. There is no control for choosing between them: a toggle would make the reader re-decide which half to see on every visit, and the row has room for two numbers. The sparkline, the shared ceiling and the fallback share bar are all scaled on **tokens**, matching the figure that leads the row — a picture scaled on cost under a tokens headline would be a second, silently different ranking, and the two orderings genuinely differ because a cheap model burns tokens cheaply. Both series still travel in the payload (`spark.cost` and `spark.tokens`); the client simply plots one.

Each repository row **expands into its directories**, and the breakdown is charged by **which files Claude touched** — not by the directory a session was launched from. Nobody opens a terminal in `/services/api` to work on it; they open the repository root and let Claude edit across services, so the old cwd-keyed rows answered "where was the terminal" and only one repository on the owner's machine expanded at all. Rows read as paths from the root (`/`, `/services/api`), sorted by cost.

The breakdown is a **tree, one level at a time**. It was a flat list of full paths: **43 rows** at depths 0 to 4 on the owner's `caprock` — `/ui/src/components`, `/ui/src/screens`, `/ui/src/lib`, `/ui/src`, `/internal/store`, `/.ai`, `/` — with nothing on screen saying the first three live inside `/ui`. Sorted by cost it answered "what is most expensive"; it could not answer "what does `/ui` cost", which is the question a monorepo asks, and at 43 rows the eye found neither. Expanding now shows the **9 rows** directly under the root, each carrying its subtree's total; each expands again, to a cap of **three levels** — `/ui/src/screens` is the deepest row reachable.

- **The cap is a display choice, so the tree is built in the client** (`ui/src/lib/pathtree.ts`) from the flat list the daemon already sends. The payload carries cost, tokens and turns per path, so the tree is arithmetic over data already on the wire; putting the cap in the API would make every future consumer inherit one panel's layout budget and would turn a stylesheet-sized decision into a contract change. The flat list stays the single truth, so the two can never disagree.
- **Spend deeper than the cap is charged to the deepest visible row, and the row says so** — `+1 deeper` beside the path, with the count on hover. It cannot be dropped (the parts would stop summing to the whole) and it cannot have a row (that is what the cap means), so the only honest option left is to absorb it visibly. A number that quietly swallows its children is exactly what rule 6 exists to prevent.
- **A parent's own spend is stated apart from the roll-up.** The row's figures are the **subtree** — `/ui` reads $390.74, everything at or below it — and when work touched a file directly in `/ui` a sub-line says `$0.39 here · $390.35 in 1 subdirectory`. Without it a reader cannot tell "this directory cost X" from "this subtree cost X", which are the same number only on a leaf.
- **A pass-through directory collapses into its child** — `/ui/src/lib` and `/ui/src/components` under a `/ui/src` that has no spend of its own show as one `/ui/src` row, not two clicks. The collapse is **conditional**: a directory with spend of its own never merges, because that would move real money into a directory the work did not happen in.
- **The repository root is not a row of the tree.** Every path is under `/`, so a tree rooted there would put one click between the reader and everything they came for, on every repository. Its children are the first level, and the root's own spend (`README.md`, `Makefile`) becomes a leaf row beside them, still written `/`.

**Nothing in the breakdown navigates.** A row is a figure, not a link — the only interactive thing is its expander, which is the same chevron the repository row uses. The rows were never links, but expanding a repository injected dozens of them, grew the panel far past the **Live activity** feed beside it, and the next click landed on a feed row — every one of which *is* a link to a session. The owner clicked a directory and arrived at a session in a **different repository**, unable to tell what he was looking at. A click that lands a user somewhere they cannot orient is worse than a row that does nothing, so the fix is both halves: fewer rows to scan, and rows that are inert and look inert.

Attribution **carries forward**: a turn counts toward the directory of the **most recent file it touched**, and keeps counting there until it touches a file somewhere else. Work happens in stretches — after "finish /app" Claude edits, runs the tests, reads the output, greps, edits again — so the commands, tests and searches between two edits count toward the directory being worked on. Reading, editing or writing a file counts as touching it; running a command does not. Each turn's cost goes **whole** to one row, never split between two — a split would need a model of how much of a turn each file deserved, and no such measurement exists — so the parts still sum exactly to the repository total (rule 6). The rule is stated on hover, because this is a **stated rule, not measured file-by-file attribution**, and the difference is the whole of its honesty.

This **replaced a strict rule** that charged a directory only when every file a turn touched was in it. That rule was exact and useless: it counted only the minutes containing a direct file edit and discarded the hour of commands around them, putting **87.6%** of the owner's monorepo into "repository-wide work". The user asked what a service cost and seven eighths of the answer was "we could not tell". Under carry-forward the same repository reads `/app` **61.0% ($2090.67)**, `/.ai` 6.8%, `/app/tests` 2.8% (measured 2026-08-22, all time).

**Two rows are not directories.** They sit under a small-caps `not a directory` heading and each carries its own explanation on hover. They used to render in **italic**, which separated them from the table by making them look like a different product — a slanted face the panel has nowhere else reads as an error state or as pasted-in text, not as a row of the same table. They are siblings of the directory rows: their spend is real and part of the repository total. So they now read as table rows — same 12px, same tone, same alignment, same right-edge numerals — and are separated by **role** instead: the heading says what they are once, and the path column drops its **monospace**, which is the difference that is actually true (`mono` in this panel means "a literal string from the machine"). Neither is called "unattributed": that word describes caprock's bookkeeping rather than the user's work, and reads as "the tool failed to figure this out".

- **`outside the repository`** — the larger of the two, **25.8%** of the owner's monorepo and **28.7%** of `caprock` (measured 2026-08-22). This is not other people's work: it is work on *this* project whose files live elsewhere — Claude's notes under `~/.claude/projects/<project>/memory`, agent scratchpads under the per-session temp directory, the e2e run's output directory, occasionally another checkout. It counts toward the repository total but is charged to no directory inside it, because folding it into the root would claim work happened in the checkout that did not.
- **`repository-wide work`** — now small and narrow: a session's **opening turns, before Claude had touched any file**, so there is no directory yet for them to count toward ($2.39 of $3426 in the monorepo). It is usually absent entirely; a bucket row that cost $0 is omitted rather than rendered as a puzzling `$0.00`.

Each directory row states **tokens, its share, and cost** — all three for the **subtree**. The share is a percentage of the **repository total including both non-directory rows**, so the column sums to 100% and the part that belongs to no one directory is visible as its own number instead of inflating the directories that could; the breakdown header says this in words, because a percentage whose base the reader has to guess is exactly what rule 6 exists to prevent. It is a share of **tokens** and sits beside the token figure rather than between the two numbers, where it would read as qualifying both; the cost share is on hover, since two competing percentages side by side would reintroduce the ambiguity. A subtree's share is **summed from the shares the daemon sent**, never recomputed: the server divides by a total the panel does not have, and recomputing would silently substitute a different base for a percentage the contract describes. Percentages **floor** (`fmtPct`) — 99.6% is 99%, and nothing reads as 100% until it is — with one exception: a row with real spend whose share floors to zero shows `<0.1%`, because a bare "0%" beside a non-zero dollar figure states two contradictory things about the same money.

The sparkline **replaced** a share-of-the-largest bar, which restated the ranking the sorted numbers already gave — the widest bar sat on the top row by construction — and so spent the row's only free horizontal space on information the reader had. When the spend happened is in no number on the row: $40 in one afternoon and $40 across three weeks are the same row until you draw them. One column per bucket, following the range (`30d` → 30 days, `7d` → 7, `today` → 24 hours), so the picture and the number beside it always describe the same period. Every row is scaled to one shared ceiling, so heights are comparable between rows; an empty bucket is drawn as a hairline rather than left blank, the way the live pulse draws a silent minute, because a gap reads as a rendering fault while a flat floor reads as silence. `range=all` sends no series and the row keeps the share bar. Model in `ui/src/lib/spark.ts`, unit-tested and kept separate from the canvas that paints it.

### A card says what its session was about

Every card carries one line under its header naming what the session was
about (FB-035): the name the user gave it, when they renamed it from its tab
or sidebar row ([21-app.md](21-app.md) § What the user sees), shown as a
title is; else the agent's own title — Claude Code's `ai-title`, the name its
own `/resume` picker shows, or Codex's or OpenCode's thread name — in the
foreground colour, or else the first
prompt that says something, muted and in quotation marks, because it is a
quotation rather than a name. Without it a list of ended sessions was a list of
identical rows: the same project, a short id, and "ended". Sessions with
neither (a `say ok` probe, a `/clear` and nothing after) show no line rather
than a guess. The rule for which prompt counts lives in
[03-contracts.md](03-contracts.md).

### Ended cards, and finding one

An ended card says **worked**, with the span it was worked in and how long ago
— "29 Sep 14:02–16:40 · 17h ago" — not the last narrated state, which read
"waiting at the prompt" on a session that stopped weeks earlier, and not when it
was closed: after a reboot every card read the reboot's time (FB-037). The close
time is on hover. It carries **continue**, or a muted **can't continue**
with the reason on hover (see § Continue, and why not). With "show ended
sessions" ticked, a search box finds a session by what it was about, any prompt
typed in it, its project or branch (searched on the server, so every session
counts, not only the loaded page), and **show N more** pages past the first
200.

### Interrupted by a restart

When Caprock (or the machine) last stopped with sessions running and they were
gone when it came back, a banner at the top of Now says so — "2 sessions were
still running when Caprock last stopped · 13h ago" — and lists each by project
and description with **continue** (FB-038). It is dismissed per stop, in the
browser, and a session drops out once continued. Checked on a copy of the
owner's database with a simulated stop: it named exactly the two sessions closed
at that moment and none of the live ones.

### One card per conversation

`/clear` and a fork each start a new session id, so one conversation in one
terminal became a row of cards (FB-039: "four sessions on one repo, and I open
two at most"). An ended session another listed session continues is folded into
that one, which says "continues N earlier sessions" with each earlier part as a
link. The link is recorded, never guessed (see `parent_session` in
[03-contracts.md](03-contracts.md)).

## The agent switch

`all / claude / opencode / gemini / codex / deepseek / hermes`, in the middle of the Today
header, showing only the agents this machine runs, when it runs more than one. It applies to the whole screen — today's totals, the live pulse, the
activity feed, the projects list and the session cards — because a filtered
list beside an unfiltered total is how a reader ends up quoting a number that
means something other than what the heading says.

Three decisions worth keeping:

- **OpenCode, Codex, DeepSeek Harness and Hermes Agent appear when the daemon
  reports it is reading them; Gemini when a Gemini session is in the list.** Neither the
  session list nor a day's summary can answer the first on their own: the
  screen fetches only live sessions unless "show ended" is ticked, and a
  machine's OpenCode or Codex history is usually all ended and older than
  today — so both are legitimately empty on exactly the machines that need the
  control. Gemini is judged by its sessions instead, because the `gemini`
  binary on PATH says nothing about whether any Gemini session exists.
- **`all`, not `both`.** Written when there were two agents, on the reasoning
  that a third would make `both` a lie — and then Gemini arrived and it would
  have. It is the part of this control a reader sees every time.
- **The selection is a filled pill, at 12px.** At 11px in a bordered strip the
  active state was a slightly lighter grey cell, and a control whose state has
  to be squinted at is one people misread — this one changes what every figure
  on the screen means.

## Today's trends

Each Today cell carries a faint line of the last 14 days and today, and one
word against the viewer's own median (`TodayTrend.tsx`, `lib/trend.ts`, from
`/v1/stats/trends`; owner, 2026-10-14, translated: "I see the number but don't
understand whether it's average or above average"):

- **Cost, sessions, turns are cut at the same time of day.** Each previous
  day is what it had reached by now's clock time, so the comparison is
  "by this time on a usual day". Against whole days every morning read low.
- **The usual is the median of the days with work.** A day with no priced
  turn is drawn but not counted: a weekend off is not a usual of zero.
  Fewer than five such days and the word is *not enough history*.
- **Burn now is set against the median active 10-minute window** of the 14
  days — any window that spent anything — the same width as the figure. Its
  line is each day's own median window; *idle now* when nothing runs.
- **Cache hit gets the line only.** It sits near 98% every day; a word under
  it would be news about nothing. Its line spans its own range, not zero up.
- **The words:** *usual* within ±15% (the cockpit's band, `lib/usual.ts`),
  *above usual +38%*, *below usual −20%*, and *above usual 2.4×* past double.
  Hover says the basis in full, starting *vs your 14-day median*; each day of
  the line has its own tooltip.
- **Calm.** Faint ink for the line, a dashed hairline for the usual, a dot for
  today; muted words, the accent only for *above*, never red or green. A busy
  day is not a fault.
- **No jump.** The block keeps its height before the answer lands, and the
  line's today point is the live figure printed above it, so the two never
  disagree. It follows the agent switch and folds with the panel.

Rows from an agent other than Claude Code carry that agent's mark
(`AgentGlyph`, `AgentMarks.tsx`). Only those: on most machines every row is
Claude Code, so marking those too would put a glyph on every line, and the
mark exists to answer "why is this one different". A project worked on with
more than one carries no mark, because claiming any one of them would be
wrong.

**Agent marks.** One glyph names the agent everywhere it is shown — the app
sidebar's rows, the tab strip, the Projects panel, the
pulse, the Now cards and the tray panel (owner, 2026-10-09: "replace CL, Cdx
and so on with the corresponding icon", translated). It replaced text
monograms (`Cl`, `Cx`, `$` in the app; `oc`, `gem`, `cdx`, `dsh` on the
dashboard), which had to be read rather than recognised.

- **The marks.** Claude Code is the Claude spark, Gemini the Gemini sparkle,
  OpenCode its frame, DeepSeek its whale: path data copied from Simple Icons
  16.34.0 (CC0 1.0), inlined in `AgentMarks.tsx` with the source in a
  comment; nothing is fetched and no icon package ships.
- **Codex keeps its original identity** (owner, 2026-10-18): our `C` in a
  rounded square as its small glyph, and the cream terminal character with
  a `>_` face. The glyph is drawn from the same stroked path in the DOM and
  on the share card. See [ADR-080](08-decisions.md#adr-080--codex-keeps-its-original-glyph-and-character-chatgpt-uses-the-openai-blossom).
- **ChatGPT imports use OpenAI's Blossom**, replacing the generic speech
  bubble. The unchanged path comes from the OpenAI logo pack, with its source
  and rules in `OpenAIBlossom.ts`. It stays pure black on light palettes and
  pure white on dark ones (`--agent-chatgpt`), with no face, tint or added
  element. Codex and ChatGPT remain distinct in a list.
- **Hermes Agent** has no licensed mark: it is an H with a wing on each post, drawn here, in a
  teal of our own (`--agent-hermes`, #2f9e86 in the same 70/30 mix; lowest
  3.47:1, on Paper, measured 2026-10-15 over the palette tokens with the WCAG
  formula). Its character on the Week card and Second Brain is a teal
  messenger with two small wings, drawn the same way ([ADR-069](08-decisions.md)). A shell is the
  `TerminalIcon` prompt; an agent the UI does not know is a dot.
- **One colour rule.** A mark wears its brand colour mixed 70/30 with
  `--color-fg-muted` (`--agent-claude`, `--agent-gemini`, `--agent-deepseek`
  in `tokens.css`), which lowers the saturation, follows the theme and
  clears 3:1 against every surface of all seven palettes (lowest: Claude's
  on Book, 3.28:1, measured 2026-10-11) — the pure colours do not on the
  light grounds. A brand whose colour is black
  (OpenCode) and our own glyphs are the muted ink; ChatGPT's Blossom is the
  one exception, pure black or white, above.
- **Same box.** 17 × 15 px, the monogram's, with a 13 px mark, so no row or
  tab changes height. Each glyph is `role="img"` with the agent's name as its
  label and title (*Claude Code*, *Codex*, *Gemini CLI*, *OpenCode*,
  *DeepSeek*, *Hermes*, *Shell*).

## At a glance

A collapsible block on Now, under the pulse and above All time (swapped on
the owner's word, 2026-10-11: the pictures first, the exact table after), that draws the all-time figures
as three donuts and an agents row (`ui/src/components/AtAGlance.tsx`, data
from `/v1/history?range=all` and `GET /v1/glance`):

- **Where the money went** — cost by model, the top four by name and the rest
  as "other"; the centre is the leader's share.
- **The bill by token type** — *cache read*, *cache write*, *output* and
  *input*, each priced at the model's current list price. The legend glosses
  two of them on a second, quieter line (*re-reading context*, *uncached*).
  The centre is the cache-read share, labelled as re-reading context: the
  same figure the context tax line gives in dollars, drawn as the part of
  the bill it is.
- **Most-used tools** — calls by tool, top five and "other".
- **Agents** — one tile per agent with its character (the Week card's),
  turns, cost and a ring of its share of all-time cost. Claude Code's
  subagents get their own tile ("1,647 subagents"), because on the owner's
  machine they were 13% of the cost and a different kind of work. The ring is
  labelled **of spend** beside it, with a tooltip giving both figures ("81%
  of all-time spend across every agent — $12,250 of $15,043"): the owner
  read a bare "81%" and could not tell of what (2026-10-04). Tiles are at
  reading size — a 48 px character, 14 px name, 12.5 px figures — and at
  phone width the cost wraps under the turns instead of running into the
  ring.

A **Charts | Numbers** switch in the header turns every donut into a table of
the same rows, for reading exact figures and for screen readers (each donut
also carries an `aria-label` listing its segments). The open/closed state and
the view are remembered per browser (`caprock-glance-open`,
`caprock-glance-view`; storage failures fall back to open charts). The SVG is
drawn by hand — no chart library.

Nothing in a donut is clipped with an ellipsis (owner, 2026-10-12: on a
14-inch MacBook the legend read "cach…" and the centre "re-reading cont…"):

- **Layout by container.** The three donuts sit side by side only when the
  panel is at least 720px wide; narrower (the app with its sidebar and
  inspector open) they stack. Each donut is a container too: under 400px
  its legend goes under the chart at full width, from 400px beside it.
- **Wrapping, not clipping.** Legend labels and the centre caption wrap; the
  caption takes a second line inside the ring.
- **Hover and focus, in place.** Hovering a slice or a legend row, or
  focusing a row (each is a tab stop), lights that slice, dims the others
  and puts its share, name and figure in the centre. No tooltip or popup.
  The same centre behaviour holds for every donut (`ui/src/components/Donut.tsx`),
  including the Cost and Lifetime ones that have a table beside them.

**Dynamics, without a graph** (owner, 2026-10-17, translated: "I only need
the rough dynamics, no graphs"). The block is all time, so the change is read
from `GET /v1/glance`'s `trend`: the last 7 days against the 7 days before,
two fixed rolling windows ending now:

- **A marker after every share.** Each legend row of the three donuts, each
  row of the Numbers tables (a `7d` column) and each agent tile or folded
  agent carries the change in that row's *share* between the two windows, in
  whole points: `↑ 9`, `↓ 4`, or `·` under one point. "other" is everything
  the named rows are not, in each window. Neutral ink, not ok/warn colours:
  more of a model or a tool is neither good nor bad. The hover says it in
  words ("Opus 5.5: 31% of spend in the last 7 days, 22% in the 7 days
  before"), and screen readers get the same sentence.
- **Nothing when there is nothing to compare.** With either window empty the
  block has no trend column at all and lays out exactly as it did before.
- **One sentence under Where the money went**, only when a model's share of
  spend moved five points or more: "Opus 5.5 is gaining: 31% of the last 7
  days' spend vs 22% the 7 before; Opus 5 fell to 38% from 70%." Measured
  shares only; it never projects when one model overtakes another.

## Hooks banner

Shown on Now when Claude Code's settings file lacks some of Caprock's hook
events (`/v1/status.hooks.missing`). One line says what it means — activity
still arrives from transcripts, a few seconds late; hooks make it live — and
**Install hooks** fixes it: `POST /v1/hooks/install` runs the code behind
`caprock hooks install` and the banner reports what the file holds
afterwards. On success it reads "Hooks installed ✓": sessions started from
now on report live, including resumed ones, and a session already running
keeps the hooks it started with until it is restarted — Claude Code loads
hooks when a session starts. The command stays as a secondary "or run in a
terminal" line, and the banner names the settings file it checked, so a
preview daemon with a temporary HOME is obvious. Dismissing it (×) is
remembered per browser, keyed on the exact missing set
(`caprock-hooks-banner-dismissed`), so it returns only when that set changes.

## Live pulse

One track per session on Now, one bar per minute of the last hour. The bar's height is how much happened in that minute and its colour is what it cost — green a cheap minute, amber typical, bright an expensive one — so the shape of a track is the shape of the work: a task that ramped and finished draws a bell, steady grinding a plateau, working in bursts a comb. Nothing is decorative; there is no motion that does not correspond to a recorded event.

The state on the right comes from the daemon's narrator, not from the bars. The bars describe the past hour; health describes this moment, and they answer different questions — a track that reads "working" while Claude sits waiting for you to type is worse than one that says nothing. A high repeat count still wins over health, because it is the one thing the bars know that health does not.

Clicking a minute opens the session **at that minute** — the timeline scrolls to those events and marks them with an accent rule. Landing at the top of a thousand-event session with no indication of what you clicked is the same as not having clicked. The route carries the instant (`#/session/{id}?at=<unix-ms>`), so the position is shareable and survives a reload.

Hovering a track names the minute under the pointer and what happened in it — event count, model turns, cost — including "nothing" for a silent minute, since "was anything happening here?" is a real question about a flat stretch.

Under the cost, a session row says **what state it is in and who is
working**: the state as a small coloured pill (working green, waiting or
repeating amber, error red, idle neutral), then the session's character with
a crowd beside it when subagents are live, the count in words ("4 agents" —
main thread plus each live subagent) and the model's display name ("Opus
5.5") at normal contrast. The first version drew 15 px icons, a faint "×4"
and a grey model, and the owner could not read it (2026-10-04). At phone
width the model gives way, and the chart takes its own line under the row
rather than shrinking to a sliver between the name and the agents. Live
subagents are the daemon's `live_subagents` (see
[03-contracts.md](03-contracts.md#at-a-glance-get-v1glance)).

**The money is one column.** Each row is its own grid, and its tracks after
the name are fixed widths — chart, then the hour's cost (right-aligned,
tabular figures), then the state and agents — so the charts are one width
and the dollars line up down the panel. The agents column used to be
`auto`, sized by each row's model name and crowd icon, and the owner's
screenshot (2026-10-15) had `$28.98`, `$0.03` and `$6.53` at three
different x positions.

**A row is named after its session.** The name leads in bold — the one the
user gave it, else the agent's title, else a few words of its first prompt
(`sessionTitle`, the sidebar's) — and the project, branch, short id and what
it is doing are the line under it, the full path on hover. Two rows used to
read "dev/caprock master" and nothing else (owner, 2026-10-14).

**Rows keep their places** (`lib/pulseOrder.ts`; owner, 2026-10-14,
translated: "the order jumps by activity — not allowed … fixed position, by
who was added when … plus let me swap them"). Sorted by the newest event,
two busy sessions swapped every few seconds. A row is placed by the session
it shows, never by what happens in it:

- **By hand first.** Drag a row onto another, or ⌥⇧↑ ⌥⇧↓ (Ctrl+Shift off
  macOS) on its chevron; kept per viewer (`caprock.pulse.order` in
  localStorage).
- **Then the tab strip.** Sessions open as tabs in the app follow the
  strip's order among themselves, in the slots their rows already held.
- **Then start time**, then the id: a new session lands last, an ended one
  leaves no gap.

Which sessions get a track (at most six, the most recently active when more
run) is still chosen by activity; where each sits is not.

**Two sessions in one checkout** (`lib/overlap.ts`, `GET /v1/overlap`;
owner, 2026-10-14: two sessions on dev/caprock master, both working, "a
decision is needed"). Caprock does not block it — sometimes it is what
someone wants — but shows it:

- **Same checkout** — the same clone or the same linked worktree, not two
  worktrees of one repository, which edit different files on disk and get
  nothing — puts a quiet chip under each row: *shares checkout with
  Router*.
- **Files edited by both** in the last 30 minutes (Edit, Write, MultiEdit,
  NotebookEdit) is the real risk, one agent writing over the other's
  change: the chip turns amber, *2 files touched by both*. Hovering or
  focusing it opens a card (`OverlapLine.tsx`) naming the sessions that
  edited them and listing the files, relative to the checkout — a card,
  not the browser's tooltip, which waits a second and cuts a long list. A
  third session in the folder that touched none of them is not named.
- **Move to a worktree…** beside the chip opens the New session dialog on
  the checkout with *Git worktree* filled from the newer session's name —
  of the two that collide, when files are shared.
- **A drag selects no text**: the press that starts it is the row's, so the
  names under the pointer are not highlighted on the way down.
  Nothing moves by itself: the running session keeps going until its user
  stops it, and Caprock never types into it (CLAUDE.md rule 7).
- **Cheap.** The panel asks only while it is unfolded and only when two of
  its tracks share a repository or folder at all, at the slow live pace
  (10 s), shared with every other reader; the daemon reads the live
  sessions and, only for sessions sharing a checkout, one query on
  `session_files`. The agent cockpit shows the same chip, one line under
  the loop warning.

**A row opens in place.** Clicking it (or its chevron) expands the project
under it rather than navigating: today's cost, tokens and sessions for the
project (from `/v1/stats/summary?range=today`, matched on the project's
directory), every live session in it, and the ways in. Which rows are open
is remembered per browser (`caprock-pulse-open`, by session id). Clicking a
minute on the chart still opens the session at that minute.

The opened row is **calm** (owner, 2026-10-14, translated: "lots of bright
buttons here; it destroys attention … as neat as possible"):

- **The ways in are quiet words** at the end of the summary line, not a row
  of two filled and two outlined buttons: the project's terminal action and
  its `⋯` menu (the Projects panel's `ProjectTerminal`, `quiet`), **Open
  repo ↗** (`RepoButtons`, `quiet`) and **Open session**. `QUIET` in
  `ProjectTerminal.tsx` is the style: muted words, a panel-2 wash on hover,
  no fill and no border. The Projects panel and the session page keep their
  loud buttons.
- **Each live session is two lines, two columns.** Left: character, agent,
  model, state pill, live subagents; under it what it is doing, its plan
  (*plan 3/7*), how long ago, and *open*. Right, right-aligned in the space
  that used to be empty: what the session has cost, what its last hour cost
  (*$0.84/h*, from its own track's bars, so only for a session with a
  track), how long it has run, its context fill (amber from 85%); under it
  the branch, its changes since the trunk, committed or not (*+123 −40*,
  `GET /v1/sessions/{id}/diff`), its pull request and that PR's CI in a
  word (`GET /v1/sessions/{id}/ci`, the deck's answer: the session's own
  PR record when GitHub is not connected, and then no CI). The two are
  asked only while the row is open, once a minute. On a phone the figures
  go under the session, left-aligned. In a browser *open* is a link to the
  session's page, as before.
- **In the desktop app a live session's row takes you into it** (owner,
  2026-10-15, translated: "From here I want to drop into the session
  itself — the same running one — with one button"; `lib/sessionJump.ts`).
  The app hands the row what a browser tab lacks through `PipelineHost`
  (`onOpenSession`, `tabDigitOf`, `onEndSession`, `onStartAgent`):
  - *A tab shows the session* — a terminal Caprock holds, or a read-only
    tab opened earlier: the whole row is the click target and brings that
    tab forward, as its ⌘N does. *Go to session ↗* with the tab's key
    (through `keys()`, so Ctrl+Shift+N off macOS) shows on hover and focus;
    the row is focusable and Enter goes in.
  - *Caprock started it and holds its terminal, no tab yet*: the same, and
    the click opens its tab.
  - *Started in the user's own terminal* (or Caprock's, with its terminal
    gone): nothing to go into — Caprock never types into or signals a
    process it did not start (rule 7). The row is not a jump target; it
    says *in your terminal* (*no terminal* for a detached one, the reason on
    hover) and offers *Continue in Caprock* for a Claude Code session: a
    second process on a copy of the conversation (`POST /v1/agents` with
    `resume` and `fork`, the same as the session page's *Continue here*),
    opened in a new tab, the hover saying the terminal's process keeps
    running untouched.
  - *details* stays on every row, a quiet link to the session's page.
  - **× End** (owner, translated: "if a process is idle, let me close it
    with one small button") appears only on a session Caprock started that
    sits at its prompt — health *idle*, or *waiting for you* after a turn;
    never while it works, shows a permission dialog or a question, or has
    subagents running. It ends the session the way *Close and end the
    agent* does (the cockpit's `kill`), closing a tab that shows only it.
    It asks first only when messages wait in its queue (ADR-053) —
    *N queued messages will be dropped. End anyway · Keep*. A session
    another terminal started never gets End: ADR-059's stop is the
    doctor's, for abandoned processes it lists, which a live session at a
    terminal never is; the hover on *in your terminal* says to end it there.
- **The legend folds away.** It shows until the viewer presses *Hide*, then
  the **?** beside "last 60 minutes" brings it back; kept per viewer
  (`caprock-pulse-legend`).

On the right, **×N same call** counts the most-repeated identical tool call within six minutes, using the same signature as the loop detector. It is worded as the measurement rather than a verdict, because polling a file with repeated Reads looks exactly like being stuck and only the person working knows which it was. Placeholder calls (`Bash true` and its cousins) are dropped outright — an agent makes them by the hundred and counting them reported every session as looping.

Colour encoded turns-vs-tool-calls first, and that was meaningless: the ratio sits near 1:2 almost every minute, so two near-identical minutes came out different colours and the legend explained a distinction nobody could see. Cost per minute varies eight-fold across an hour on a real session, which is worth noticing. A minute with no events draws a hairline rather than nothing, because a gap in a bar chart reads as a rendering fault while a flat floor reads as silence — and the legend shows the colours as chips rather than naming them in prose.

**The canvas repaints only when the data changes, never on a timer.** The first version ran a `requestAnimationFrame` loop that repainted every frame and visibly shimmered; a dashboard that flickers while you read it is worse than a still one.

The model (`ui/src/lib/pulse.ts`) is pure and unit-tested apart from the drawing, so a wrong bar can be told from a wrong pixel.

## Repos on Now

Under Live pulse, a foldable **Repos** table (`components/ReposPanel.tsx`,
`Panel fold="now.repos"`, the folded state kept per viewer with the other
panels'): one row per listed repository with a session running in it
(`GET /v1/repos/activity`, [03-contracts.md](03-contracts.md)). Owner,
2026-10-13, translated: "on this page can we add statistics for the git
repos?"

- **From git, always** — the name with a green dot (hover: how many sessions
  run in it), under it *last commit 9m ago* and *· N uncommitted* in amber
  when the working tree has changes (the line wraps rather than cut the
  count off); commits a day over 14 days as bars (today's lighter,
  hover says each day), today's and the last 7 days' commits large, and the
  lines added and removed in those 7 days. Every branch, merges left out.
  The lines end in a faint *…* while some commits' lines are still being
  counted (`lines_partial`; hover says so): a big repository's first read
  counts its commits at once and its lines over the next minutes.
  *git could not be read* means listing the commits failed; the hover, and
  the opened row, give git's own words.
- **From GitHub, only as the deck read it** — open pull requests with the
  merged count of 7 days, the share of red runs with the median successful
  run of the slowest workflow, and the newest release tag with its age. A
  part not read yet is a dash whose hover says where it is read (the deck's
  Pipeline, its Release view); the section never asks GitHub itself.
  While GitHub is not connected the three cells are one line, *GitHub not
  connected · Settings*, and the local figures stay; a remote that is not
  GitHub says *not on GitHub*.
- **A row opens under itself** (owner, 2026-10-16, translated: let the Repos
  row expand to show a bit more per repository) — the chevron before its
  name, or *More* / *Less* beside the *Repository* heading for every row;
  the rows opened are kept per viewer (`caprock.repos.open`, by root). The
  opened part is one wrapping line of labelled facts across the whole row,
  only what the answer already carries: *Branch* (checked out at the root,
  *detached* when none), *Last CI* (the newest run of the kept runs list —
  *passed*, *failed*, *running* — its workflow, branch and age, opening the
  run on GitHub), *Pull requests* (open, merged in 7 days), *CI red · 14d*
  (share, runs, median), *Release*, *Lines · 7d*, *Folder*, and *Git* with
  the full error when git could not be read. On a phone it is where the
  figures the columns drop are read.

Folded, the body is not mounted and nothing is asked; open, it asks every
minute while the window is visible and keeps its last answer in
`lib/deckCache` (`repos:now`), and the daemon reads git at most every 10
minutes a repository (every minute while lines are still being counted). A
container query drops the bars and the lines below
980px and the GitHub cells below 600px, so a phone reads the name, today and
the week.

## CI/CD on Now

Under Repos, a foldable **CI/CD** section (`components/CICDPanel.tsx`,
`Panel fold="now.cicd"`, words in `lib/reposci.ts`; `GET /v1/repos/ci`,
[03-contracts.md](03-contracts.md)). Owner, 2026-10-14, translated: "find a
place on the dashboard for CI/CD indicators and metrics — costs, time,
possible GitHub outages, or something stuck … the core is the release: a
team lead wants to understand where the money for tokens goes, how much each
PR costs, who pushes there — literally face and name."

- **The Pipeline** (owner, 2026-10-15, translated: "so that metrics from it
  can be harmoniously put into the Dashboard"; `components/PipelineCard.tsx`)
  — at the head of the section, not as a second CI block beside it: the
  first five of the Pipeline page's figures for the repository the Pipeline
  shows (`pickPipeline` in `lib/pipeline.ts`: the one shown last, else one
  with an agent running) — *In flight*, *Needs a fix* (red when any),
  *Ready* (green when any), *Throughput · 14d*, *CI median* — as the same
  Stat tiles (`FlowFigures compact`), each with its last 14 days and today
  against them where it has a history (*In flight*, *Throughput*, *CI
  median*; § Pipeline, *The figures in dynamics*), the repository's name and
  *Open Pipeline →* to `#/pipeline?repo=…` (in the app, the Pipeline tab).
  It reads the page's own answer
  (`usePipelineStats`, `…/ci/stats`, kept as `stats:<repository>`), so it
  draws the page's last copy at once, and asks only while this section is
  unfolded and Home is in front — the page is hidden then and asks nothing,
  so the two never poll together. Not drawn without an agent session in a
  repository, or while GitHub is not connected.
- **GitHub's status** — one line on top from githubstatus.com: a green dot
  and *GitHub operational*, or amber/red with the degraded parts (*GitHub:
  Actions degraded*) and the first open incident; the hover lists every
  part and incident, the link opens the incident or the status page.
- **A block per repository** (the listed ones on GitHub with activity in
  the last 14 days — a session there, or its being added — those with a
  session running first, then the most recent, at most six): its name,
  sessions running or *active 3d ago*, a **Hide** button right after that
  (an eye crossed out and the word), the newest release with its age at the
  right; then five figures — *Actions this month*
  (GitHub's billing report for the owner, asked once per owner every 30
  minutes; without the permission it says *grant Plan (read) to see it*, or
  *Administration (read)* for an organization, and opens the token settings), *CI time* (the busiest
  workflow's median and a line per day), *Red runs · 14d* (share, count,
  re-runs, bars per day), *Stuck* (count in red when any, the longest in
  words — *ci on feat/x running 41m, median 9m* — and its link), *Pull
  requests* (open, merged in 7 days).
- **Cost per pull request** — the five open or merged-this-week pull
  requests the agents spent most on: state dot, number (opens GitHub),
  title, the author's face, agents running, a quiet *shared* tag when a
  session charged to it also worked on other pull requests and is cut
  between them (its hover says how), the tokens in compact units beside
  the cost (*9.4M tok · $2.12*; the hover splits input, output, cache read
  and write), agent cost (owner, 2026-10-14, translated: "add tokens next
  to the dollars, neatly"). **Who · 7 days** —
  each author's face and login, pull requests and merges, agent cost. Every
  cost is at API list price and its hover says how it is counted.

**Older and hidden repositories** (owner, 2026-10-14, translated: "why do
I see projects from a hundred years ago? … either drop them, or let me hide
them, or just don't show such old ones"): a repository without activity in
14 days, or hidden, is not read from GitHub and folds into one quiet line
under the blocks: *2 older repositories — show* and *1 hidden · Show*.
The first reads the older ones too (their blocks below the recent ones) and
becomes *Fold older repositories*; the second lists the hidden ones, asking
nothing, each with *Show again*, and *Show all N again* when there are
several. **Hide is one click** (owner, 2026-10-15/16, translated: "I need to
hide many and it annoys me"; it was the only item of a ⋯ menu until then):
the block goes at once and *home-trader hidden from CI/CD · Undo* stands in
its place for ten seconds (`data-cicd-undo`). Hidden repositories are this
browser's, as hidden projects are (`caprock.app.cicd-hidden-repos`), and
travel with each ask as `hide=`.

**GitHub slow or away** is one calm line, not a wall of orange: the daemon
reads at most three repositories at once, each within 12 seconds, so one
slow repository no longer runs the rest out of time. A row GitHub did not
answer says *GitHub didn't answer in time — retrying* (or *GitHub not
reachable — retrying*) in grey, the raw error in its hover; when every row
says the same, it is said once for the section. The first answer comes at
once with the repositories' names (*reading GitHub…*) and the section asks
again in 3 seconds until the figures are in; a block already drawn keeps
its figures meanwhile (`keepWhileReading`), so *show* or *Hide* never blanks
it.

Folded, the body is not mounted and nothing is asked; open, it asks every 2
minutes while the window is visible and keeps its last answer in
`lib/deckCache` (`cicd:now`). Not connected, one line points to Settings.

## What did it ask?

**A session at a permission dialog says so** ([ADR-036](08-decisions.md)): the stored `permission.prompt` makes the card and the session page read *waiting for approval — running `go test …`* (the call it asks about; *waiting for your answer* for `AskUserQuestion`) with the *waiting on you* badge, for every session, not only Caprock's own. Before, a session stuck at a dialog looked busy, since "waiting" meant only "the last event was `Stop`". The timeline shows the event as *waiting for approval: Bash* and the activity feed as *waiting for your approval*.

A session waiting on you is the one case where the terminal holds something the dashboard does not — except it does not: every assistant turn is already stored. A **what did it ask?** button appears on a waiting session's card and on its attention row, and opens the last thing Claude said. On the attention row it is the secondary action: the row itself, and its *Open terminal →*, go to the session.

It shows the last *complete* thought rather than the newest text. Roughly a third of sessions end mid-sentence ("Let me check that…"), and presenting that as the answer would send the reader to the terminal anyway — which is the trip this exists to save. When a newer line was skipped for that reason, the panel says so.

It shows and does not act. Typing into a session Caprock did not spawn is refused by rule 7, and that rule is worth more than the convenience: writing blind into a terminal interrupts work you cannot see. Reading carries no such cost, so this works for every session — including the ones started by hand, which is nearly all of them. The footer names where the reply goes instead of offering a box that would fail.

## Memory — does the handoff help?

With **Measure whether it helps** ticked under Settings → Memory between
sessions → Experiments (it holds the handoff back from one new session in
four), the Memory screen
opens with the comparison: median minutes and tool calls to a session's first
edit, with the handoff and without. Until each group has 5 sessions that
reached an edit it says how far the count has got instead of a number that
would swing with the next session.

## Memory — paging and the link back

The list pages backwards on demand (`load older answers`) rather than loading a fixed window. The fixed window looked like data loss: on a busy machine 500 notes is half a day, so the list jumped from "22h ago" to "30d ago" with nothing between, and the first outside user reasonably read the gap as missing history. Nothing was missing — the middle had never been fetched. `GET /v1/notes` takes `before=<event_id>` for this.

Each answer links to **the moment it was written** (`#/session/{id}?at=…`), not to the top of the session. The same reporter said the answers "did not feel connected to the session", which was exactly right: opening a thousand-event timeline at the beginning and leaving someone to find the passage they just clicked is the same as not linking at all.

## Dialogs

Every modal leaves the same three ways (`components/Dialog.tsx`): a 30px **×** in its header (`CloseButton`, 44px on a phone, *Close (Esc)* on hover), a click on the dimmed backdrop, and **Esc**. `Sheet` is built on them; a one-off dialog wraps its panel in `DialogBackdrop` and puts `CloseButton` in its header. Quick chat had no × at all and the owner could not close it (2026-10-10), which is why none of this is left to each dialog.

- **Esc closes the newest dialog only.** Dialogs register on a stack and one capture-phase listener closes the top one and stops the key, so nothing under it — a terminal, the Settings page — hears it.
- **A backdrop click must start and end on the backdrop.** A text selection dragged out of a field ends on the backdrop; closing then would throw away what was typed.
- **The × is never the first focus.** A sheet still focuses its first field, so Enter on an opened sheet never closes it.
- **The backdrop is drawn on `document.body`** (a portal). The app sidebar's translucent material (`backdrop-filter`) makes a containing block for `position: fixed`, and a dialog opened from the sidebar was confined to its 260px.

**Settings is for deciding, quickly** (owner, 2026-10-11, translated:
"everything large — big buttons; nobody wants to read this; quick settings
and numbers in view; maybe a fold toggle"). Section titles are 17px with
their icon, choices are 14px buttons, checkboxes 18px. Explanations are
hidden by default: **Show explanations** beside the heading turns them on for
the page (`caprock-settings-explain`), and each section's **?** for that
section alone. What is hidden is a section's muted and faint paragraphs and
every `[data-help]` (a toggle's hint); a status line keeps `data-keep`, and a
problem section (`explained`) always shows its text. A section's title folds
it, kept per viewer (`caprock-settings-folds`).

**Settings sections** (`components/SettingsNav.tsx`, `SettingsIcons.tsx`):
from the `xl` breakpoint a sticky menu left of the page lists every section
with a simple line icon, marks the one in view and scrolls to the one
clicked; each section heading carries the same icon. It reads the
`[data-settings-section]` headings the page renders, so a new `Section`
joins it on its own. The owner, looking for one setting among twenty-odd,
asked for a menu and for icons to tell the sections apart (2026-10-11).

**Settings search** (`components/SettingsSearch.tsx`, `lib/settingsSearch.ts`;
owner, 2026-10-18, translated: "the settings menu is already complicated —
there's no search"): a field at the top of the menu, and above the sections
under `xl` where the menu is not drawn. Typing filters live: a section the
query does not find is `hidden` (not unmounted), and the menu lists the ones
it finds, best first — the whole query in the title, then every word in the
title or a synonym, then the whole query in one text inside, then every word
somewhere in the section — each with the line inside that matched. The index
is the rendered page: every `[data-settings-section]`'s title and its text
nodes, `aria-label`s, placeholders and `title`s (not the **?** toggle's), so a
new option is searchable with nothing to register. A folded section has no
body; what it said the last time it was open is kept in memory, and until
then its title and the synonyms find it. Synonyms map the words people type
to a title that says it differently (*token*, *bot* → Telegram; *phone*, *qr*
→ Use on your phone; *theme*, *dark* → Appearance; *keys*, *shortcut* →
Global shortcut; *licence* → Plan & licence, and so on). The words are marked
with the CSS Custom Highlight API (`::highlight(settings-search)`), so the
page's text is never rewritten; an engine without it marks nothing. Enter, or
a click on a result, unfolds the section if folded, opens a `<details>` the
hit is in, scrolls to the label, button or line that matched (or the nearest
drawn ancestor when it is a hidden explanation) and lights it for 1.6s
(`[data-settings-flash]`). Nothing found: *Nothing matches “x”*. Esc clears
the field; on an empty field it lets go of the cursor. **⌘F** (Ctrl+Shift+F
off macOS, the app's Find, drawn through `keys()`; plain Ctrl+F also works
there, since nothing on this page holds it) puts the cursor in the field
that is drawn: in the app the shell holds the key and, with Settings in
front, sends `caprock:settings-find` instead of the terminal's find event;
in a browser the page hears the key itself.

**Settings** is a page, not a dialog, but has the same way out: a × beside its heading and Esc from anywhere on it but a field or an open dialog (`lib/settingsClose.ts`). In the app both do exactly what the Settings tab's × in the strip does (the app shell provides its `closeDashboard`); in a browser they go back to the screen Settings was opened from. Closing the Dashboard tab while it is in front moves the hash in the same render (`useHash` in `AppShell.tsx`); before, the render on the old hash reopened the tab, and a *Settings* tab stayed in the strip after its × was clicked.

## Feedback

A **Feedback** button (an amber bug and the word, on a faint panel fill so it reads as a button in both themes; a grey speech bubble until 2026-10-11, which the owner did not see) in the header, on every screen, and in the desktop app a labelled *Send feedback* row above *Dashboard* in the sidebar's bottom bar, which is on screen whatever tab is in front (`components/Feedback.tsx`). It was a bare megaphone icon beside Settings and the theme switch until 2026-10-10, and the owner read it as a volume control. It was an 11px grey word among the header's chips until 2026-10-10, and the owner never noticed it. The arrow at the right of that row folds the whole bottom block (feedback, Dashboard, Settings, Pipeline, theme) to one row of 32px icons with their keys above them (§ The sidebar footer) and back, kept as `footer` in the sidebar's folds key (owner, 2026-10-11). ⇧⌘F (Ctrl+Alt+Shift+F off macOS, where Ctrl+Shift+F is ⌘F's Find) opens the same form from anywhere in the app, its key drawn at the end of the row where it fits (§ The sidebar footer; owner, 2026-10-14, translated: "inside it everything is already keyboard-driven … maybe people will file more issues"); AppShell holds that dialog, so it opens with the sidebar hidden too. The dialog closes like every other (§ Dialogs).

**The sidebar footer** (`components/SidebarFooter.tsx`, owner, 2026-10-14, translated: "the CI/CD window — the bottom bar, and its separate full page — could hide behind one button with the bar's modes … Expand/Hide inside the bar is not noticeable; bottom-left in the sidebar is where it's handy. Also put the colour theme setting there as its own button"). Four rows: *Send feedback ⇧⌘F* with the fold arrow; **Mail ⇧⌘M** (the envelope in the accent; owner, 2026-10-14, translated: "put it on the main screen as a button — in the left bottom too — and it opens another system tab with its own key combo"), which in the desktop app opens Mail in a window of its own and in a browser opens the Mail tab or brings it to the front (§ Mail), marked `aria-current` while Mail is in front, and an envelope icon in the folded row; *Dashboard ⌘D* on a full row (⌘0 still works and is in its tooltip); under it one row of two equal halves, each a 32px outlined row like the others (owner, 2026-10-14, translated: "move them onto a separate row and split it into two parts — two buttons in one row, neat, so it's clear what's there"): **Pipeline ⌘P** — its glyph (four stops on a line, `PipelineIcon`), the word and its key — and the **theme ⌥⌘P**, sun or moon, the palette's name and its key (on the right; its picker opens up and to the left); then *Settings ⌘,* with its phone and Telegram words. A half is 110–150px, so a half's chip shows only where the row measures room for it beside the name, the key staying in its tooltip either way (below). Off macOS the halves are rows of their own (`splitModeRow`), with *Pipeline Ctrl+Shift+P* and the theme's Ctrl+Alt+Shift+P, each kept where its row has room. **Every key chip in the block is the New agent chip's size** (`.app-kbd-key`, 13px; owner, 2026-10-15, translated: "these buttons fold down; the shortcuts there are tiny, you can't see a damn thing"), and **a chip that has no room is left out whole, never drawn smaller**: each row measures itself (`lib/keyChipFit.ts`: `useChipFit`, a ResizeObserver and the words' natural width) and drops its chip when the words would be cut or the row would spill, the key staying in the row's tooltip; the chip comes back when the row widens. The theme's half is measured around its picker's button, so a long palette name drops ⌥⌘P rather than shorten. Hints give way first: *bug, idea, question* on Send feedback and *phone, Telegram* on Settings shrink before the chip goes, and the words themselves never shorten. Off macOS no chip is in its row at rest (since 2026-10-19): each shows just above its row under keyboard focus, and all of them while Ctrl is held; under the pointer only the row's tooltip, which names the key (a Windows tester, 2026-10-20) (`keyChipMode`, `.app-kbd-reveal`; the **Keyboard** item under § The app workspace). **Folded**, the block is one row of 18px glyphs, 32px tall: Send feedback, Mail, Dashboard, Settings, Pipeline, theme, then the arrow back. Each key is drawn above its icon through `keys()` (owner, 2026-10-14, translated: "I'd put the key combos above them, that would be super, and the icons can be one size bigger"), at the same size: until 2026-10-15 they were small faint chips (`.app-kbd-faint`, 10px) that could not be read. The theme's is ⌥⌘P (⇧⌘T until 2026-10-20, when that became Reopen closed tab), which opens its picker from anywhere (the sidebar shown first when hidden): the same portalled `ThemeMenu` a click opens, kept inside the window, with the focus on the palette in use, and pressed again it closes. On a Mac a column is as wide as its chip (32px for ⌘D, ⌘P and ⌘,, 40px for ⇧⌘F, ⇧⌘M and ⌥⌘P); where the six do not fit, the widest chip goes first, the rightmost of a tie (`fitFoldedChips`), its icon keeping the key in its tooltip, and a row with no chip left draws no key line. Off macOS the chips are 63–141px (Ctrl+Shift+M, Ctrl+Alt+Shift+F), wider than any column, so there a column shows its chip only under keyboard focus or while Ctrl is held (on hover the icon's tooltip names it), floating above the row on the panel and held to the sidebar's nearer edge (`foldedKeys`). The Pipeline button brings the pinned Pipeline tab to the front (`#/pipeline`, § The Pipeline), and from it goes back to the tab or screen before it — what ⌘P does, ⇧⌘B being a silent alias (owner, 2026-10-14, translated: "⇧⌘B opens the Pipeline page as a tab; again, back"; a page of the dashboard for part of 2026-10-15, then a system tab of its own beside the Dashboard tab); it is in the accent and `aria-current` while the Pipeline tab is in front, and its name and tooltip say so with the key through `keys()` (`pipelineLabel`: *… in its own tab (⌘P)*, *… click to go back to where you were*). It was a three-mode button until the evening of 2026-10-14 (*CI Off / CI Bar / CI Page*, a click cycling them); *Page* stopped being a mode, and the bar's on and off moved to the bar itself — *Pipeline* and a × at the end of the deck's header (`data-deck-expand`, `data-deck-hide`) — and to the palette (*Pipeline bar: On/Off*, beside *Open Pipeline ⌘P*); the bar itself, ⌘B and the strip's *Deck ⌘B* button went on 2026-10-20 (§ CI on the Pipeline and in the cockpit).

**The footer is a band of its own, under the list and never over it** (owner, 2026-10-15, translated: "there isn't a clear enough boundary between the tools and what's above … its caption can't even be seen"). The sidebar is one flex column: the list (`data-sidebar-scroll`, projects then Second Brain) takes what is left and scrolls inside its own area, and the footer is its next sibling, in the flow — nothing absolute, nothing drawn under it. The footer sits on a solid fill one step off the panel (`.app-sidebar-footer`, `--color-fg` at 3.5% over `--color-panel`, so the translucent sidebar does not run through it) with an `--app-hairline` on top, which reads as an edge in every palette, dark, light, Book and Sunlight. The list keeps 16px of padding at its end, so its last line — Second Brain's *Local · your own Claude · free* — is whole when scrolled to the end, folded footer or open, at 240–320px and in a 560px window. **Folded, a key is part of its icon's button** (owner, 2026-10-15, translated: "I wanted to click the key combo with the mouse and it doesn't click"): a click on the chip is a click on the icon's own button, the same handler, and the chip carries that button's tooltip. The column stays one control — one tab stop, one name for a screen reader (the icon's; the chip is `aria-hidden`, a larger mouse target) — and the pointer over either lights both, the chip's border one step stronger; at rest the chip looks as before.

**The form is one box** (owner, 2026-10-16, translated: "I want the feedback button as short as humanly possible: just type what's wrong, maybe drop a screenshot, and send"). Until then it was three Bug / Idea / Question cards picked with 1 2 3, a required title, a description and a screenshot strip; now, top to bottom:

- **What's wrong?** — one textarea, focused on open, its placeholder saying a screenshot can be dropped or pasted anywhere. Its first line becomes the issue's title (cut at 200 characters; GitHub refuses a title over 256), the rest the description; a first line too long for a title goes into the description whole (`fromBox` in `lib/feedback.ts`). A few characters, or a screenshot on its own (titled *Screenshot from <screen>*), are all it needs: nothing is required beyond that.
- **The whole sheet is the drop zone**: an image dropped anywhere on it (the border turns the accent and *Drop to attach* covers it), ⌘V / Ctrl+V of one anywhere while it is up (a text paste is left alone), or in the desktop app a file dropped on the window, which arrives as a path (`DROP_PATHS_EVENT`, below). Up to 4, each at most 10 MB (GitHub's limit for a pasted image), a thumbnail each with a ×; a line says what was left out and why. A non-PNG image is redrawn as PNG when attached, because the clipboard takes `image/png` only (`lib/attachments.ts`).
- **One quiet line and Send.** "Opens a GitHub issue in your browser, with version and OS; you submit it there." — rule 4's "say where it goes", in one line — then a small **Details** and the **Send** button (⌘↩, Ctrl+Enter off macOS through `keys()`; a bare Enter is a new line).
- **Details**, closed by default, holds every choice the old form showed: the kind (Bug, Idea, Question; Bug unless changed — each maps to a label that exists on dspv/caprock, `bug`, `enhancement`, `question`, and a test pins that, because GitHub drops an unknown label without a word), **Include diagnostics** with the exact lines it adds (version, OS, screen, scale, hooks; on by default, struck through when unticked), *Choose image…*, and in the desktop app on macOS and Windows **Capture window**.

**After Send** with no screenshots the sheet says *Opened on GitHub — thanks* for 1.2 s and closes. With screenshots it turns into the *Attach your screenshots* step below. If opening the browser throws, the words stay, the line says *Could not open the browser. Your words are kept.*, and the button reads **Retry**.

**The draft is kept** per viewer in `localStorage` (`caprock.feedback.draft`, every access in try/catch) from the first keystroke until it is sent: Esc, the × in the box's corner and a click outside close the sheet like every dialog (§ Dialogs), and the words are there the next time it opens. A sent report clears it; screenshots are not kept.

**Nothing is transmitted.** *Send* composes a prefilled GitHub new-issue URL and opens it in the browser — a new tab, or in the desktop app the default browser through the shell's `open_external` command (`openExternal` in `lib/nudges.ts`); the user reads it, edits it and submits it. Feedback stays a public GitHub issue on purpose; a report that may hold personal data goes the other way, privately, through *Report a bug* below (ADR-073).

**When a screen fails to draw** (`components/ErrorBoundary.tsx`, `lib/crashreport.ts`). Each dashboard screen has its own boundary: the panel says it failed, with *retry* and the report links below, and the rest of the window stays. Around the whole page sits one more, `full`, so a throw above them shows *Caprock hit a snag* instead of the blank page (a black window in the app) it used to leave, which was all a user saw when 0.115.1's deck read a `null` job list. It offers *Reload*; *Reset the view and reload*, which clears `caprock-swr-v1:*`, `caprock.deck.*` and `caprock.app.deck` (a crash that lives in saved state survives a plain reload) and keeps tabs and settings; and the report three ways: *Report on GitHub* (a prefilled issue through `lib/feedback.ts`), *Email us* (`mailto:hi@caprock.dev`, body cut to 1,500 characters for mail clients) and *Copy details*. The report carries the error, the first stack and component frames, the version from the page's `caprock-version` meta, app or browser, and the OS; the route keeps its name, not its query, a home directory is folded to `~` and the local origin is dropped. As with Feedback, nothing is sent until the user does it. In the desktop app the full boundary also writes `crash screen: <error> on Screen: <route>` to the app's own log on the machine (`app_log`, `<data_dir>/app.log`).

**A page that never starts** (the second inline script in `index.html`, tested in `lib/boot.test.ts`). A crash screen needs React; a page whose bundle did not load, or that threw before React drew, is an empty `#root` and a blank window. The watchdog is plain script, so it runs anyway: 10 s with nothing drawn (1.5 s after a script or stylesheet failed to load) shows *Reconnecting to Caprock…* — or *This window did not finish loading* when the daemon answers — with *Reload*, in the system's colours over the whole page. It asks `/healthz` with a pause that doubles to 30 s, and reloads when it answers: at once after a failure, after 30 s for a page merely empty, five times in ten minutes at most (`caprock.boot.reloads` in sessionStorage); then only the button. A root that empties after React drew gets the same after 3 s. Once anything is drawn the panel goes. In the app it tells the app's log once why it showed (.ai/21-app.md § A blank window recovers by itself).

**Screenshots go by clipboard.** A new-issue URL cannot carry a file, and uploading the images anywhere from Caprock is the outbound call rule 4 forbids. So *Send* starts copying screenshot 1 to the clipboard inside the click (WebKit, and so the macOS app, allows a clipboard write only there), then opens the issue, whose body ends "**Screenshots: 2 — paste them here (⌘V).**" The dialog turns into a small *Attach your screenshots* step: "Screenshot 1 of 2 is on your clipboard — press ⌘V in the GitHub comment box", the thumbnails with the current one ringed, **Copy next (2 of 2)** — it wraps round after the last — and **Done**. GitHub uploads what is pasted into its own page, from the user's browser and account. *Copy next* appears only with more than one screenshot. The paste key is the platform's: ⌘V on macOS, Ctrl+V elsewhere. When the clipboard refuses an image there is no error: the line becomes "One more step: save the screenshots, then drag them into the GitHub comment box", *Copy next* goes, and **Save screenshots** downloads every one (in the app, through the shell's save dialog). With no screenshots the issue opens and the dialog closes.

**In the desktop app the shell does the native parts** (`.ai/21-app.md` § Feedback screenshots):

- **The clipboard** is the OS's, through the shell's `clipboard_image`, not `navigator.clipboard`: whether a webview lets a page write an image differs by OS and version.
- **Capture window** (macOS and Windows) attaches a picture of the app's own page, drawn by its webview — never the screen, so no Screen Recording permission. The dialog hides itself for the capture (two frames and 50 ms), so the shot shows the app rather than the form, and comes back with the image attached. Hidden in a browser and on Linux, whose WebKitGTK capture is not built.
