  the newest finished filling places the working leave; in **groups of
  five** with a wider gap between groups (`chipGroups`; owner, 2026-10-14:
  "lay them out in fives so you can see by fives how many there are",
  translated), wrapping between groups onto a second line. When the
  working need more than two full lines — more than 20 beside *N working*
  in a 324px inspector, computed from the width (`chipLayout`), never a
  fixed number — the chips go **compact**: a 10px square with no letter,
  tint and state kept, still a button; when even two compact lines do not
  hold them, as many as fit with *+N* for the working left out, 80 at most.
  No chip is ever clipped. With none, *no subagents running* holds the line
  (owner, 2026-10-12: "why doesn't it show more agents?"); each chip is a button,
  hover names its type, task, model and state, a click opens that
  subagent's row in the *Subagents* section and scrolls it into view.
  The rows stand in a fixed order (`orderSubagents` in
  `lib/subagentview.ts`): by when each subagent started, first on top,
  its start kept as first seen so a long run's sliding window start moves
  nothing; a new one is appended, and nothing it does — its state, its
  activity, its cost — re-sorts them. One that finishes keeps its place,
  dimmed, its chip muted and a green ✓ beside its chevron, until it is
  folded away as before (owner, 2026-10-14: "their order jumps too, bad, I
  want fixed positions", translated). A row leads with the same chip —
  its type's letter in its tint, the full type on hover — then what it is
  doing now in words across the row's whole width, wrapping to two lines
  (`line-clamp-2`), the full phrase on hover: the daemon's `doing`
  (`subagentDoing`: its newest call's own description, else its tool and
  target, else its task), a finished one's task. Under it, dimmer, its
  current tool and how long that call has run — the raw command or file
  only when the phrase does not already say it — and on the right the
  figures picked in *Show ▾* in the section header: *Compact* (none),
  *Cost* (cost and time, the default) or *Full* (calls, cost, model,
  time), or each figure ticked on its own, remembered per viewer in
  `localStorage` (`caprock.app.subagent-fields`). With no figure picked a
  row is its chip and its phrase alone; while it waits on you, *waiting on
  you* leads the line below in place of the tool (owner, 2026-10-13: "a
  letter for the type, and the text is what it is roughly doing now";
  2026-10-14: "there's calls, dollars, model and time — maybe a switch for
  what to see?", both translated). Each
  row there (`SubagentDetail.tsx`, `lib/subagentFeed.ts`) is a button with
  a chevron and `aria-expanded` that opens in place, one at a time, the
  finished rows too (*+N finished* lists up to 30 of them as rows, *+N
  more working* the working past five): *Task*, the prompt the parent's
  call gave it, folded to six lines with *Show all*; *Activity · N calls*,
  its calls oldest first, newest at the bottom, each a tool glyph, name,
  short line and duration, ✕ in red for a failed one, the open one green
  and counting, the newest 30 with *Show earlier · N*; *Result*, what it
  returned, folded the same way, *Still working…* while it works; and
  *Numbers*: model, time, calls, cost, tokens, and the four costliest tool
  kinds with their calls and cost. It is read from
  `/subagents/{agent}` once on opening and once when it stops, its calls
  followed on the live socket in between (owner, 2026-10-12: "can we
  expand the subagents to look inside?"); a loop
  warning while `loop` is set; *Recent
  tools* (`Timeline`; `toolGroups`, `toolTarget` in `lib/cockpit.ts`),
  the main thread's last seven rows, newest first. It had a log-scaled
  duration bar beside each call, which said nothing (owner, 2026-10-14,
  translated: "honestly gives little information — the indicators say
  nothing, the time is clear, OK"); a row is now the kind's glyph, the
  tool, **what it acted on** and **how it ended**, then how long ago
  (*now*, *42s*, *4m*, *3h*). What it acted on: a file relative to the
  session's folder (`shortPath`: *ui/src/lib/cockpit.ts*, a long one
  *internal/…/folder/x_test.go*, one outside it its last two parts), a
  command's program and first two words it acts on past `cd`, `set -e`,
  variable assignments, `env`/`sudo`, redirections and anything after a
  pipe or a later flag (`commandTarget`: `S=/private/tmp/… python3
  /private/tmp/…/shots.py --out a` reads *python3 …/scratchpad/shots.py*),
  a search's pattern in quotes, a page's host and path. How it ended, in
  its own column: a faint ✓ for a quick success, the duration for one of
  five seconds or more (`SLOW_MS`), *exit 1* in red for a failed command
  whose result says its exit code (the transcript's *Exit code N* at the
  head of an erroring result, Codex's `exit_code`; `resultOutcome`),
  *error* for any other failure, *stopped* for an interrupted call, the
  running time in green for the call running now, *no result* for one
  past 15 minutes without one. The same tool on the same target with the
  same outcome, in a row, is one row with *×N* (three Reads of one file,
  a retried test), its total time on hover; a running call is never
  folded. The hover has the full command or path, the description Claude
  wrote for the call, the outcome in words and the clock time. The
  heading counts the failures among the rows (*1 failed*) and *31,713
  this session* — `stats.tool_calls`, every `tool.pre` the session
  recorded, its subagents' included, as the hover says; it used to read
  *31713 in all*, which named no scope. Nothing new is read: the outcome
  comes from the `tool.post` events the cockpit already holds. Subagents
  and Recent tools **fold** on their label exactly as Context and Plan
  limits switch looks — the same chevron button, `aria-expanded`, kept in
  `caprock.app.cockpit-looks` (`subagents`, `tools`), **full** until a
  viewer folds one (owner, 2026-10-14, translated: "Subagents and Recent
  tools should be collapsible like Context, Plan, Spend etc."). Folded,
  Subagents is *SUBAGENTS · 3* with *2 finished $0.42* at the right, and
  Recent tools is the newest call on one line, its target, outcome and
  age; a hero chip unfolds Subagents to open its row. **Every other
  section folds too** (owner, 2026-10-14, translated: "I want to
  collapse/expand, if possible without overload … the CI and Processes
  too"): Now, Tasks, Changes, CI and Processes are wrapped in `Foldable`
  (`Cockpit.tsx`), which turns their `SectionLabel` into the same chevron
  button and, folded, hides everything under the label row
  (`[data-fold][data-folded]` in `tokens.css`), so the figures the label
  already carries are the section's one line — *3 of 7 done*, *12% · 210
  MB*; Now adds what it is doing, Changes *2 files +40 −3*, CI the newest
  PR's number and checks (`useFolded`). Kept in the same
  `caprock.app.cockpit-looks` (`now`, `tasks`, `changes`, `ci`,
  `release`, `processes`), full by default. **Arrange
  sections** (`ArrangeSections`, `lib/cockpitorder.ts`): the sections
  under the agent — Now (with the loop warning and the shared-checkout
  line), Context, Plan limits, Spend, Subagents, Tasks, Recent tools,
  Changes, CI, **Release** and Processes (the inspector hands Changes, CI,
  Release and Processes to the cockpit as nodes) — stand in the order above
  until a viewer changes it (owner,
  2026-10-14, translated: "I want to be able to reorder the sections, but
  so it doesn't trigger by accident — so everyone sets up what interests
  them"). Release (`CockpitRelease` in `components/ReleasePlan.tsx`) is the
  session's repository's release plan, compact ([§ Release
  plan](#release-plan)); it asks GitHub nothing — it draws the Pipeline
  answer the CI section, the Pipeline page or Work last kept for the
  repository (`stats:<repo>` in `lib/deckCache`) — and reads `GET /v1/work`
  while unfolded, again only on a `github` or `reset` frame, never on the
  agent's turns; folded, its line is the version and *N in work*; a session
  outside a repository has none. **Each section also moves by its grip**
  (`components/SectionFrames.tsx`; owner, 2026-10-15, translated: "I want
  the sections in the right panel — Plan, Subagents, Context, CI/CD — to be
  reorderable by users … and to be able to move all of it higher"): a small
  six-dot grip in the gutter at the section's top left, shown on hovering
  the section or focusing the grip (always, faint, without a hover). Only
  the grip drags — never a heading, so a stray drag moves nothing — and a
  2px accent line shows where the section lands among the others
  (`dropIndex`, `dropSection`; a press that travels under 4px is a click).
  On the focused grip ⌥⇧↑ ⌥⇧↓ move it a place (Ctrl+Shift+↑ ↓ off macOS:
  the sidebar's move-a-row keys, `isRowMoveKey`, spelt through `keys()` in
  its tooltip and menu; the shortcut sheet's row names both uses), the
  focus staying on it. A click opens its menu (`MenuBox`): *Move up*, *Move
  down*, *Move to top* and *Reset order*, each disabled where it would do
  nothing. Moves skip a section that draws nothing for this session (a CI
  with no pull requests, no release plan), whose frame is hidden, so every
  press visibly moves one (`stepSection`). A faint *Arrange sections* under the last section opens Arrange
  mode, which shows the sections as a numbered list in their place, each
  with ↑ and ↓ (*Move Spend up*), *Reset to default order* (disabled when
  it is the default) and *Done*; Esc leaves too. There is no drag on a
  heading, so nothing moves by accident, and every move is one press with
  the pointer or the keyboard, the focus staying on the button pressed.
  The agent's own block stays on top, and the actions (open in the
  dashboard, the editor, close the tab) stay after the sections. The order is kept per viewer in
  `localStorage` (`caprock.app.cockpit-order`, every read and write in
  try/catch); the default is stored as nothing, so a later default reaches
  a viewer who never chose, an unknown name in a stored order is dropped,
  and a section a stored order lacks takes the place after the section
  before it by default. The uncommitted changes (`/diff`, every 20 s while open); the plan
  windows (`rate_limits` for Claude Code, `codex_rate_limits` for Codex,
  none for the rest), then open in the dashboard, close the tab, stop.
  Beside a shell it stays the plain inspector: folder, changes, actions. With the inspector closed, the
  permission card sits under the terminal instead, whether or not the
  terminal is in front (owner, 2026-10-07; 0.78.2 hid it there). Other tabs' prompts reach you through their badge, their marked row in the sidebar, ⌘J,
  the menu bar and the notification. **Open in <editor>** (F18)
  opens the session's folder. A changed file's name opens it in a file tab
  when the session runs in a listed repository's worktree; elsewhere it
  opens the file in the editor at its first changed line (the first hunk's
  new start). The editor actions are not shown without an editor, or on a
  paired device.
  *Review and commit* beside *Changes* opens the Changes view of the
  worktree the session runs in.
- **Changes view** (`ChangesView.tsx`, `DiffView.tsx`, `CommitBox.tsx`,
  client in `lib/changes.ts`, kept current by `lib/useChanges.ts`;
  [03-contracts.md § Changes](03-contracts.md#changes-a-worktrees-status-diff-commit-and-push)).
  Opened from a worktree's **±N** in the sidebar (a button; on hover the row
  shows it among its actions, and a one-checkout project carries it in its
  own row), *Review and commit* in the inspector, or *Review changes: …* in
  the palette; it covers the terminals until Esc, its close button, or a tab
  or session is opened. Header: branch, upstream (or *not published* / *no
  remote*), ↑ahead ↓behind, **Fetch**, **Pull** (fast-forward only),
  **Push** (*Publish branch* before the first push, *Push N* after; never
  forced). Left, 340 px: *Conflicts*, *Staged* (*Unstage all*), *Changes*
  (*Stage all*), one 26 px row per file with its status letter, name, folder
  and +/−; hover shows stage, unstage or discard. Under the list, the commit
  box: *Commit N staged*, or *Commit all N* when nothing is staged (it sends
  `all`), and *Commit & Push*; the draft is kept per worktree in
  localStorage. Right: the selected file's diff with a *Unified* / *Side by
  side* switch (kept per browser) and *Stage hunk* / *Unstage hunk* on each
  hunk of a modified file. Discard is two-step: the first call's preview
  becomes an inline confirmation naming the files (new ones "will be
  deleted"); a file changed since is shown again with the fresh token.
  Results and failures (with git's or the hook's output, open by default on
  failure) appear under the header or the commit box. Keys while the view
  has focus and no text box does: j/k or ↓/↑, s, u, d, o (open the file in
  a file tab; also the ↗ on a row and the path above the diff), v, c
  (message), r (read again), Esc, ⇧⌘↵ (send the review notes); in the message ⌘↵ commits, ⇧⌘↵ commits and pushes.
  **Review notes** (`ReviewNotes.tsx`, `lib/reviewNotes.ts`): each line
  number of the diff (both layouts) is a button; a click opens a note box
  under that line, a shift-click stretches it from the first line clicked to
  the one clicked, on the same side (the new file, or the old one for a
  removed line, said as *(before the change)*). Enter saves, ⇧Enter is a new
  line, Esc cancels without closing the view. A saved note is drawn under the
  line it ends on, monospaced so `DiffView` computes its height (wrapped at
  spaces) like any other row, with *Edit* and *Delete*; the lines it covers
  have their numbers in the accent colour. Notes are kept per worktree in
  localStorage (`caprock.review.<project>:<worktree>`) with the diff lines
  they were written on, quoted at that moment (40 at most), so a note still
  says what it meant after the file moved; one whose line left the diff is
  only in the list. Under the diff a bar shows *N notes (on M files)*,
  *Show* (the list; a click goes to the note), *Clear* (asks), and the send
  button. The prompt is one message: a line naming the branch and asking for
  each note to be addressed, then per note `path:line` or `path:start-end`,
  the quoted lines in a fence and the note. It goes through
  `components/AgentHandoff.tsx`: to the newest running session Caprock
  started in that worktree (`liveOwnedSession`: owned, not ended, not
  detached, not a shell — rule 7; the daemon refuses any other id too), as
  the chat field types — escape sequences and control characters stripped,
  tabs as spaces, newlines as ESC CR, Enter after a pause that grows with
  the length — and never while the session waits on a permission prompt
  (`GET /v1/agents/{id}/permission`), which would turn the text into a menu
  answer. *Sent to <session>. Open it* follows. With no such session the
  button reads *New agent with the notes…* and opens New agent on that
  worktree (*Existing copy*, or *This folder* for the main checkout) with
  the prompt as the first message. The notes are cleared when the text is
  typed in or the new agent starts, not before.
  **Fix with agent** (`FixWithAgent` in `AgentHandoff.tsx`, prompts in
  `lib/fixPrompts.ts`) sends a failure the view shows through the same
  handoff, so the same rule-7 and permission-prompt guards hold; the button
  reads *Fix with a new agent…* when no running Caprock-started session is
  in the worktree, and is not drawn when there is nowhere to send it (the
  phone's Changes tab passes no target). It appears on: a commit or push a
  git hook refused (`kind: hook`; under the commit box's failure, or the
  header's for *Push*) — the prompt names the step and git's message and
  quotes the last 120 lines / 6,000 characters of the hook's output, escape
  sequences stripped (the output shown on screen is stripped the same way);
  files in conflict — the strip under the header that names the operation
  under way (*A merge is under way…*, now a noun from `opName`) also says
  *N files are in conflict* and lists the first 50 in the prompt; and in
  the GitHub strip, failing checks (each by name and link, with how to read
  their logs with `gh`; Caprock does not fetch logs) and a pull request
  GitHub reports as conflicting with its base (not for a draft). Every
  prompt opens "This failed: …", asks the agent to find the cause and fix
  it and say what it changed, and to leave committing, pushing, continuing
  or aborting to the user (the pull-request conflict asks it to merge the
  base in and stage the result, not commit).
  The status is read on open, on a `project` frame for the project (400 ms
  debounce), on window focus and after every action — never on a timer.
  **Large diffs**: a patch over 3,000 lines or 400 KB is collapsed behind
  *Show the diff*; once shown, `DiffView` keeps only the rows within 600 px
  of the view in the DOM (unified rows 18 px, one line, scrolled sideways
  together; side by side, each half wraps by character, its height computed
  from the line's length and the measured monospace width). Measured
  2026-10-06 in headless Chrome at 1440×900 on a 6,000-line rewrite (12,000
  rows): 239 frames scrolled 220 px each, mean 16.7 ms, worst 16.8 ms, about
  100 rows in the DOM.
- **File tabs** (`FileView.tsx`, `FileTools.tsx`, `FilePicker.tsx`; client
  and helpers in `lib/files.ts` and `lib/mdreader.ts`;
  [03-contracts.md § Files](03-contracts.md#files-read-a-projects-file)).
  A project's file, read-only, in a tab named after it with its path as
  the tooltip. Opened from the Changes view (o, ↗, the diff's path), the
  cockpit's changed files, a relative link in a Markdown file, a `.md`
  path an agent printed (below), and ⌘K → *Open file…*, which reads `GET
  /v1/projects/{id}/files` once for the worktree in front (the file tab's,
  else the focused session's, else the project's main checkout) and
  filters it here — a subsequence match that favours the file name and
  segment starts, 60 rows drawn. With nothing typed it lists the Markdown
  files first, shallowest first (README.md and the docs a keystroke away);
  typed, a Markdown file wins a tie. There is no shortcut of its own: ⌘P
  is the Pipeline.
  - **Reading Markdown** (`.md`, `.mdx`, `.markdown`). Drawn by `ProseDoc`,
    the chat's `Prose.tsx` read as a document, built from strings with no
    HTML set: heading levels, fenced code, numbered, nested and task items,
    a table's header, rules, links and images. The text sits at a 72ch
    measure, 15px on 1.72 leading, centred, with a heading scale of
    30/22/18/16px and room above each heading. A code block has a *Copy*
    button (and its language) that shows on hover or focus. A table scrolls
    sideways in its own frame, never the page, its cells capped at 44ch so
    prose wraps. A task item (`- [ ]`, `- [x]`) is a read-only check box,
    a done one muted. Each heading carries a GitHub-style anchor in
    `data-anchor` (scoped to the tab, as several file tabs share the page);
    an in-page link `#anchor` scrolls to it. A link resolves against the
    file (`resolveLink`): http(s) and mailto open in the browser
    (`target="_blank"`), a relative path inside the worktree opens its file
    tab, anything else stays text. A relative image is read from the project
    through `GET /v1/projects/{id}/image` with the device token and shown
    from an object URL (alt text until it arrives, and if it never does);
    an image on the web stays its alt text, so reading a file fetches
    nothing from outside the machine. A document with three or more headings
    gets contents (levels one to three): beside the text in a sticky
    220px rail when the tab's body is at least 960px wide (`RAIL_MIN_WIDTH`;
    the toolbar's list icon hides and shows it, kept per browser in
    `caprock.file.toc`), else a dropdown from that icon; the heading being
    read is marked. A 2px accent line at the top of the body is the reading
    progress. No syntax highlighting: it was left out to keep the bundle as
    it was.
  - **Other text** is monospace with a line-number gutter (not selectable,
    so a copy is the code alone), *Wrap* on by default (kept per browser in
    `caprock.file.wrap`), in blocks of 400 lines with
    `content-visibility: auto`; no syntax highlighting. *Rendered* /
    *Source* switches a Markdown file between the two.
  - **The toolbar** (`FileTools.tsx`): quiet 14px icons in the tab's header,
    each with a tooltip, and a short note beside them saying what happened.
    *Copy path* — the absolute path on this machine (the worktree's folder
    joined with the file's path; disabled when the folder is not known
    here). *Copy for an agent* — `@<absolute path>`, in double quotes when
    the path holds a space, the reference Claude Code, Codex and the others
    read as a file; its tooltip says *Paste into any agent's prompt*. *Copy
    Markdown* (*Copy contents* for other text) — the raw text. *Download* —
    the file under its own name, through an `<a download>` of a blob: the
    browser's downloads, or in the desktop app the save dialog its download
    handler opens (as the share cards do). Both are disabled for a file past
    1 MB, where only the first megabyte is here. *Send to a session* — a
    menu of the user's running agents that Caprock started
    (`attachable`, shells left out; newest first, with project and branch).
    Picking one types `@<path> ` into that agent's prompt through `POST
    /v1/agents/{id}/input`, **without Enter**, and brings its tab forward
    so the user adds words and sends; nothing is typed while the agent
    waits on a permission prompt (the text would pick a menu answer). Rule
    7 twice: only owned sessions are offered, and the daemon refuses input
    for any other. *Open in <editor>* stays at the end. There is no *Reveal
    in Finder*: the app has no reveal command, and none was added. Every
    "link" here is a local path; nothing is uploaded or hosted (rule 4).
  - **A `.md` path an agent prints** opens in a tab. In the chat
    (`ChatView` → `Prose` `onPath`) such a path in a reply — `docs/PLAN.md`,
    `./README.md:12`, `/Users/…/NOTES.md`, `~/…`, a Windows path — is an
    accent button; in the app's terminal (`lib/termlinks.ts`, an xterm link
    provider) it is a link on the same ⌘-click (Ctrl+click off macOS) as a
    URL, its tooltip *⌘-click to open in a tab*. Either raises
    `caprock:open-path` with the session's id; the workspace resolves the
    path against that session's folder and opens it when it lies inside a
    project's checkout or one of its worktrees (the deepest wins), else
    says it is not inside a project Caprock knows. The tail of a URL is
    never a path.
  - **Find and freshness.** ⌘F opens a find bar that marks matches with the
    CSS Custom Highlight API (`::highlight` in `tokens.css`), Enter and
    ⇧Enter step, Esc closes. A binary is one line (*A binary file of N KB;
    it is not shown here.*) and *Open in <editor>*; a file past 1 MB shows
    its first megabyte under a line saying so. The file is read when the
    tab comes to the front, on window focus while it is, and on a live
    `project` frame for its project or an agent's `tool.post` naming its
    path (400 ms debounce) — never on a timer; the same text keeps the same
    object, so the reader's place stays. A file tab reads on the page's
    ground in the app's theme, not on the terminal slab.
- **Browser tabs** (the in-app browser, beta; `BrowserView.tsx`,
  `BrowserSetting.tsx`, `lib/browsertab.ts`; the shell's
  `src-tauri/src/browser.rs`; [ADR-075](08-decisions.md)). A web page inside
  Caprock, apart from the agents. **Off by default**: Settings → *In-app
  browser (beta)* turns it on (this viewer's, `caprock.app.in-app-browser`),
  as does picking *Caprock browser* in the link chooser, which says so in a
  toast; with it off nothing below exists — *New browser tab* says where to
  turn it on, and a link asked for opens in the default browser. The desktop
  app's only. A tab kind of its own, `page` (`lib/tabs.ts`), keyed `page:…`
  with its address ('' for a new tab) and, when a session's link opened it,
  that session (`of`):
  - **In the strip, apart.** Every browser tab sits after every other tab,
    behind a hairline divider (the reducer keeps it so through opens, drags
    and the flat sidebar's order); a globe and the page's title (its host
    until the page names itself, *New tab* before it goes anywhere),
    outlined with a dashed hairline and never filled while behind
    (`.app-tab-page`). No ⌘1–⌘9 (`numberedTabs`), no project name, no state
    dot, no rename, no sidebar row, never split; ⌘W and its × close it with
    no question. The inspector and the view icon are not shown beside it.
  - **Opened by** File → *New Browser Tab* (macOS menu, a globe), ⌘K →
    *New browser tab*, the + menu's *New browser tab*, an address typed into
    ⌘K (when nothing else matches and the text is surely an address —
    `looksLikeAddress`, so `README.md` stays a task — the fallback row is
    *Open https://…*), *Caprock browser* in the link chooser
    (`pickCaprock`), and a page's target=_blank link or
    `window.open`, which opens another browser tab. The same address brings
    its existing tab. ⌘-click on a link still opens the default browser.
  - **The toolbar** (38px): back, forward, reload (turning while it loads),
    the address box — *Search with DuckDuckGo or type an address*; Enter
    goes to an address (`localhost:5173` and `:3000` are http, `example.com`
    https — `pageUrl`) or searches anything else with the engine chosen in
    Settings (DuckDuckGo, Google, Bing, Brave Search, Startpage;
    `addressOrSearch`) — *from <session>* when a session's link opened it,
    **Phone** (phone mode, below), copy the address, open in your browser,
    close. A new tab puts the cursor in the box as soon as it is in front. A line under it says what the page
    did that Caprock would not: *Saved report.pdf to Downloads*, *Not
    followed: zoommtg: links would open another app.*
  - **The page** is a window of its own laid over the box under the toolbar
    (the box's CSS rectangle times the interface size), moved with every
    resize and once a second while in front, hidden while the tab is behind,
    the window is hidden, or a sheet, dialog or menu lies over it
    (`overlayOpen`; a menu in the sidebar leaves it shown). Hidden is all:
    switching back shows it exactly where it was. Typing an address gives
    the page the keyboard; a click on the strip or the toolbar gives it back.
  - **Keys** (`browserKey`, rule 12): ⌥← ⌥→ the tab before or after, outside
    a text field (Ctrl+PageUp, Ctrl+PageDown off macOS, where Alt+← → are
    Back and Forward); ⌘L (Ctrl+L) the address box — from the dashboard and
    from the page alike. Every other key is the page's while it has the
    keyboard.
  - **When an agent starts waiting on you** while a browser tab is in front,
    a strip under the toolbar names it — *<title> is waiting for you ·
    paused the video* — with *Go* (its tab) and ×, and the page's video and
    audio are paused (Settings: *Pause a video when an agent needs me*, on
    by default). One already waiting when the tab came up is not news.
  - **Nothing loads without a click** (rule 4): a tab restored after a
    restart, or the one in front when the window loaded, shows its address
    and *Load the page* until it is brought to the front by hand.
  - **Phone mode** (ADR-075, amended 2026-10-21; `lib/phonemode.ts`,
    `PhoneFrame.tsx`, `DeviceBar.tsx`). **Phone** on the toolbar, or ⌥⌘R
    (Ctrl+Alt+Shift+D off macOS; `PHONE_MODE_KEY`, a browser tab's key
    only), on per tab. A device bar under the toolbar: the device (iPhone
    16, iPhone SE (3rd gen), Pixel 9, Galaxy S24, *Custom W×H* with two
    fields), *Bars shown* / *Bars scrolled away*, *Rotate*, *Touch* (on by
    default), **?**, and a readout — *page 393×681 est. · screen 393×852 ·
    DPR 2 here, 3 on the phone · Safari · iOS 18*; *est.* is dotted and
    says the bars are estimates (OQ-23). The tab's box draws the phone
    centred — status bar with the island or punch hole, Chrome's top bar or
    Safari's bottom bar with the page's host, the home indicator, the SE's
    button — and the page's window is laid over the page area at the
    device's size in logical px (never scaled). A phone taller than the tab
    is drawn as the page area alone in a thin rim; a page area taller than
    the tab keeps its size and runs past the edge; either says so in a line
    under the bar. **?** opens *How true this is*, a list per OS (macOS,
    Windows, Linux; this one first) of ✓ real, ≈ approximate and × not
    emulated, from the approved mockup; while it is open the page hides, as
    under any menu. The device, side, bars, touch and custom size are this
    viewer's (`caprock.app.browser-phone`). A new device's user agent
    reloads the page; its size, side and bars only resize it.
- **GitHub** (WP-19, `lib/github.ts`, `PullRequest.tsx`, `RepoPicker.tsx`,
  `CreateRepoSheet.tsx`; [03-contracts.md § GitHub](03-contracts.md#github)).
  A strip under the Changes view's header (`GitHubStrip`) says where the
  worktree stands: *Not on GitHub yet* with **Create a GitHub
  repository…** (a sheet: owner, name from the folder, description,
  *Private* ticked, *Push the current branch after*, HTTPS or SSH remote)
  or *Connect GitHub to publish it*; else the pull request — `#N` linked,
  its title, state (draft, merged, closed), checks (*2 of 3 passed*, the
  failing ones by name and linked), review (*approved*, *changes
  requested by @x*), mergeability, *read 40 s ago* and a re-read button —
  or **New pull request…**. That opens a sheet (`CreatePRForm`): title and
  description prefilled from the commits (`draft` from the daemon), *Use
  the agent's summary* when a session runs there (its last note, like the
  commit box), base = the default branch, *Draft*, and a line saying the
  branch is pushed first when it is not published or is ahead. A pull
  request already open shows the error with *Open #N on GitHub*. The
  sidebar shows a 12 px pull-request icon on a worktree's (or a
  one-checkout project's) row while it has a pull request (`PRDot`),
  coloured with a small dot: green checks passed, red a check failed or
  changes requested, amber checks running, grey merged, plain when
  there are no checks; its title says which (`#12 title · build failed ·
  approved`). The state
  comes from `GET /v1/github/prs` once, then `github` live frames
  (`prStore`); nothing in the page polls GitHub. **⌘O → Clone** shows the
  repository picker above the address field once connected (search with a
  350 ms pause, an owner menu, *More…* for the next page, HTTPS or SSH kept
  per browser); picking fills the address, which stays editable.
- **Open in editor** (F18, `lib/editors.ts`): the daemon's list
  (`GET /v1/editors`) is asked once per page. A right-click on a worktree
  in the sidebar opens a menu (`EditorMenu.tsx`) with every editor found,
  the default first, and the project menu lists them the same way; the palette has *Open <project> in <editor>* for
  each project and *Open this folder in <editor>* for the focused session.
  A launch that fails is a toast with the daemon's reason.
- **Resource meter** (`ResourceMeter.tsx`, `lib/resources.ts`; 2026-10-10,
  the owner asked, translated, "can we measure the computer's CPU/memory load
  in particular sessions, or Caprock's own consumption?"). One report from
  `GET /v1/resources` and the `resources` frame every 5 s
  ([03-contracts.md § Resources](03-contracts.md#resources)), read four ways:
  the status strip's figures for the session in front; a small flame in the
  warning colour after the title of a session's sidebar row and its tab while
  the session is **hot** — its process tree over one core, or over 2 GiB,
  for a minute — with what and for how long in the tooltip ("Hot: its
  processes have used 182% CPU for 3 min — mostly node vite"); *Processes*
  in the inspector under the cockpit (beside a shell under its changes), the
  three busiest with CPU and memory and *Hot for N min*; and in Settings'
  *About this install*, *Caprock itself*: memory and CPU of the daemon, the
  app's own process and the terminal holders, the holders no Caprock will
  attach to again as *N orphans*, and on the machine (not a paired device)
  **Stop N orphans** for the ones a reap may stop, saying afterwards how many
  stopped and how many were left because something still runs in them. Absent
  everywhere until the daemon has measured, and with a daemon without the
  meter.
- **Caprock footprint** (`FootprintPanel.tsx`, `lib/footprint.ts`;
  2026-10-13, the owner asked, translated: "at the bottom, in the status
  bar, show the memory Caprock takes and what it is spent on, so a user can
  tell at a glance whether a slowdown is Caprock or not; also disk"). One
  source, `GET /v1/footprint` ([03-contracts.md §
  Footprint](03-contracts.md#footprint)), pushed as a `footprint` frame on
  `/v1/live` when it changes, and asked on mount and once a minute while the
  page is visible as the fallback, not at all while it is hidden (`useApi` →
  `everyWhileVisible`).
  The status strip shows *caprock <memory> · <disk>*: `memory.total_mem_bytes`
  (daemon, app, its web view helpers, terminal holders, other daemons) and
  `disk.total_bytes`. Below a 1,250 px window (`NARROW_STRIP_PX`, measured:
  narrower, an agent's figures with the sidebar closed were cut) it says
  memory alone and the disk moves to the tooltip. While the last orphan scan
  counted any, a third figure follows the disk, *· 3 orphans*
  (`orphanFigure(…, true)`, `data-orphan-figure`; gone in a narrow window
  with the disk), and the tooltip says *Orphans: 3 · 410 MB*. Muted grey; the warning
  colour with a small dot while any verdict is `warn`. Before the first
  answer, and from a daemon without the endpoint, it falls back to the
  resource meter's memory and opens nothing. A click (or the palette's
  *Caprock doctor*, no chord of its own — rule 12) opens the doctor as a
  420 px popover above the strip, held inside the viewport on narrow windows, closed by Escape or a click outside
  (`useMenuDismiss`). It is written for a person who is not an engineer
  (2026-10-15; the owner, translated: "what is this, nothing is
  understandable" about the earlier wall of pids and paths):
  - **Header.** *Caprock resources*, *Checked just now* and **Check
    now** (asks at once, *Checking…* until a newer answer).
  - **One verdict line** (`doctorHeadline`): *✓ All good* in the ok colour,
    or an amber dot and *Needs attention* (*Caprock is not running* without
    a daemon). Memory and on-disk totals sit on a separate labelled line.
    The verdict is 19 px, row titles 15 px, explanations 14 px and each
    row's measured value 18 px (owner, 2026-10-18: the earlier small text
    was hard to read). On the machine, while anything listed is safe to
    stop, **Clean up (frees ~154 MB)** offers the existing per-item review.
    Counts read *3 processes to review · 410 MB*, without the orphan jargon.
  - **Rows** (`doctorRows`), problems first, each a title with a count, one
    short line of why, then the size and **Stop…** on their own line: *Test copies of
    Caprock* (other daemons whose data or binary sits in a temporary folder,
    an agent scratchpad or a `.claude/worktrees` checkout — "Started by agent
    work, not your app"; *Other copies of Caprock* otherwise), *The desktop
    app is open twice*, *Unused Caprock terminals*, *Older install sessions* (`caprock_old_holder`, 2026-10-18: "Still running from a previous Caprock install." — never amber), *Background commands*, *Leftover Claude sessions*, *Leftover Codex sessions* (2026-10-18), *Leftover helpers*
    (MCP servers and what a Claude Code session started), *Disk reads*
    (*1.8 GB / hour*, "Above the usual 500 MB/hour limit." or
    "Normal for Caprock"), *History is large*, *A database file has grown*.
    A row is amber (dot, and the disk figure) only while its verdict warns;
    the rest are neutral grey. Before the panel's own scan lands, the
    strip's counts stand in as *Leftover processes (N)* with *Looking for
    what can be stopped…*, and no Stop.
  - **Details**, a disclosure under each row, closed on every open: per
    process its label, pid, memory, CPU (once there is a rate) and age,
    *started by: worktree phone-end* / *agent job 0f3a9c1e* (a
    `~/.claude/jobs/<id>/` copy, counted as a test copy) / *agent scratchpad
    caprock-triage* / *a temporary folder* (`startedFrom`), the command line
    as `$ <cmd>` (`data-orphan-cmd`, credentials already hidden by the
    daemon), the executable and data directory in small monospace
    wrapping anywhere, the daemon's `why`; then the verdict's own sentence.
  - **The stop.** Clean up and every row's Stop open the same inline
    confirmation (under the verdict, or under that row): *Stop these? Untick
    any you want to keep. Anything unsaved in them is lost.*, then every
    process on its own line with its own checkbox, its plain name (*Test
    copy of Caprock · worktree phone-end*, *Caprock Mail helper · running
    7m*) and memory, and **Stop N · frees ~X** (focused) with **Cancel**;
    Escape cancels it without closing the panel. Ticked by default
    (`safeToStop`): test copies, terminals the daemon would reap itself,
    caprock commands outside an app bundle, ended claudes and codexes, MCP
    servers and Claude Code leftovers. Unticked, with why: a daemon for a
    non-temporary data folder ("it may be a Caprock you use"), a terminal
    something still runs in, an older install's terminal ("A session still
    runs in it; stopping ends that session."), a caprock command inside an
    app. The click sends `api.stopOrphans` with each ticked pid and the
    `started_at` shown — SIGTERM — then says *Stopped 6, about 154 MB
    freed.* (or how many could not be) and asks again. Absent on a paired
    device ([ADR-059](08-decisions.md)).
  - **Force stop** (2026-10-18, ADR-059 amended). What the daemon reports
    `running` — still there 5 s after SIGTERM — is listed in its own box
    under the verdict (`data-force-stop-box`): *Still running after a polite
    stop. Force stop ends it at once; nothing in it gets to save.*, each by
    plain name, pid and memory, then **Force stop N** (`data-force-stop`,
    the same targets with `force: true`, SIGKILL) and **Leave running**.
    Nothing is force-stopped without that second click.
  - **Footer.** *Resource breakdown* (`aria-expanded`; *Hide
    breakdown* when open) shows the earlier sections under small headers —
    Memory (daemon, window = app + web views, terminal holders, orphans,
    other daemons; CPU beside each), Disk (history database, pastes, logs,
    programs, other; *Storage settings →*), Disk activity (last minute and
    last hour), Done on its own (the last two actions) — and **Copy
    report**. The report is built client-side and is `caprock doctor`'s text
    line for line: both are pinned to `testdata/footprint/doctor.{json,txt}`
    (`footprint.test.ts`, `cmd/caprock/doctor_text_test.go`).
  While the panel is open it asks `GET /v1/footprint?orphans=1` every 10 s
  itself (`useFootprintWithOrphans`), which the daemon answers from a scan
  up to 30 s old; **Check now** adds `&fresh=1` for a new one. The earlier layouts — stacked sections
  (owner's pick, 2026-10-13) with a warnings box and an *Orphans* list whose
  Stop sat behind an *Advanced* checkbox — were replaced on 2026-10-15; the
  sections live on behind the breakdown.
- **Status strip** (`StatusStrip.tsx`): connection, the 5-hour and 7-day plan
  windows and today's spend (only while the sidebar is hidden — its Today
  strip shows them otherwise); while either window is at 70% or more and no
  licence is active, *pause before the limit* beside them, which opens the
  Premium dialog on the plan-window stop (PremiumHint's rule: beside the
  problem, never on a quiet day); at the right end the header's way to buy at
  the strip's size, *Premium*, or *premium yearly/lifetime* once paid (owner,
  2026-10-17: with a terminal in front the header is not on screen); with an agent's tab in front its model,
  context fill, next-call price and session cost (`SessionFigures`; added
  2026-10-10 because the bar stood empty beside the open sidebar), then what
  that session's processes use now — *agent cpu 182% ram 1.3 GB*, labelled
  so it is not read as Caprock's (owner, 2026-10-12), CPU summed over its
  process tree so 100% is one core, the three busiest processes in the
  tooltip, the CPU in the warning colour while the session is hot
  (`SessionLoad`, § Resource meter). Resting the pointer on it for 250 ms,
  focusing it or clicking it opens the **resource popover**
  (`ResourcePopover.tsx`, `lib/resourceHistory.ts`; 2026-10-15, the owner
  asked, translated: "I want a popover here that shows graphs of resource
  consumption"): a 380 px card above the bar, drawn into the page's body and
  kept inside the window, with *15 min* / *1 hour*, then CPU (*100% is one
  core*) and Memory as two small SVG charts on one scale each — this agent
  (accent, filled), all sessions (violet) and Caprock (muted, dashed) — a
  line per series with its figure now, peak and average under each chart;
  the pointer over a chart draws a hairline and shows each figure at that
  sample. *Using the most now* lists up to five sessions by CPU, then
  memory, from the latest report (the session in front marked *in front*,
  the hot one's CPU in the warning colour, its busiest processes in the
  tooltip); a row brings that session's tab forward (AppShell's
  `openSessionById`). When the history is shorter than the window it says
  since when and that Caprock measures only while one of its windows is
  open. A hover-opened card closes 200 ms after the pointer leaves both; a
  click keeps it until Escape (which hands the focus back), its × or a
  click outside. History comes from `GET /v1/resources/history`
  ([03-contracts.md § Resources](03-contracts.md#resources)) when it opens,
  after the newest sample already held, and while open each `resources`
  frame is appended: no polling of its own, nothing drawn while closed, and
  nothing animates — the charts' right edge is the newest sample, so they
  change only when one lands. Theme tokens throughout, so light, dark and
  Sunlight need nothing of their own. Then, at the right and whether or not
  a session is in front, *caprock 1.1 GB · 2.2 GB*, which opens the
  doctor panel (§ Caprock footprint); the front
  terminal's state only while it is
  not live (*reconnecting*), the keep-awake mark whenever the mode is not off
  (`KeepAwakeIndicator`, asked every 15 s, every 3 s while open; ADR-049):
  *awake* in accent with a green dot while Caprock holds the computer awake,
  *may sleep* faint while it does not and *awake off* faint when the mode is off (always drawn, so the status and the way back are one click away), and a click opens a popover with what
  it is doing in a few large words (*Awake now · 1 agent working*), the
  three modes, one short line for the chosen one and a close button (owner,
  2026-10-11: he could not tell whether it was active, then: too many words,
  no way to close it), the version, which opens the version panel
  (`VersionPanel`, owner 2026-10-11: "I want to see whether there is an
  update and when it was last checked"): the version large; up to date says
  so in a sentence, *✓ You're on the latest version* (2026-10-16, owner: "no
  clear message that it's up to date, all good" — a small green tag beside
  the title did not read); or *X is out* (an accent dot on the version when
  one is); *Checked …*; then **one button, the useful one**: the one-click
  update (or the commands) with *Release notes* when one is out, **Check
  again** right after a check that found nothing newer (under 10 minutes),
  with *What's new in vX* as a link on the *Checked …* line (2026-10-19,
  owner: Check again is pressed far more often), and *Check now* otherwise; and a × in its top corner beside Esc and a click
  outside (2026-10-14). The release's
  notes were in the panel until the same evening, raw markdown wrapped at
  380px, and the owner found them unreadable ("keep it simple about the
  version; whoever needs the release notes will click", translated); when the
  app and its daemon run different versions, an amber dot on the version
  and, in the panel, both versions and *Quit and reopen Caprock* (the app
  replaces its own daemon on launch, ADR-040); the
  daemon's version (read again whenever the live link reconnects and on
  window focus, because the app can swap its daemon under a loaded page;
  `lib/useDaemonVersion.ts`), and — when a newer release is out and release checks are on — *vX.Y.Z is
  out* in the accent colour (F12, `AppUpdateNotice.tsx`; see § Update
  notice).
  The version's tooltip carries the front terminal's protocol and size
  (*terminal protocol v2 · 167×36*); they sat in the strip until the owner
  called them "incomprehensible junk" (2026-10-09, translated).
  Percentages are rounded as Now rounds them (`readWindow`).
- **Command palette** (⌘K, `CommandPalette.tsx`): *Waiting* first, then
  actions, *Saved*, tabs, sessions and projects (with *New agent in <project>*). A
  query ranks a match at the start of the name over one at a word start over
  one anywhere, and the group with the best match leads. Text that matches
  nothing offers *New agent on "<text>"*: the new-agent sheet with the text as
  the first message and a new worktree named after it (`worktreeSlug`).
- **Attempts** (ADR-052). New agent, under *New copy from a branch*:
  *Attempts* (1 agent, 2–4 agents with the same message; 1 every time the
  sheet opens), the line under it naming `caprock/<name>-1` to `-N` and the
  base, and Start reading *Start 3*; two or more without a first message is
  refused in the sheet's error line. `POST /v1/agents {attempts}` answers
  `session_ids`; the shell opens one tab per attempt in order and brings
  the first to the front, then a toast (*3 attempts started, one copy each.
  Compare them from ⌘K or the inspector.*, or the attempt that did not
  start). `sessionTitle` prefixes *attempt n/N · * (`attemptLabel`), so tabs,
  the sidebar and the palette tell them apart. ⌘K lists *Compare attempts:
  <title>* per group in the session list; the inspector of an attempt says
  *Attempt n of N · Compare*. **Compare** (`AttemptsCompare.tsx`) covers the
  terminals like the Changes view (z-20; moving to a tab or Esc closes it):
  a header *Compare attempts · title · from base*, then one card per attempt
  in an auto-fit grid (220 px minimum, so it stacks on a narrow window) with
  *Attempt n of N* and its state, the sidebar's own reading (`dotOf`):
  *working* green, *waiting on you* amber, *looping* red, *idle*, *done*,
  *exited N* red, *running* for one the sidebar does not list; agent, model and
  branch, then Files changed, Lines (+green −red), Cost so far (*cheapest*
  when the attempts differ) and Time (to now while it runs, to its last work
  once ended), a grey line when the copy is gone, and *Open* and *Review
  changes* (the copy's Changes view; absent without changes to read). A
  closing line says nothing is merged for you. Fetched every 5 s while open.
- **From GitHub** (`GitHubItemPicker.tsx`, `lib/fromGitHub.ts`). New agent
  shows a link-styled *From a GitHub issue or pull request…* under Project
  and Where only when the project's remote is on github.com and GitHub is
  connected; otherwise nothing, so a user who never connected GitHub sees no
  new row. ⌘K → *New agent from issue…* opens the sheet on the picker
  itself; there, not connected reads *Connect GitHub in Settings → GitHub to
  start from an issue or a pull request.* The picker is a search field over
  a 220 px list: *#number*, a *PR* or *draft PR* tag, the title, then the
  author and up to four labels as neutral tags (GitHub's label colours are
  not used: they ignore the theme). Typing filters by number, title words,
  author or label; when GitHub has more than the 100 listed, two or more
  letters also go to GitHub's search after 350 ms and the hits join the
  list. ↑↓ move, Enter or a click picks, Esc closes the picker before the
  sheet. A pick shows a one-line strip (*Issue #12* · the title, linked ·
  *Change* · ×) and sets, all editable: Where = new copy (for this start;
  the remembered choice is untouched), the name `issue-12-short-slug` or
  `pr-13-short-slug`, and the first message — *Work on issue #12: title*,
  the link, the text; for a pull request *Review and continue pull request
  #13: title*, the link, a line that the copy is at its head, the text. The
  text drops HTML comments (templates' hints) and is cut at about 3,000
  characters at a line break with *[…trimmed; the rest is at the link
  above]*. A pull request replaces *From branch* with *From · pull request
  #13, its head* and hides the pull checkbox; Start sends `worktree_pr`.
  × keeps the fields as they are and only drops the link.
- **Saved prompts and commands** (ADR-051, `lib/saved.ts`,
  `SavedPromptsSetting.tsx`). One list kept by the daemon
  (`/v1/saved-prompts`), read once per page and shared by Settings, the
  palette and the chips (`useSavedPrompts`; a change made here bumps a
  version the per-session lists refetch on). The palette's *Saved* group and
  *Run saved…* (⌘K, the strip's + menu; a palette narrowed to them, with
  *Add or edit saved prompts and commands* opening Settings at the section)
  list the rows for every project and for the project in front (the focused
  session's folder, else the current project). A prompt is typed into the
  focused session when it is a running session Caprock started (not a shell,
  not detached) — `sendPrompt`, the message field's `deliver`: ESC CR per
  line break, then `\r` after 80 ms — with a toast *Sent “name” to title*;
  otherwise New agent opens with it as the first message. A command is
  `POST /v1/shells {type, run: true}` in the focused session's cwd, else the
  project root, opened as a new shell tab. Settings → *Saved prompts and
  commands* (machine only, after *Keep awake* in both orders): the list with
  kind, name, scope and text, *Edit* (the form below takes the row, accent
  border on it) and *Delete*; the form has Kind, Name, For (*Every project*
  or a project, unlisted ones kept by name), and a textarea for a prompt or
  a one-line mono field for a command (Enter saves), checked as the daemon
  checks before a save (`draftProblem`). The chips (`TerminalKeys`
  `chips`, in `ChatView` and the terminal's keys bar) are the session's
  prompts from `?session=`, one scrolling row of rounded buttons above the
  field, absent when there are none; a tap puts the text in the field (on
  a new line after what is there) without focusing it, so a phone's
  keyboard does not rise, and never sends.
- **Menu bar popover** (macOS, `screens/TrayPanel.tsx`, model in
  `lib/traydata.ts`, route `#/tray` as its own chunk): needs-you first
  (permission prompts oldest first; Approve only when the request is
  non-empty, one line and at most 200 characters, else Deny and *Review in
  Caprock*; then turns that ended), then running sessions (agent, project ·
  title, activity, elapsed, cost; 8 at most), then a fixed band with today's
  spend and the 5h/7d bars for Claude and Codex (`readWindow` rounding, reset
  countdown, stale greyed), and *Open Caprock* with the hotkey. Calm state:
  "Nothing needs you". ↑ ↓ move, Enter opens, Esc hides. It fetches only
  while shown ([app/README.md](../app/README.md) § Menu bar popover).
- **Keyboard** (`lib/appkeys.ts`, one function both the terminal and the
  window ask): ⌘T new shell, ⇧⌘N new agent (agent, model, permissions, the
  checkout or a worktree or a new one, a first message), ⌥⌘N a quick chat
  (Ctrl+Alt+Shift+N off macOS; the one app chord with Option, which is
  otherwise the terminal's Meta: since ADR-071 it starts
  `POST /v1/agents {chat: true}` at once on the vendor and model used last,
  opened as a tab of *Outside projects* named *Quick chat · <model>* that
  shows the conversation (ChatView), not the terminal; a click on *Quick
  chat* in the sidebar, the strip's + menu or the palette does the same, and
  *Quick chat with…* in the + menu and the palette opens the vendor and
  model chooser, which from 2026-10-12 to ADR-071 every Quick chat opened
  first ([21-app.md](21-app.md) § What the user sees; an error is a toast), ⌘O add a project
  (folder, new, clone), ⇧⌘H the quick capture into Second Brain
  (Ctrl+Shift+H off macOS, which no shell, PSReadLine or GNOME binds; ⌘M
  stays the window's Minimize; § Second Brain), ⌘1–8 the strip's Nth tab and ⌘9 the last
  (Ctrl+Shift+1–9 off macOS), the strip in the order the tabs were opened:
  a new tab goes last and takes the next number, and only closing a tab or
  dragging one changes the numbers; the order is kept across restarts
  with the workspace (owner, 2026-10-14, translated: "I sometimes work
  with 5 projects at once, sometimes 2. I just want to switch between them
  with key combos"). Projects have no digit chords: ⌃⌘1–⌃⌘9 (Alt+Shift+1–9
  off macOS) were tried for them and turned down the same day. ⌘N meant
  the sidebar's Nth top-level item from 2026-10-13 to 2026-10-14
  ("Command+digit focuses that project right away", translated) and the
  strip's Nth tab before that. A one-time line says so after the update
  (`KeysHint`: "⌘1–9 switch tabs in the order you opened them ·
  double-click a tab to rename", *All keys* opens the shortcuts sheet,
  *Got it* puts it away,
  `caprock.app.keysHint` in this browser's storage; shown while a tab is
  open, one line in the flow above the bottom strips, so it covers no
  terminal and no banner). ⌥⌘↓ / ⌥⌘↑ the next /
  previous session of the front tab's project, in either sidebar mode (`sessionStops` in
  `ProjectRow.tsx`: its tabs and its active rows with no tab, in the
  sidebar's fixed order, round the end; one with no tab opens as a click
  does), ⌥⌘→ / ⌥⌘← the next / previous project, round the end — Alt+Shift
  arrows off macOS. He asked for ⌘↑ ⌘↓ and ⌥← ⌥→: ⌥← ⌥→ are word-jump in
  every shell and in Claude Code's prompt, and ⌘ arrows are scroll, prompt
  jumps and line start / end in the Mac terminals; ⌥⌘ arrows reach no shell
  and already mean next tab or pane in Chrome and iTerm. Off macOS every
  other spelling is taken: Ctrl+Shift+← → are PowerShell's
  SelectBackwardWord / SelectNextWord (PSReadLine's Windows mode, pwsh on
  Linux too; about_PSReadLine_Functions), Ctrl+arrows its and readline's
  word moves, Ctrl+PageUp / PageDown its ScrollDisplayUpLine / DownLine,
  Ctrl+Alt arrows switch GNOME workspaces and Ctrl+Alt+Shift arrows move the
  window to one. Alt+Shift arrows are bound by none of PSReadLine (any
  mode), bash's readline, zsh's emacs keymap or GNOME, and VS Code already
  ships them as Windows / Linux defaults, so the chords are Alt+Shift
  arrows there (first shipped as Ctrl+Shift and changed before merge). In a
  text field (not the terminal's own) the arrow chords stay the field's
  word and line selection (`isArrowCommand`, `isTextField` in AppShell),
  and held down they repeat. An arrow pressed as one of these chords stays
  the app's until that key is released (`watchChordArrows`, `isChordTail`):
  let go of the modifiers before the arrow and the OS goes on repeating it
  as ⌥↓ or a bare ↓, which reached the terminal in front, the session the
  chord had just opened, and Claude Code took that Down into its
  background-agents list and opened one (owner, 2026-10-14, translated:
  "sometimes it switches me to a subagent, I never understood why"). The
  sidebar's ⌥⇧↑ ⌥⇧↓ (Ctrl+Shift off macOS) do nothing in a terminal for the
  same reason: typed, they were Down too (`isRowMoveKey`). Plain arrows and
  ⌥← ⌥→ word jumps are the terminal's as before; the terminal asks
  `ownsTerminalKey`, and `TerminalPane.keys.test.tsx` presses every arrow
  chord as macOS, Windows and Linux spell it against the real xterm and
  checks that nothing reaches the socket. ⌃Tab / ⇧⌘[ ] cycle, ⌘W
  close the tab (or the focused pane) and with the Dashboard in front
  nothing — in the macOS app its menu's File → Close Tab holds ⌘W and hands
  it to the page as `CLOSE_TAB_EVENT`, so the window is never closed by it
  ([21-app.md](21-app.md) F09) — ⌘K the command palette, ⌘I the
  inspector (⌘B, the session deck's until 2026-10-20, is no longer the app's)
  ([21-app.md](21-app.md) § What the user sees), ⌘\ the sidebar, ⌘D or ⌘0 (or ⇧⌘D, unlisted) the Dashboard tab where it was left, and with the dashboard in front Home at its top, and from Home's top back to the terminals (§ The dashboard keeps its place), ⇧⌘G (Ctrl+Shift+G) the Changes view — the diff — of the session in front's worktree (a toast when it is not in a git project; the Changes view shows the key) — its key is drawn as a key in the sidebar, and the pinned tab is its icon and its key (⌘0 for Dashboard, ⌘, for Settings), its name in the tooltip (owner, 2026-10-11: it was a word until five tabs were open; its key went into the tooltip on 2026-10-13 and back onto the tab on 2026-10-14) — ⌘E / ⇧⌘E split right / down,
  ⌘[ ⌘] the previous / next pane (macOS), ⌘J the next session waiting on you,
  ⌘F find in the focused terminal (with Settings in front, its search; § Settings search), ⌘P the Pipeline tab and back, ⇧⌘W the
  Work page and back, ⇧⌘T the last closed tab back (§ Recently closed,
  under the Flat list), ⌥⌘P the theme picker at the foot of the sidebar
  (the sidebar is shown first when hidden; the picker takes the focus on
  the palette in use, Tab tries the next, Enter keeps, Esc closes), ⌘+ ⌘− ⇧⌘0 the interface size (the app
  only: `lib/uiscale.ts` → the shell's native `set_zoom`, 100–140%, 110% by
  default; CSS zoom would desync xterm's mouse). The new-agent sheet is filled without the
  mouse (owner, 2026-10-07): focus opens on the first message, Tab walks
  Project, Where, Agent, Model, Permissions, First message, Cancel, Start;
  ↑ ↓ change a select in place (`lib/selectKeys.ts` — WebKit and Chrome on
  macOS otherwise open the popup); ⌘↩ starts from anywhere in the sheet,
  buttons included; Esc cancels; the footer names the keys and Start shows
  its own (`⌘↩`, `Ctrl+↵` off macOS, `aria-keyshortcuts`). `Sheet` focuses
  once on open and restores once on close — its `onClose` is read through a
  ref, because an inline callback from a re-rendering AppShell used to re-run
  the effect and throw focus back to Project mid-typing (owner, 2026-10-09).
  *Where* avoids the word worktree (it is in the tooltip): *This folder ·
  branch*, *Existing copy · branch*, and first *New copy from a branch*,
  which shows *From branch* (`BranchPicker.tsx`: type to filter, main
  branches first, the default one marked), *New branch name* (optional), a
  *Pull the latest … first* box when the branch has a remote (on by default,
  remembered) and one line naming `caprock/<name>` from the base, then
  **Setup** (`ProjectSetupCommand.tsx`, ADR-050): the project's setup
  command, *runs in a new copy before its agent starts*, with *Add* / *Edit*
  (Enter saves, Esc cancels, neither reaches the sheet) and a line saying its
  output shows in the agent's terminal, a failure or ten minutes starts the
  agent anyway and Ctrl+C skips it; *None.* when unset, the tooltip naming
  `.worktreeinclude` (the visible text never says worktree). Read-only on a
  paired device. Choosing a
  new copy is remembered across sheets (`caprock.newAgent.where`): the owner
  starts every agent from a fresh main (2026-10-10). The dashboard's
  New session dialog takes the same arrows. The first bypass session on a
  machine shows `BypassConsentNote` above the start button, which reads
  *Accept and start* (ADR-041); a 409 `bypass_consent` from the daemon shows
  the same note instead of an error line. Off macOS each is Ctrl+Shift, and Ctrl+Shift+C/V stay the
  terminal's copy and paste. The terminal never sees these keys and they never
  fire from the terminal's own (Ctrl+C, Ctrl+W, Option as Meta). Every
  key shown in the UI (hints, tooltips, the shortcuts sheet) is written once
  in macOS spelling and passed through `keys()` in `lib/appkeys.ts`, which
  writes it Ctrl+Shift, Ctrl+Alt+Shift, Ctrl+Enter or F5 off macOS and drops
  the pane keys, which have none there. Until 2026-10-10 the labels were
  hard-coded ⌘ and Windows users reported shortcuts they could not press.
  Every key hint is drawn as a key (`.app-kbd` and `.app-kbd-key` are one
  look since 2026-10-11, 13px, bordered): the sidebar's *New agent*, *Quick
  chat*, *Add project*, *Dashboard* and *Settings* hints were faint text, and
  the owner wants people to work from the keyboard ("every key shown, big").
  **Off macOS a key chip is drawn on demand** (`keyChipMode`, a Windows
  user's screenshot, 2026-10-19): there the same keys are Ctrl+Shift+N and
  Ctrl+Alt+Shift+T, 80–140px, and drawn always they crowded and cut the
  labels beside them (*Settings* under its chip, the palette's name half
  hidden, *Quick chat* on two lines, the header's Ctrl+Shift+B and
  Ctrl+Shift+I dominating it). A row's chip (`.app-kbd-reveal`: the
  sidebar's New agent, Quick chat and Add project, the footer's rows, the
  modules' rows, Inspector) is out of the flow and shows while the
  keyboard focus is on its row, floating just above the row's right end
  (under the button for Inspector), so it covers none of the row's
  words or links. **Not on hover** (since 2026-10-20): under the pointer the
  row's tooltip, which names the key, is the one hint. Both used to show, the
  chip at once and the tooltip a second later, and a Windows tester
  (2026-10-20, translated) said "showing both makes no sense — one is
  enough, the fuller one"; every row with such a chip carries its key in its
  `title`. Every chip shows at once while Ctrl is held
  alone for 450ms (`lib/keyreveal.ts`, `data-show-keys` on the root, the
  way Windows apps show access keys while Alt is held); any other key, a
  click, letting go or the window losing focus hides them, so Ctrl+C in a
  terminal never flashes them. A tab's chip and a session row's
  (`.app-kbd-held`) show only while Ctrl is held, since hovering either
  puts its × where the key is. At rest off macOS every label is whole; on
  a Mac the chips are short and always drawn, as before. **No label off
  macOS holds a Mac glyph** (`isPlainKeyLabel`, checked by the *every
  listed shortcut, on every OS* test): the tabs' `Ctrl⇧1` read "Ctrl@1"
  on Windows, since the bundled JetBrains Mono has no ⇧ (U+21E7 is in none
  of its subsets), nor has Consolas, and the fallback Windows drew at 10px
  looked like @. `stripKey` and `tabKey` now write the whole chord there,
  Ctrl+Shift+1, the key that switches to that tab.
  Every tab shows its ⌘1–⌘9 key (Ctrl+Shift+1 off macOS, while Ctrl is held; tabs past the ninth
  none, ⌘9 the last), as a compact `.app-kbd-sm` in the slot the × takes
  on hover; the tab in front shows the key and its × side by side. The
  tab's sidebar row shows the same key (`tabDigits` on `Sidebar`, `digits`
  on `TabStrip` and `SessionList`, by tab id), in Flat and in Tree, as the
  footer's readable `.app-kbd-key`; a project row has none. The project in
  front is told by its bold name alone — its faint edge went on 2026-10-15,
  when it read as a second selection beside the tab in front's.
  ⇧⌘E and ⇧⌘0 are Ctrl+Alt+Shift+E and Ctrl+Alt+Shift+0 off macOS: as
  Ctrl+Shift they were already the terminal's split and the interface size's
  reset on another key; so are ⇧⌘F, ⇧⌘T and ⇧⌘W (Ctrl+Alt+Shift+F, T, W),
  beside ⌘F Find, ⌘T New shell and ⌘W Close, and the modules' ⇧⌘1–9 beside
  the tabs (`OFF_MAC_ALT`). **Keys named
  after what they open** (owner, 2026-10-15, translated: "bind shortcuts to
  the names: ⌘D Dashboard (keep ⌘0 too), ⌘F feedback, ⌘P Pipeline, ⌘T
  theme"): ⌘D Dashboard, ⌘P Pipeline, and — since ⌘F and ⌘T stay a
  terminal's Find and New shell — ⇧⌘F feedback (unchanged) and ⇧⌘T the
  theme; ⇧⌘W Work beside them. **On 2026-10-20 ⇧⌘T became Reopen closed
  tab**, as in every browser (owner), and the theme moved to **⌥⌘P** (P
  for the palettes it lists; Ctrl+Alt+Shift+P off macOS). Not ⌥⌘T: off
  macOS that would read Ctrl+Alt+Shift+T, the reopen key there. ⌥⌘P is no
  item of Tauri's default macOS menu, no Mac browser's page-reaching
  default (their tools are ⌥⌘I, J, C, U, L and B), and ⌘ never reaches a
  shell; Ctrl+Alt+Shift+P is bound by no shell, readline, PSReadLine,
  Windows Terminal or GNOME default, and P is no AltGr letter on Polish,
  Czech, German or French layouts, where Ctrl+Alt types characters. Each was checked against Tauri's default
  macOS menu (no ⌘D, ⌘P, ⇧⌘T, ⌥⌘P or ⇧⌘W item; ⌘W is the app's Close Tab,
  there is no Print), the terminal (an app chord never reaches xterm,
  `ownsTerminalKey`) and every `SHORTCUTS` row: off macOS ⌘D is
  Ctrl+Shift+D, never bare Ctrl+D (a shell's end of input), and ⌘P is
  Ctrl+Shift+P, which no other key of the app spells; a test fails when two
  rows read the same off macOS. In a browser tab ⇧⌘T and ⇧⌘W stay the
  browser's (reopen a tab, close the window); the palette and the buttons
  reach both there. **Any change to a shortcut is checked on Windows and
  Linux in the same PR** (owner, 2026-10-11): the *every listed shortcut, on
  every OS* suite in `lib/appkeys.test.ts` presses the off-macOS spelling of
  every `SHORTCUTS` row and fails when it does not reach the same command.
  Keys drawn as keys use `.app-kbd-key` (fg colour, 600 weight): the faint
  grey chip was not seen beside *Review changes*.
- **Outside the window** (WP-09, WP-10; the shell side is
  [app/README.md](../app/README.md) § Menu bar, badge and global hotkey). The
  page computes and the shell draws: the menu bar (tray off macOS) with
  Claude's and Codex's 5-hour and 7-day windows, today's spend and the
  sessions waiting for approval, each opening its session; the 5-hour figure
  and the waiting count beside the macOS icon; the Dock badge with the
  waiting count (`lib/tray.ts`). ⌃⌥⌘C (Win+Alt+C) shows or hides the window,
  set in Settings → *Global shortcut*. An OS notification for a permission
  prompt names the project, the session and the call (`lib/notify.ts`),
  never for the session in the focused tab of a focused window; on macOS it
  carries **Approve** only when its body holds the whole request unclipped,
  otherwise **Open in Caprock**, plus **Deny** either way
  ([21-app.md § Notifications](21-app.md#notifications)).
- **User docs.** [docs/app.md](../docs/app.md) is the public description of
  this layout, with demo-data screenshots (`docs/shot-app*.png`); change it
  with the layout.


**From anywhere (ADR-058).** The same dashboard, built with `vite build
--mode remote`, is the phone's page at an https origin of its own
(`ui/src/remote/`). `RemoteRoot` decides the machine before the dashboard
loads: a pairing link adds it and hands its code to the ordinary pairing
screen; `fetch` and `WebSocket` for `/v1/…` go through the encrypted channel;
everything below that is the screens described above. A strip along the top
names the machine, a dot says whether the channel is open, connecting or the
machine is unreachable, and a tap lists the machines on this phone with
*Forget* and *+ Add a machine* (paste a pairing link: an iPhone home-screen
app keeps its own storage). Settings → *Open Caprock on your phone* gains a
*From anywhere* row with its switch and state; with it on, the QR opens the
phone's page through the relay, *Anywhere* leads the address choice, and
*Copy link* sits under the code.

**The channel is never silent** (owner, 2026-10-14, translated: "On the
phone I now have 'Connecting' and nothing"). `remote/transport.ts`'s
`Channel` gives an attempt 10 s from dialling the relay to the machine's
reply; while open it pings the relay every 25 s and with each ping asks the
machine itself (`/healthz` through the channel, 15 s; 5 s after the page
comes back to the foreground), because a relay's pong says nothing about a
machine asleep behind it. Down, it retries by itself after 1, 2, 4, 8, 15,
then every 30 s, and at once on `visibilitychange`, `online` and a
back-forward restore. Each failure carries a `problem`: `machine-offline`
(4404 from the relay), `no-answer`, `relay-unreachable`, `no-internet`,
`lost`, `wrong-machine` or `removed`. The last two are the only ones that
ask to pair again, and neither is read off one answer (owner, 2026-10-14,
translated: "my connection to Caprock dropped and it asks me to go to
Caprock settings again. It has to never drop"):

- **`wrong-machine`**: the machine hung up on the handshake (the relay's
  close 1000 before any reply) three times in a row with no good handshake
  between: its keys cannot read this phone's. A message that does not
  finish the handshake is skipped, not a verdict — a relay can hand a new
  phone a frame still on its way to the one before it, and the real reply
  follows (`noise.ts`'s `finish` leaves the handshake as it was).
- **`removed`**: a 401 to a `/v1` request that carried the device token
  (outside `/v1/pair`) makes the channel ask `GET /v1/pair/me` with that
  token; one 401 there drops the channel and asks again on a fresh
  connection, and only the second, on a separate connection, is `removed`.
  A 200 forgets the first. 403, 404 and 5xx — what an older daemon answers
  a newer page's new routes — never start a check. The daemon is unchanged
  for this: an unknown token and a revoked one still get the same 401
  (`lanauth.go`), and a v0.117.0 daemon is checked the same way.

Behind either pair-again screen the channel still checks once a minute
(`RECHECK_MS`) and comes back by itself if the machine answers. The
dashboard's own start-up check agrees: on the phone's page a 401 with a
token held is left to the channel (`App.tsx`'s `accessAfter`), and a tablet
on the Wi-Fi asks three times before the pairing form. `RemoteRoot` mounts the dashboard only once the
channel has opened, so its one-time pairing check never runs over a
half-made channel; before that it says *Reaching <name>…*. Down for 5 s, or
at once for the two pairing problems, `ConnectionProblem` says what is
wrong in a headline and a sentence, the next try's countdown, **Retry** and
**Pair again** (the add steps and the paste box). The three failures a
person can act on are told apart, each with its own fix (Vova, 2026-10-15,
translated: "the link doesn't load; maybe it's some restrictions, my VPN
isn't working well right now"): *Can't reach the relay* — *This phone
cannot reach relay.caprock.dev / m.caprock.dev. Check your connection or
VPN* (the relay's host and the page's); *Connected to the relay — your
computer is offline* (4404: asleep, off the internet, Caprock not running;
*… isn't answering* for `no-answer`); *Paired device removed: pair again*.
*This phone is offline* stays for `no-internet`. Under them a folded
**Details** (`connection-details`, `detailRows`) lists the page, the relay,
the last error in the channel's words with the relay's close code (`Channel.
lastError`), when it happened, the last try, the last time the channel was
open (*not since the page loaded*), when the page loaded and the tries since
— for a screenshot to whoever helps. The bar keeps a two-word version.

**A browser that is not paired says so** (Vova, 2026-10-15). The page
opened with nothing stored leads its first screen with *This browser isn't
paired. Paired this phone before? Its storage was cleared, or this is
another browser* (*— a Home Screen app keeps storage of its own* when
opened from one) *On your computer: Caprock → Settings → Use on your phone
→ Connect a phone*, before the steps (`not-paired-here`). A browser that has
the machine on its list but no token for it and no pairing under way
(`boot.notPairedHere`) gets the same words as a screen of its own with
**Pair this browser**, never a pairing form or a connection error. The
token is the pairing, so the page asks the browser to keep this origin's
storage (`navigator.storage.persist()`, `boot.keepStorage`) once a pairing
completes and on every load with a token; a refusal changes nothing.
Opening the page's plain address again is all a paired phone needs:
`prepare()` takes the current machine's token from its record, with
nothing in the address. A pairing link
for a machine already on the phone keeps its old token on the record while
the code is redeemed; a code that fails (spent by a second opening of the
same QR) falls back to it at once (`caprock:pair-failed` →
`boot.abandonPairing`), and a link with no code leaves the pairing alone.
The machine record is where the token lives: `prepare()` replaces it only
when a pairing completed, and a record from an older page that has the
token only in `caprock.device.token` takes it from there. The Ready card
never gates a phone redeeming a code or a machine that does not answer
`/v1/pair/me`. A new build of the page that
removed a chunk an open page asks for reloads it (`vite:preloadError`, at
most once a minute).

**Ready, on the phone** (`remote/ReadyCard.tsx`, 2026-10-14). Once the
channel is open, the dashboard waits behind a short card the first time
after pairing (`caprock.pair.justPaired`, set by the pairing screen before
its reload) and whenever the phone cannot type: **Ready** or *Connected,
view only*; ✓ *Connected to <computer>*; *This phone can type, start
agents, approve and stop* ✓, or ✗ *View only. To type, on the computer:
Caprock app → Settings → Use on your phone → Allow control next
to this phone* (asked again every 4 s, so it turns ✓ by itself); the
install button, *on this phone* ✓, or the iPhone steps; **Open sessions**.
Closed (× or Open sessions), it stays away until the role changes
(`caprock.remote.readyDismissed`, per machine and role). While the app is
not on this phone, the card ends with the way back (`ready-return`): *Next
time, just open m.caprock.dev on this phone: it stays paired.* — the page's
own host. Its install row gives Chrome on Android, when Chrome has not
offered its own install, *In Chrome: ⋮ → Install app. If the menu says Open
in app, it is already installed.* — never *Add to Home screen*, which makes
a second, plain shortcut beside an installed app. The card's
`GET /v1/pair/me?app=1|0` tells the computer whether this phone runs as the
installed app, for its checklist. The landing's steps carry the same four
lines.

**Installing, once** (`remote/install.ts`). Chrome's automatic install
prompt is held back (`beforeinstallprompt` prevented) and kept for the
page's own **Install the Caprock app** — on the first screen, and as
**Install** on a one-line strip under the bar in a browser tab — which
exists only while Chrome says the app is not installed. The app records in
storage (`caprock.remote.installed`) that it ran installed; Android shares
that storage with Chrome, so a browser tab then reads *Caprock is on your
home screen* instead. Safari on an iPhone gets *Share, then Add to Home
Screen*; other browsers get nothing. Each strip is dismissed for good per
offer. The manifest's `id` stays `/`: a redeploy is the same app, and a new
id would install a second one. The browser dashboard's own install hint
(`PhoneHome`) is not shown in this build, nor on a Wi-Fi link while access
from anywhere is on.

### The scrolling rule

`lib/useStickToBottom.ts` with the `NewPill` component (WP-11): a list follows
new rows only within 4 px of its live edge; elsewhere the first visible row and
its offset are recorded and restored after every change (a MutationObserver,
before paint; `overflow-anchor` off so Safari and Chrome behave alike); a
"↓ N new" pill counts what arrived and jumps back. It serves lists that grow
downwards and newest-first feeds (`edge: 'top'`): the session timeline and the
activity feed use it now. xterm.js already keeps a scrolled-up viewport still;
the app's terminal adds the "↓ N new lines" pill. Tests: 200 rows streamed
while scrolled up leave the first visible row within ±1 px, both directions,
and a trimmed capped list keeps its row (`useStickToBottom.test.tsx`); in
headless Chrome at 1440×900, the app terminal scrolled up while ~1,100 lines
streamed was pixel-identical before and after, with the pill reading
"↓ 990 new lines" (2026-10-05). The chat view uses it too (bottom edge); its
"load earlier" line has no height of its own, because older messages are
inserted below it and the first visible row with a height is the anchor. That
line also carries the auto top margin that holds a short conversation at the
bottom of the log. The dashboard terminal fits again when xterm's grid changes
size inside an unchanged box: the WebGL renderer, swapped in late, measures a
taller cell and kept the row count, and at 390 px the grid ran 62 px past its
box and over the keys bar's field.

### Session labels and state

`lib/sessionLabels.ts`, shared by Now, the pulse and the app sidebar (owner
report, 2026-10-05). When two different folders carry the same project name,
each session is labelled with the shortest unique path suffix
(`Downloads/caprock` vs `dev/caprock`); a unique name stays as it is. `HEAD`
is never printed as a branch (git's word for a detached checkout, and what is
recorded for a folder that is not a repository; the API carries no commit id
to say "detached @ sha"). State is `status` first: `ended` is ended whatever
the last narration's health said — a card once read "idle · was responding
20h ago" for a session `/v1/sessions` listed as ended. The card leads with the
title or first prompt; the short id is secondary.

## Repository and pull-request links

Owner request (2026-10-04): from a session or a project, one click to the
repository. The session header carries these on every tab. The two buttons
sit at the right end of the title line, opposite the title, which truncates
to make room; at phone width they wrap under it. The PR list sits at the
right end of the activity line. A row of their own pushed the terminal down
for nothing (owner feedback, 2026-10-04). On a phone's Chat tab the header is
compact and these sit under its **Details** toggle
([21-app.md § Phase A](21-app.md#phase-a--resilience-and-starting-work-p0)).

- **Open repo ↗** — the checked-out branch's page where the host has one,
  otherwise the repository; read from the local remote, never a forge's API
  (`SessionDetail.repo`, [03-contracts.md](03-contracts.md)).
- **Open PR #N ↗** — the newest pull request the session opened that no
  recorded command merged or closed.
- **The three latest pull requests** — `#N title ↗`, tagged *merged* or
  *closed* only when that action was recorded, then `+N more`.
- **Copy path** — when the repository has no remote a browser can open, the
  directory is shown with a copy button instead.

A Projects row carries **Open repo ↗** and **last PR #N ↗** beside its terminal
button. Every link opens a new tab with `rel="noopener"`, and says so: ↗ and
*Opens in a new tab* (§ Where am I).

### CI on the Pipeline and in the cockpit, and Investigate

The **session deck** — a band under the agent's terminal with one cell,
*Pull requests & CI* (`components/SessionDeck.tsx`, ⌘B, the strip's *Deck*
button, *Pipeline bar: On/Off* in the palette) — was removed on 2026-10-20
(owner, translated: "not needed any more"): the inspector's CI and release
