      or, with Memory between sessions off, that nothing goes until it is
      on (the list dimmed). Then the share held back at random to measure
      it (*25% of new sessions are held back at random*) and the status's
      count, *2 sessions got 9 memories in the last 24 h, 6 in all* (or
      *None has been handed any yet*). Nothing for the project: *Nothing
      kept for X yet*. It is read again on every `brain` frame, so a pin
      or a delete moves it at once.
    - **Recent memories**, the one way to browse: its count (*N memories*,
      *N memories match*, *the newest N read* past the limit, *including
      close matches*, *pinned first* without a search), a search (debounced
      200 ms; *Search titles and summaries — typos are fine*, its tooltip
      saying every word has to match, in any case or accent, and that a
      close spelling counts when little matches exactly; 03-contracts.md §
      Second Brain, *Search*), a project picker (projects with memories, by
      count) and a span (All time, 7, 30, 90 days), then the list below,
      40 rows and *Show 40 more*. The *List* view and its remembered choice
      (`caprock-brain-view`) are gone: the list is always here and the
      graph is the card.
  - **The side column**: the memory opened, when one is (§ *A memory
    opened*), then **the graph card** (`screens/brain/BrainGraphCard.tsx`):
    a square of the dot disc (the canvas's `mini` form — no labels, no pan
    or zoom), its dots in their projects' colours as in the opened graph,
    the caption *278 memories · 91 topics* and *Open the graph*.
    The card is one button and nothing in it reacts in place: a click, or
    Enter, unfolds the graph out of it. It always draws the whole brain, whatever the
    list is filtered to. Before the first memory it is the living brain
    (`BrainOrb`) instead, sparks flying in from the agents while it reads,
    and opens nothing. Under 1024px the column follows the list; a memory
    opened there scrolls into view and the card waits under it until the
    memory is closed.
  - **The unfold** (the owner, 2026-10-17, translated: "I'm missing that
    Second Brain unfolds and there is a beautiful graph with useful info").
    The card opens into the graph rather than cutting to it: the dialog is
    laid out at its full size at once and revealed from the card's
    rectangle outward (a clip-path over 480ms, so nothing inside is
    squashed), the backdrop darkening with it; the card's own picture, taken
    from its canvas at the click, grows from where it was to the middle of
    the graph and fades as the full graph fades in; the header and the
    panel fade up once the reveal is half done. The card stays in place,
    faded, while the graph is open. Closing (×, the backdrop, Escape) folds
    the dialog back into the card over 300ms when the card is on screen,
    else fades it. Under prefers-reduced-motion it opens and closes at once.
  - **The graph opened** (`BrainGraphDialog`): a dialog over the page, 20px
    from the window's edges from 640px and a full-screen sheet on a phone.
    The header has *Second Brain graph*, its caption (*12 memories picked
    out of 278* while something is picked out), **Find in the graph** and a
    project picker (*Every project*, then each project with its count), and
    a × (*Close the graph*). Find and the picker pick nodes out rather than
    reading the graph again: what they pick stays lit with a soft halo and
    its own links brighter, the rest fades to an eighth, and nothing is laid
    out again (`pickNodes`, `lib/brainview.ts`: every word in a memory's
    title, project or topics, folded as the daemon folds; a project keeps
    its node, its memories and the topics most of them are in). The
    interactive graph fills the dialog (below) and the panel sits beside it
    (300px, from 768px; under it on a phone, at most 46% of the height).
    Focus moves to the graph and back to the card on close. The ways out
    are those of every dialog: ×, a click on the backdrop, Escape — but
    Escape first lets go of a graph in hand, as it does on its own, and the
    next one closes.
    **Hide details / Show details** collapses the inspector to give the graph
    the whole width; clicking a node opens it again. **Display**, folded over
    the upper left of the canvas, adjusts node size, link visibility and
    label density, with **Reset display**. These controls last for the open
    dialog and redraw without moving nodes or resetting the camera. Zero
    label density hides ambient names; a focus or search still names its
    matches. Bottom-right **−**, **+**, and **Fit** work without first taking
    the canvas in hand. The graph uses the background surface, with no inner
    card border; the inspector uses the panel surface.
  - **The panel** (`screens/brain/BrainGraphPanel.tsx`) says what the node
    under the pointer is, else the opened one, else the whole graph; it
    holds a hovered node 450ms after the pointer leaves it, so crossing the
    gap between two dots does not flash the overview. Every figure is a
    count of the graph read on screen (`indexGraph`, `lib/brainview.ts`).
    - **Nothing in focus**: four figures — memories, topics, projects,
      links — and, when the read holds fewer memories than are kept, *The
      newest N of M memories are drawn*; **Projects**, each with its
      colour, its count and a hairline bar against the biggest, a click
      picking it out (again, every project); **Newest**, the five newest
      memories, a click opening one; and **Reading it**, the three sizes of
      dot and one line each on colour and on how to explore.
    - **A project or a topic**: its kind and colour (a topic says which
      project most of its memories are in), its name, *47 memories · from
      6 sessions · newest 11s ago*, an accent *Open*, **Latest** (its six
      newest memories, a click opening one), a project's **Topics** (with
      how many of its memories each holds, a click opening the topic) and
      **From sessions** (the sessions its memories were taken from, newest
      first, each a link to the session at its last memory there, with how
      many it gave). *Open* closes the graph and shows Recent memories on
      the screen filtered to it: a project as the list's project, a topic
      as its search.
    - **A memory under the pointer**: a peek — its kind, its project in
      colour, its title, when it was kept and a link to its session — and
      *Click the dot to open it*. A memory opened is the full card (§ *A
      memory opened*) in the panel's place, on a phone too.
  The header carries, once there is something, *Add a note* (⇧⌘H in its
  tooltip) and one gear, *Settings* (`BrainSettings.tsx`): a popover with
  *Build automatically* (on/off), *Model* (Haiku | Sonnet) and *Daily
  limit* (100k | 300k | 1M) — the same `PUT /v1/brain/settings` as
  Settings → Second Brain — and under them **Where it's kept**
  (`components/BrainWhere.tsx`, owner, 2026-10-15: show where the data
  lives): the database file as the daemon resolved it (`db_path` on `GET
  /v1/brain/status`, `caprock.db` in its data directory — never a per-OS
  guess in the page), with *Copy path*; the tables, *brain_nodes ·
  brain_edges · brain_runs*; and what leaves the machine, *The session text
  goes to Anthropic through your own Claude to be read; nothing goes to
  Caprock.* There is no *Show in folder*: nothing in Caprock reveals a file
  in Finder or Explorer, and this block adds no OS dialog for it. Escape or
  a click outside closes it.
- **The graph** (`components/BrainGraphCanvas.tsx`, layout in
  `lib/forcegraph.ts`) fills the dialog it opens in (§ *The graph opened*)
  and looks like a knowledge graph (the owner, 2026-10-15, pointing at
  Obsidian's graph view: "exactly this"):
  - **Clusters of small dots.** No anchors and no ring of projects: the
    many-body push (which falls off as 1/d) against a gentle pull to the
    middle lets the real links shape neighbourhoods, with space between
    disconnected islands. The view fits both axes of the canvas with room
    for labels, never past 3× its world size. Every memory, topic and project
    of the selection is in it, and only the real links are drawn. A memory's tie to its project is
    loose and long, its tie to a topic short, so topics and projects read
    as little bursts of their memories; while a first look settles, each
    project is held on the centroid of its memories (a hub that busy, held
    only by springs, is pushed to the edge of its own burst).
  - **Restrained.** A memory is a tiny dot (2px across at the fitted zoom)
    at 85%; a topic a little bigger; a project bigger and full strength —
    the panel's *Reading it* draws the three the same way. Every dot is in
    its project's colour (`tint`; `PROJECT_COLORS`, `lib/brainview.ts`:
    seven hues mixed with the muted ink like the memory kinds, so each
    palette draws its own, the biggest project first; past seven, and with
    no project, the text colour at 72%). The accent is never a project's
    colour: it stays the one mark of the node hovered or opened. A memory's
    project is its source's, else the project it is part of; a topic's is
    the one most of its memories are in. Edges are hairlines (0.75px) at
    19% by default, adjustable in Display.
  - **Labels come with zoom.** In the opened graph the projects and the six
    busiest topics are named at any zoom (`restLabels`), faded with what a
    search or a project did not pick out; the card names nothing. Past 1.5×
    the fitted zoom the other hubs' names fade in, the busiest first and more as one zooms; past 3.2×
    every memory on screen is named. Names sit under their dot and one that
    would overlap a name already placed is left out. The hovered node and
    its neighbours are named at any zoom (its neighbouring memories only
    when there are twelve or fewer).
  - **Focus.** Hovering a node brightens it and its neighbours with a soft
    glow (two faint halos, not a canvas blur), draws its links brighter and
    fades the rest to a fifth, over 180ms; moving off a dot lingers 140ms,
    so going from dot to dot does not fade the whole graph out and in. An
    opened node keeps the focus with less dimmed (to 42%) and a ring. A
    graph of more than 3,000 edges lights only an opened node.
  - **Motion.** A first look settles off screen in slices of 12ms a frame
    (at most 1.2s, after which a graph of thousands finishes on screen) and
    fades in already in shape; then nothing runs until something changes.
    Another selection (a search, a project, a span that keeps less than
    80% of the nodes) is laid out afresh as its own disc and fitted; a
    re-read that only grew keeps its places. Under prefers-reduced-motion
    nothing fades, glides or drops. A tint or a highlight changing redraws
    the picture only: it is read from a ref, so no layout, no new effect.
  - **A thought landing.** A memory added while the graph is drawn (the
    card or the opened graph) flies out of its project's hub to its place
    as an accent spark with a short tail along a slight curve, 900ms, and a
    faint ring spreads where it lands; one with no project drops in from
    above. New nodes are seeded beside a placed neighbour, so it lands in
    its project's cluster. Off with *Animate thoughts arriving* (§ *Thoughts
    arriving*) and under prefers-reduced-motion.
  - **Click to interact.** At rest the graph is part of the page: the
    wheel, a trackpad and a touch swipe scroll the page past it and zoom
    nothing (the owner, 2026-10-16, translated: "when I scroll the page,
    the graph zooms instead"). Hovering shows *Click to zoom and move* in
    the bottom left corner. A click (a press without a drag), or Enter or Space
    on the focused graph, takes it in hand and an accent ring says so: then
    drag the background to pan, a dot to move it (its neighbours follow on
    their links), scroll or pinch to zoom, and from the keyboard the arrows
    pan, + and - zoom and 0 fits. Escape (which then closes nothing else),
    a click outside, the mouse leaving the graph or the focus moving away
    lets it go. A click on a dot opens it whether or not the graph was in
    hand. *Fit* (bottom right) or a double-click glides back to the fitted
    view in either state. The sidebar's small graph never zoomed and is
    unchanged.
  Measured 2026-10-15 on a preview stand (305 memories, 43 topics, 5
  projects, 764 links), production build, in a software-rendered headless
  shell: the main thread was busy 0.02% over 20 seconds with the graph
  settled, 0.13% with the pointer resting on a project, and 7.1% while the
  pointer swept across the graph for ten seconds (the old picture: 1.0%,
  with no fade). At rest the picture is drawn straight onto the screen's
  canvas; the cached layer is kept for pans, zooms and drop-ins only
  (copying the whole layer each frame was most of a hover's cost).
- **The list** (Recent memories) shows each memory's kind (a
  coloured dot and the word; the word from 640px), title, topics, project
  (from 640px) and age, pinned first; in a search, best match first, with
  the search's words (and the close words that matched them) marked in the
  title and topics wherever they occur in any case or accent
  (`lib/brainsearch.ts`, folding as the daemon does).
- **A memory opened** (in the side column, or beside the graph in its
  dialog; `#/brain?node=<id>` opens one): title
  and summary, *Edit* (title, summary, kind), the project, *From* — a link to
  the session at the moment it was taken — and when and how it was kept; what
  it is linked to, each with the edge's word (*part of*, *related to*,
  *follows*, *contradicts*) and an × to unlink; *Link to a topic…*; *Pin* and
  *Delete* (asks once). The screen and the panel re-read on every `brain`
  frame.
- **Settings → Second Brain** (after *Memory between sessions*; its icon is
  a small graph; the same for everyone). *Animate thoughts arriving* (on;
  kept per browser, `caprock.brain-arrivals`; § *Thoughts arriving*).
  *Build it from finished sessions* with the same disclosure line,
  shown whether or not *Show explanations* is on (`data-keep`), because it
  is the switch's condition rather than help; a warning when `claude` was
  not found; *Daily limit* 100k, 300k, 1M; the tokens used in the last 24
  hours against the limit, how many sessions are waiting and when it last
  looked; the last problem, when there is one. *Build from my last 30 days*
  (disabled while off) first shows the estimate — sessions, tokens, days at
  the limit, *An estimate from the transcripts' length, before anything is
  sent* — then *Start* or *Cancel*. *Recent reads* (folded) lists the last
  reads: project, age, memories kept and tokens, or why it failed.
- **The sidebar's Second Brain card** (`components/BrainSidebar.tsx`, in
  the app's sidebar under the list and above the footer, never inside the
  list's scroll: with a long list of sessions its status line was cut off
  by the footer, owner, 2026-10-15). The list is sized by its content and
  scrolls; when the two do not fit, the card gives way first (`shrink-[20]`
  against the list's 1), and inside it only the orb, from 120px to 64px,
  down to a floor that holds the heading, the orb and the whole status
  line (136px; the banner's 140px, the banner scrolling inside it). Under
  640px of window height the orb is not drawn and the floor is the heading
  and the line (64px). Folded it is one row and never shrinks. The whole card — header and body —
  is one way into `#/brain` (the owner, 2026-10-15: "the whole block as an
  interactive element"): a click anywhere, Enter or Space opens the
  screen; it takes focus (a ring) and lights up on hover. The chevron and
  *How it works* keep their own jobs and stop the click there. The
  chevron folds and unfolds it, remembered per browser
  (`caprock.app.brain-fold`). **It starts folded** (owner, 2026-10-15,
  translated: "compact but interactive, not grabbing attention"); a fold a
  viewer saved is kept, so only the default changed. On hover the card
  lifts a pixel (a faint shadow; none under reduced motion).
  - **Folded**: one quiet row in muted colours — a small glyph and the
    mode in words: *Second Brain · Up to date · 140 memories*, *Second
    Brain · Paused · 12 memories*, *Second Brain · Not started*; while it
    builds, *Building · 12 of 41* (the session being read of all in reach;
    the session's title on hover) over a 2px bar along the row's foot.
    The glyph is the only thing that moves, a slow pulse (2.8 s) toward the
    accent, and only while a read actually runs (`reading` or `running`,
    not just sessions queued); no dot, no sparks. `brainMode`,
    `lib/brain.ts`.
  - **Unfolded, off and empty**: the banner — `BrainHero`, *Free*, *One
    memory for all your agents*, the line naming the agents, *Start
    building* and *How it works*. *Start building* sends nothing: it opens
    the screen, where the one-click start sits with its line and Details
    (the consent once opened in this narrow column was unreadable, the
    owner, 2026-10-15).
  - **Unfolded otherwise**: the living brain, the calm one (`BrainOrb`
    with `calm`, 120px tall at most: half the turn, 60% of the contrast,
    half the sparks of the screen's), a still dot, and
    one quiet line under it: while it works *Reading acme-api… · 12 of 41*
    (*Linking* while a read's memories are stored) or *3 sessions to read…
    · 24 of 25* over a thin bar with a light running along it; up to date
    *Up to date · last built 14:02*; otherwise the mode and the count. Its sparks follow the extraction and, beside it, the
    sessions working now (`working`: the Today strip's count from the
    sidebar's own model — nothing more is fetched): one slow spark (2.6 s
    across, fainter, lighting no point, since no memory landed) every 4 s
    per working session, at most six sessions counted; at 10 frames a
    second while only those fly, 30 while the extraction's do, 6 while it
    only turns. No session working and nothing extracting: the turn alone.
- **Thoughts arriving** (`components/BrainArrivals.tsx`, mounted by the
  sidebar's card; the owner, 2026-10-17, translated: "an animation when
  thoughts from an open tab go in — so you can see how they get in"). A
  `brain` frame with op `added` carries the memory's `session` and
  `project` (03-contracts.md § Second Brain, Live). A spark — a 10px accent
  dot with a glow and three fainter tail dots — flies from that session's
  place on screen into the card along a slight curve (950ms): its sidebar
  row (`data-session-id` on the row), else its tab in the strip, else its
  project's sidebar row (`data-project-name`), whichever is drawn and in
  the window. It lands on the folded row's glyph, or the unfolded card's
  orb, and the card pulses: a ring spreads from it (620ms) and the glyph
  swells once in the accent. With no place to fly from, the card only
  pulses. A burst is coalesced: one flight every 260ms, at most three in
  the air (more only pulse), at most four waiting (more are dropped). Cost:
  one fixed element per dot moved by the Web Animations API on transform
  and opacity, removed when it lands; no React state, no re-render, two
  boxes read at take-off. Nothing flies under prefers-reduced-motion, in a
  hidden window, or with Settings → Second Brain, *Animate thoughts
  arriving* off.
- **How it works** (the banner's link) opens `SecondBrainModal`, big and
  short (the owner, 2026-10-15, translated: "big, short, clear"), all of it
  in view without scrolling at 800px tall: the agents you use feeding the
  brain, large (`BrainHero`, below); a *Free* pill above the title (Free
  said plainly, the owner, 2026-10-15); the headline *One memory for all
  your agents*; a line naming the agents drawn (*Claude Code, Codex and
  Gemini CLI remember together.*, or *Claude Code remembers across
  sessions.* for one); three steps of one line each, each with an icon —
  *Sessions end* → *Your Claude reads them, on this computer* → *The next
  agent starts smarter*; the long-term promise in one accent line, *Over
  time: your personal Jarvis* (the owner's idea, and the one place the word
  appears); *Free — runs on your own Claude, on this computer.*; and *Open
  Second Brain* (*Got it* where there is nowhere to open). The numbers sit
  in *Details*, folded, three short lines in the text colour (the owner,
  2026-10-15: four long grey bullets "don't read at all"): *Reads a session
  when it ends or goes quiet 10 min*; *Haiku, up to 300k tokens a day —
  change in Settings*; *Up to 5 memories go into your next session there*;
  then *Where it's kept* (`BrainWhere`, as in the screen's Settings, asking
  `GET /v1/brain/status` once for the path).
  The ×, Escape and the backdrop close it; there is no second Close. No
  price, nothing sent.
- **`BrainOrb`** (`components/BrainOrb.tsx`) is the living brain, the
  sidebar card's and the *Building* panel's one picture (the owner,
  2026-10-15, translated: "as if thoughts are being pulled out of each
  session, neat little things, and it rotates slowly, and you can see
  something is going on"). Points on a Fibonacci sphere, a little jittered,
  linked to their two nearest, turned slowly about the Y axis and tilted,
  scaled by perspective with their alpha by depth — a few lines of
  arithmetic on one canvas, no library. It speaks the Graph view's language:
  tiny dots in the text colour, hairline links, sparks and the landing glow
  in the one accent; one point per memory between 56 and 150. The
  agents this user runs sit at the rim as the app's characters. While the
  extraction works, sparks fly from them into the cloud, more while a
  session is being read; each memory added (a `brain` frame) sends one and
  lights the point it lands on. Idle, it only turns. Cost: a timer waits
  out the gap and one animation frame draws — 30 a second while sparks fly,
  6 while it only turns — each frame a handful of batched paths (a depth
  band and colour at a time); nothing runs while it is out of view
  (IntersectionObserver) or the window hidden, and under
  prefers-reduced-motion it is one still frame. Measured 2026-10-15 in a
  software-rendered headless shell over 20 seconds on the app's Home: the
  main thread was busy 0.46% with the card folded and 1.08% with it
  unfolded and turning.
- **`BrainHero`** (`components/BrainAgents.tsx`, styles in `BrainMark.css`)
  is the hero of the dialog and the empty banner: "all your agents feed one
  memory, and it is yours" in one picture. The agents this user actually
  runs, drawn as the app's own characters (`Characters.tsx`: Claude wears
  the face picked for new sessions — the hard hat, or a duck — Codex's
  terminal character, Gemini's spark, OpenCode's braces, DeepSeek's whale), sit
  on discs either side of `BrainMark`, each joined to it by a dotted line
  along which a pulse in a memory kind's colour travels into the brain and
  fades there, a beat apart. Who is drawn comes from usage: the agent
  split of the last 30 days that `GET /v1/glance?range=30d` already answers
  (the daemon caches it for a minute; no new endpoint), main threads and
  subagents added together, most sessions first, then most turns, up to
  five; only agents with a character are drawn (Grok and custom agents are
  not), so the line naming them always matches the picture. Before
  anything is known — loading, a failed read, no sessions yet — it is
  Claude Code alone. The pulse is one short dash of a path normalised to
  length 1, its offset animated: CSS only; under prefers-reduced-motion
  each pulse holds still halfway along its line. The *Free* pill
  (`FreePill`, `.brain-free`) is the accent at an eighth of its strength
  with a hairline of it and the label pulled towards the text — the quiet
  register of *Get premium*, never a solid block.
- **`BrainMark`** (`components/BrainMark.tsx`, `BrainMark.css`) is Second
  Brain's picture, at the middle of `BrainHero` in the dialog (large) and
  the empty banner (medium): nodes and links laid out in the side view of a brain — the
  cerebrum with a scalloped edge for its folds, the cerebellum with its
  folds under the back, the brain stem — in the four memory kinds' colours
  (`--brain-idea`, `-decision`, `-finding`, `-question`), on a faint accent
  tint of the silhouette, so every palette draws its own. It is alive with
  CSS alone, no script after the first render: six nodes pulse a beat
  apart, and every ten seconds a new node drops in, its links draw to its
  neighbours and a ring spreads where it lands (two such nodes, half a
  cycle apart). Under prefers-reduced-motion it holds still, the new nodes
  already in place; like every animation it pauses while the window is
  hidden.
- **Colours.** Each kind has a token (`--brain-idea` … `--brain-project`,
  design/tokens.css), mixed with the muted ink like the agent marks so it
  follows every theme.

### Obsidian vault import

Second Brain's header offers *Import from Obsidian…* to the machine owner,
including before automatic extraction is enabled. `ObsidianVaults.tsx` uses
the existing sheet: a full folder path, *Choose folder…* in the native app,
*Import vault*, and *Let agents use these notes*, unchecked. The explanation
beside it names handoff, MCP and model topic lists, and says answers can reach
the agent's model. Importing does not turn on automatic extraction.

Saved vaults show their actual name, folder, note count, last check and error,
a per-vault sharing checkbox, *Refresh now* and *Remove…*. Removal asks inline
*Remove this import? Your files stay.*, with *Remove import* and *Keep*.
Operations disable action controls while running; errors keep the path to fix.
The sheet closes through the shared ×, Escape, backdrop and *Done* paths.

A notes-only brain shows its graph and list even with extraction off; its
sidebar says Obsidian and the note count rather than showing the empty start
banner. The extraction strip remains separate from automatic vault refresh. Session
memory and imported-note counts stay distinct; the list and graph caption say
*memories and notes* when both kinds may be present. Imported note nodes are
diamonds, with a legend in the opened graph. List rows, graph peeks and node
detail mark *Obsidian* and the source vault; detail shows source files and
separate excerpts. Imported notes are edited in Obsidian; pinning and linking
remain available, while removal of an import lives in the vault sheet.

The API, limits and permission boundary are owned by
[03-contracts.md § Obsidian vaults](03-contracts.md#obsidian-vaults);
[ADR-079](08-decisions.md) records why.

## Offers, and when they are allowed to speak

Several surfaces in the dashboard point at something that costs money — the
team tier, the paid features, and the share card that carries the product's name
off the machine. Each opens with a fact from the reader's own screen, quotes no
price, and either appears only on a condition or can be put away for a month.

**Caprock for Teams is explained in place.** Every team surface — the Cost
banner, the Projects line and the footer — opens the same dialog
(`TeamsModal`) rather than the website. Owner request, 2026-10-03: the footer
link alone sent people off the dashboard to learn what the offer even was.

**Both upsell dialogs show before they tell** (owner, 2026-10-04: the text-wall
versions were "bad"). They share one frame, `UpsellDialog`: a picture of what
you get first, drawn in the Week card's language (its ground, characters and
big figures), then a few lines with icons, then the two ways forward. Every
figure in a picture is an example and the picture carries an **Example** tag,
in its visible text and its `aria-label` (rule 6); tests assert both.

- **Teams** pictures an example team's week — laptops, PRs, ≈cost per PR, the
  share re-reading context, an agent strip and a repository strip — then this
  machine's own spend when the caller has it, four benefits (who is eating the
  budget, loops on every machine, every agent in one total, what the money
  bought), one trust line exactly as the team page states it (the team's own
  infrastructure, counters only, prompts and code never leave a laptop) and one
  on the price's shape (one flat price, never per seat — no figure; the
  pricing page owns it). **Book a demo** is the amber primary, **See the team
  page** the outline secondary.
- **Premium** (`PremiumModal`, one component for every paid feature — the
  header chip, the hint, the banner and each locked panel open it) pictures the
  feature working: the daily cap stopping at its line, the plan window at 90%
  with Caprock's sessions paused until the reset, a question asked of
  Gemini about your own sessions with its cost beside the Claude spend, the
  Monday message from your own bot, a tool opened into its commands with
  output and failure rate per group (example figures, labelled so). Then three lines with icons and the setup
  the feature demands, named before payment.
- **The whole plan, in one row** (owner, 2026-10-16: readers took the plan for
  the one feature they had clicked, translated). The eyebrow reads *Premium ·
  N features · $30 a year*: the count from `PremiumModal`'s `FEATURES`, never
  typed, and the price from `GET /v1/premium`. Under the clicked feature, one
  row of chips, *Also in Premium:* and every other feature as an icon and a
  short name (`short`). A chip swaps the picture, lines and setup in place, and
  the buy and Read more links follow the feature on screen; pressing the
  pressed chip goes back to the clicked one. Arrow keys, Home and End move
  between chips, one chip is in the Tab order, and the dialog never opens with
  focus on one (`data-dialog-later` in `UpsellDialog`). The chips are 13 px type with
  13 px icons (12 px type below `sm`), three lines at 520 px: at 10.5 px the
  owner did not see the row at first ("make it a little bigger, I didn't even
  see it at first, and it's VERY well done", 2026-10-16, translated). **New** marks a feature for 30 days after its `since`: the day the
  commit adding it to `PaidFeature` landed, from git. Nothing unbuilt is listed
  (rules 6 and 11).
- **The header's Get premium** (`PremiumChip`, *Get premium $30/yr*, on
  every screen until a licence is active) is framed like Feedback and
  Settings beside it — 24 px, 6 px radius, 12 px medium — in a quiet tint
  (`.premium-cta` in `tokens.css`): the premium colour at 12% behind, a 45%
  hairline around, 20% and 70% on hover and keyboard focus. The label is the
  premium ink pulled 40% towards the palette's text, because
  `--color-premium-strong` alone is 4.1:1 on that tint in Dark Warm and Dark
  Cold; the price is the label at 85%. Measured in every palette: label
  5.8:1 or more, price 4.6:1 or more, at rest and on hover. It was a solid
  violet block with white text until 2026-10-15, the loudest thing in the
  header (owner: "too bright", translated).
- **Prices exactly as caprock.dev sells them:** $30 a year and $100 once, with
  **Best value** on the lifetime as the site marks it; the monthly price is not
  linked from the site and is not offered here. Figures come from
  `GET /v1/premium`. The buy buttons are the product's amber — filled for the
  recommended one, outline for the other — not the premium ultramarine, which
  made them read as an advert. The free-forever line stays at the foot, smaller.
- **Dialog behaviour:** Escape, the backdrop and both close controls close it;
  focus moves into it on open, Tab stays inside, and focus returns to the
  opener on close. All links open a new tab. At 390 px the pictures fit and the
  two price cards stack.

The rule behind all of them: **an offer that is always visible is wallpaper** —
read once, ignored after, and faintly grubby on a tool someone installed for
its own sake. An offer tied to a condition is either useful or absent.

| Surface           | Appears when                                      | Where                         |
| ----------------- | ------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------- |
| Caprock for Teams | 10 or more repositories in the selected range     | Projects panel, Now           |
| Teams card        | 2+ commit authors in 30 days; with spend          | Cost, Lifetime                |
| Premium banner    | measured spend; not dismissed this month          | Now, Lifetime                 |
| The spend cap     | today has cost ≥ 1.5× the 30-day median day       | All-time line, Now            |
| Daily-cap nudge   | today in the top quarter of 8+ earlier spend days | Cost                          |
| Plan-limit nudge  | a Claude plan window ≥ 80%                        | Plan limits, app Today strip  |
| Plan-window stop  | a plan window ≥ 90%, no licence                   | Now (plan-limit row)          |
| Share toast       | Monday, or a milestone; once a week               | Bottom right, dashboard + app |
| Star strip        | 3+ days with sessions and 10+ sessions            | Bottom, dashboard + app       |

**The thresholds are judgement, not measurement.** Nobody has data on which
number converts; they were picked to be defensible rather than optimal, and
each is one line to change:

- **Ten repositories.** Below it, "your team" is a guess about someone who may
  well work alone, and guessing at a stranger's situation inside their own
  dashboard is how a tool starts feeling like a funnel. Ten is roughly where a
  single-person explanation stops being the obvious one.
- **1.5× the median day.** Against the **median**, never the mean: one runaway
  afternoon drags a mean upward and would then suppress the hint on exactly the
  days that follow it. Fewer than seven days of spend is not a baseline, so
  nothing is shown until there is one.
- **Within a tenth of a round figure.** People post milestones, not arbitrary
  Tuesdays. Crossing $10,000 is worth a mention for a while; by $18,000 the
  moment has gone and a button still announcing it is noise.

The share card carries no project names and no session identifiers — a share
button that quietly publishes which repositories someone works on is a trap —
and it carries the same caveat every cost figure does, because a dollar amount
posted without it reads as a bill somebody paid.

Rules are unit-tested in `Projects.test.tsx`, `Lifetime.test.tsx` and
`ShareCard.test.tsx`, including the absences: the team line is pinned to stay
away at six repositories, and the cap hint to stay away on an ordinary day.

### Product-led surfaces

**One banner system** (`ui/src/components/Banner.tsx`, owner 2026-10-11:
"banners must be gorgeous, with a picture — small but perfect; use your own
designs", translated). Every offer and notice — the team card, the Premium
banner, the cap and plan-limit nudges, the setup and star strips, the update
offer and notice, the hooks notice, the plan question under Plan limits and
the foot of each locked panel — is the same shape:

- **A small scene on the left**, drawn from the product's own cast
  (`Characters.tsx`) and nothing else: a team of agents for Teams, an agent
  paused under a line for the cap, an agent holding a phone for the setup
  strip, a padlock for a locked panel, a plug for hooks. Inline SVG, no
  external image, decorative (`aria-hidden`).
- **A title in a confident weight, one short line, one primary button and a
  quiet dismiss** ("Not now" or ×). One idea per banner; the reader's own
  figure is set in the banner's tone and kept short (`fmtUSDShort`: $135,
  $1.4k, $16.8k), with the exact sum on hover. Every figure is measured.
- **One row on a desktop, about 76 px tall**; under 640 px of its own width
  (a container query) the actions move under the words. The strips at the
  page's bottom edge and a locked panel's foot use the edge form: no card, a
  top rule, 60 px.
- **Colour is theme tokens only**, so it holds in every palette. Premium
  surfaces are ultramarine (`--color-premium`); everything else uses the
  accent, a notice its state colour (warn for missing hooks, ok once
  installed).

The banners keep their own logic: when they show, their dismissal keys and
the one offer slot below are unchanged.

Caprock is free and run product-led: it has to sell Premium and Caprock for
Teams from inside the product, and it asks one favour, a GitHub star (owner,
2026-10-09: "it must sell the Pro version and the Team version, with the right
banners"; translated). The rules that keep that from turning a tool into a
funnel live in `ui/src/lib/nudges.ts`, each a pure function with a test in
`nudges.test.tsx`:

- **One offer on screen at a time, anywhere.** Every offer above — the team
  card and the Projects team line, the Premium banner, hint, cap hint and both
  nudges, the share toast and the star strip — asks `useNudgeSlot` for the one
  slot while its own rule holds, and only the highest-priority instance
  renders: plan-limit nudge, Premium hint, cap nudges, team offers, share
  toast, setup strip, Mail strip, offers, Premium banner, star strip. Answering one lets the next in.
- **Never to a payer.** Every Premium offer checks `useLicensed`
  (`GET /v1/premium`'s licence) and stays away when one is active.
- **The reader's own figure first, no saving promised (rule 6).** The
  plan-limit nudge says which window is at what share ("Claude's weekly window
  is at 84%") and offers **Pause before the limit** (Premium), which
  opens `PremiumModal` on the plan-window stop. The daily-cap nudge appears
  only when today is in the top quarter of at least eight earlier days with
  spend on this machine, states today's spend ("$221 today, one of your
  busiest days"), and offers **Set a daily cap** (Premium) (`PremiumModal` on the cap). No new gate: both open the dialog the
  existing gated features already use.
- **The team card needs a second person.** `GET /v1/team-signal` counts
  distinct commit authors across the listed repositories in 30 days (git on
  this machine, counts only); at two or more, Cost and Lifetime show "Your
  team runs agents too", the people count and this machine's spend, and
  **Caprock for Teams →** opens `https://caprock.dev/teams/?ref=app` in the
  browser. `ref=app` is the only attribution anywhere (rule 4: no
  telemetry). Rule 11: the copy presents the team tier as the product.
- **The setup strip** (`SetupStrip.tsx`, owner 2026-10-10) is the same slim
  row: *Take Caprock with you*, its *0 of 2*, and two buttons (owner,
  2026-10-15: five on one strip were too many). **Set up** opens a small
  list above it — *Phone* and *Telegram*, each with its tick or its one-line
  purpose, its own action (*Open on your phone*, *Connect Telegram*, each
  opening Settings at its section) and a small *Not needed* — and **Later**
  puts the strip away for three days (`setup-later`). Each item ticks itself
  from the daemon — a paired phone in `GET /v1/pair/state`, a bot with a
  paired chat in `GET /v1/telegram` — polled every 30 s, so the status is
  tracked, not claimed. *Not needed* retires one item for good
  (`setup-phone-skip`, `setup-telegram-skip`). **× folds, it does not
  dismiss:** the strip becomes a *0 of 2* chip in the app's status strip
  (the dashboard's footer in a browser), and a click on the chip unfolds it
  (`setup-collapsed` and `setup-expanded`, the later mark wins). Folded, it
  keeps the one nudge slot, so folding never brings another banner up in
  its place. With every item connected or skipped it never appears again,
  folded or not. Never on a paired device. It sells nothing, so it ranks
  above the Premium banner and the star.
- **The star strip** is the banner's edge form above the dashboard's footer and above the
  app's status strip, never over content: "Enjoying Caprock? A star on GitHub
  helps others find it." with **★ Star on GitHub** (the browser, through the
  app's `open_external`), **I starred it** (shows "Thank you!" for three
  seconds, then never again) and **×** (a month). Only after real use — three
  days with sessions and ten sessions — so never on a first run or during
  setup. The footer's star link disappears once "I starred it" is said.
- **The offers** (`OfferStrip.tsx`, `lib/offer.ts`,
  [ADR-062](08-decisions.md)): three demand surveys, the
  banner's edge form in the flow above the footer and the app's status
  strip, so never over a terminal or a permission prompt. The copy is the
  owner's, word for word (2026-10-14). The Second Brain pre-order sale that
  led them is gone: Second Brain was built and made free (ADR-063). Tried
  in this order:
  - **Hugging Face models, a survey.** *Would you run Hugging Face models on
    this computer from Caprock?* — what would you run first (four picks),
    what is this computer (five). Every install, at most twice on different
    days.
  - **Ads or YouTube, a survey.** *Would you watch short ads in Caprock to
    earn money?* or *Would you watch YouTube in Caprock while agents work?*
    — hours a day (Under 4, 4–8, 8–12, 12+) and how much of that is in
    Caprock (Under 30%, 30–70%, Over 70%). Each install draws one at random,
    kept in `settings.prompts` when its card first shows, and is asked it
    once.

  A survey card is a headline, one line, two one-click questions, the email
  field *Email, if you want to hear when it's ready (optional)*, **Send**,
  **Not interested** and ×, and under them *Sends these answers, and your
  email if you add one, to caprock.dev. Nothing else.* After Send: *Thanks —
  that helps us decide.* **Send** is the only thing that sends (`POST
  /v1/offer`). **Not interested** and × send nothing, and every answer is
  final. Shared rules: after two days with sessions, one offer a day at most
  across all of them, once the keyboard has been still for 20 s (latched, so
  it does not vanish when typing resumes), never while a session waits on a
  permission prompt, never on a paired phone, and only when it holds the one
  offer slot. For QA, `?offer=<id>` (`local-models`, `ads`,
  `youtube`) in the page's address shows that card now and records nothing;
  a survey's Send still posts, so set `CAPROCK_OFFER_URL` to a stub first.
- **The share toast** replaces the old inline nudge: when a week closes
  (Monday) or a milestone passes — 100, 500, 1,000… sessions, a billion
  tokens, a week of 95% cache hits, or a money milestone — "Your week with
  Claude Code: N sessions, $X at API price — share the card?", labelled *at
  API list price · not a bill*. Once a week, whichever way it is answered.
- **Snoozes:** Premium nudges 14 days, the team card and star × 30 days,
  the share toast 7 days, "I starred it" and the offers forever. Answers are kept on the
  daemon (`settings.prompts`, [03-contracts.md](03-contracts.md)) and
  mirrored in the browser, so the app and a browser tab agree; a paired
  phone keeps its own.

**The share sheet** (`ShareDialog`, from the header's Share pill — amber
tint, not a fill, so it is found without outshouting the figures — the
All-time panel, the toast and the app's ⌘K *Share my numbers*; the app's
Today strip carried a tinted *Share* corner until 2026-10-16) is 760px wide and shows the card
itself, large, before any button. Above it, one row of small segmented
controls: Today, This week, This month or All time; Figures or Story; Dark
or Paper; and Landscape or Portrait for Story. Under it one filled primary
action — **Share…** (the OS share sheet) where the browser has one, **Copy
image** where it does not — and the other ways out, quiet, beside it:
**Copy image**, **Save image** and **Post to X**. X cannot be handed an image by URL, so
Post to X copies the card (or saves it when the clipboard refuses) and
opens a post with words and `caprock.dev` — no figures in the text, since a
number typed into a tweet travels without its caveat; the note says where
the image is.

**One set of ways out for every card** (`components/ShareActions.tsx`;
owner, 2026-10-15, translated: "I want the images locally; an option to
share to a social network, with text; send to Telegram, to myself or to
someone else"). The dashboard's sheet and the Pipeline's draw the same
block under their card:

- **Share…, Copy image, Save image** as above. Copy goes through the app's
  own clipboard command in the desktop app (`copyImage`); Save is a
  download, which the app turns into its save dialog.
- **Post** — the words in an editable box, then **Post to X** and **Post to
  LinkedIn**: each copies the card (else saves it) and opens the site with
  the words written (X's `intent/post`, LinkedIn's `feed/?shareActive`);
  the line beside them says to attach the card from the clipboard.
- **Telegram** — a row of its own right under Share… / Copy / Save, always
  shown, on both sheets (*Share your figures* and the Pipeline's). The
  owner's "I can't share to Telegram" (2026-10-15) was *Share your
  figures* in a release from before this block existed: Share…, Copy,
  Save, Post to X, and the macOS share sheet, which lists Telegram only
  when Telegram's share extension is installed and on. The block's first
  version then put *To me* and *To someone else* under a small caps label
  beside the post box, below the fold of the app's window, and without a
  bot *To me* was a text link.
  - **Send to my Telegram** sends the card, with the words as its caption,
    through the person's own bot to their own paired chat
    (`POST /v1/telegram/photo`, [03-contracts.md](03-contracts.md) §
    Telegram bridge), on that click only; the line beside it names the
    chat. With no bot or no paired chat, or no answer from the bridge, it
    is **Set up Telegram**, to `#/settings/telegram`.
  - **Send to someone in Telegram** copies the card and opens Telegram's
    chat picker with the words and caprock.dev: in the desktop app the
    `tg://msg_url` link when an app here handles `tg:` (the shell checks
    and refuses otherwise, `open_external`), else `t.me/share/url`. Neither
    link can carry an image, so the note says *Image copied — paste it in
    the chat*.
  - Settings → Telegram says a share card goes to the chat only on Send to
    my Telegram.

**The Pipeline card** (`components/PipelineShare.tsx`,
`components/PipelineShareCard.ts`). A tinted *Share* pill in the Pipeline
page's head, once the Flow figures are read, opens a sheet with a
1200×630 card drawn at 2x (a 2400×1260 PNG): the brand, *PIPELINE · LAST
14 DAYS* and the dates, the repository line, the eight Flow figures as
tiles — value, caption, and each one's 14-day line with today a dot —
*Agent cost* captioned *at API list price — not a bill*, and caprock.dev.
The figures are the page's own (`flowFigures`), never recomputed. Dark or
Light, starting on the screen's theme. The post's first draft is the
repository as the card names it, the fortnight's throughput, CI median and
opened → merged that have a value, then "— via caprock.dev".

**Anonymous by default** (owner, 2026-10-15: anonymisation is a must). A
repository's name can be private, and a picture travels further than its
poster meant. So:

- The card, the post's words, the Telegram caption and the file name say
  **"a GitHub repo"** in place of the name. "A GitHub repo", not "a private
  repo": Caprock does not know a repository's visibility.
- They carry **only aggregates**: the figures, their lines, the window and
  caprock.dev. Never a pull request title or number, a branch, an author or
  avatar, an organisation, a release name, a path or a session title — the
  card is built from `board.flow` and the name alone, so nothing else is
  even read.
- **Show repo name** is a checkbox above the buttons, beside a line that
  says what the card names. It is unticked every time the sheet opens and
  never remembered, so a private name cannot leak by habit; ticked, the
  name replaces "a GitHub repo" on the card and in the words, and nothing
  else is added. The test draws a board full of identifying strings and
  checks none reaches the card or the words, and that the tick adds the
  name only.

## Hierarchy

The dashboard is dense on purpose, but density without hierarchy is texture. A review found 86% of its type sitting between 10px and 12px, no `font-semibold` anywhere, and the root at `14px` — which silently rendered every rem-based class 12.5% smaller than the identical class on the marketing site.

- **`Stat` has three sizes.** `hero` (34px) for the one figure a screen is about, `default` (28px), `compact` (22px) for reference figures in dense rows. Values are `font-semibold`: weight separates a number from its neighbours without spending a pixel of density, which is exactly what a dense surface needs. Uniform sizing made a row of six an inventory list with no landing point.
- **A `Stat` figure never overflows its tile** (testers, 2026-10-12: *$12,793.46* in a one-sixth tile ran into *Burn now* and lost its leading digits, its caption cut to *…rice · not a…*). The tile is a size container and a text figure gets `max(12px, min(<step>, 100cqi / (0.6 × characters)))` (`statFit` in `components/ui.tsx`): the figure is mono, so each character is 0.6em and the arithmetic is exact — it keeps its step while it fits and shrinks only as far as the tile needs, on one line. The caption under it wraps instead of ending in an ellipsis. Checked at $13,050.49 and $130,489.86 with seven-figure burn rates, 1100–1512px, in the browser and in the app with the sidebar open.
- **Cost leads, and carries the accent.** It previously sat fourth of six at the same weight as a turn counter, while the two *tinted* cells were cache hit and burn — so colour pointed away from the money. Now the day's cost is the hero, the per-repo costs are accent, and everything else steps down.
- **Panels are objects, not outlines.** `--shadow-panel` plus a recessed header band: the panel-to-page luminance step is 6 of 255, so without a shadow the edge is guesswork.
- **A tone means "act", not "a value exists".** Cache hit was green whenever the rate exceeded 50% — it is ~99% permanently on Claude Code, so the signal was always on, which teaches the eye that green means nothing. It now speaks only below 90%, where it means a prefix broke. Burn lost its permanent tint for the same reason.

What was deliberately *not* done: making everything bigger. Six stats at 34px would still be six equal cells — a bigger flat field, not a hierarchy. The gain comes from ratio and weight, and from stepping reference figures *down* to make room.

## Loading states

Panels show **placeholder rows** while their first fetch is in flight, never an empty state. Screens used to test `list.length === 0` without asking whether the data had arrived, so the first paint of Cost, History, Now, Projects and Answers announced "No history yet" and then replaced it with real numbers — telling a new user the product has nothing for them at the exact moment it is fetching everything. `Skeleton` in `ui/src/components/ui.tsx`; it is `aria-hidden`, since it carries no information, and its pulse respects `prefers-reduced-motion`.

An empty state is now only shown when the answer really is empty, which is what makes it worth reading.

## The dashboard keeps its place

Owner, 2026-10-14, translated: "When I switch between the dashboard and tabs,
I lose the position — where I was, what I was looking at on the dashboard.
Don't do that; I want to continue from the same place. At worst, be able to
quickly go back to the main screen." (`ui/src/lib/screenPlace.ts`.)

- **Kept screens.** The dashboard's own screens — Home, Work, Cost, Lifetime,
  Week, Pipeline, Memory, Tasks (`KEPT_SCREENS`) — stay mounted once opened, hidden
  rather than dropped while another screen or a terminal tab is in front;
  in the app the Dashboard tab's whole view stays mounted behind the
  terminals until its × (`data-dashboard-view`, `hidden`). An opened Live
  pulse row, an expanded CI/CD panel, a fold, a filter: all still there on
  return, with no reload and no flash. A session's page, Settings, Brain (it
  draws a live graph), the graph and Start work mount only while in front: Settings listens for
  Escape on the window, which must never reach it from behind a terminal.
- **A hidden screen does no work.** `ScreenShown` (`lib/refresh.ts`) is false
  under a kept screen out of sight: `useApi` asks nothing and its live tick
  rests (a folded section's rule: asked once when shown again), and
  `useNow` / `useNowFor` stop their clocks and read the time on showing.
- **Scroll, per screen.** `useScrollPlace` records each screen's scroll as
  it scrolls (keyed by screen; other routes by their hash) and puts it back
  in the layout phase, before the first frame, when the screen changes or
  the dashboard is shown again; a screen never opened before starts at its
  top. Hidden (`display: none`) reads 0 and is never recorded.
- **What keeping it costs** (measured 2026-10-14, headless Chromium against
  a preview daemon with 14 sessions): Home mounted adds about 1,200 DOM
  nodes and 2 MB of JS heap; hidden behind the terminals, the page asked
  `/v1/` twice in 10 s, both the workspace's own polls.
- **The quick way home.** ⌘0 from a terminal or Mail brings the dashboard
  back where it was left; with the dashboard in front it goes to Home at its
  top (`goHomeTop`: Home's rows stay as they were, only the scroll resets);
  on Home already at its top it goes back to the terminals, as ⌘0 always
  did. The sidebar's *Dashboard* row and the palette's *Open the dashboard*
  do the same; a click on the Dashboard tab only brings it to the front.
  Clicking *Home* in the dashboard's nav while on Home scrolls it to the
  top. The tab's and the row's tooltips say so, and *All keys* reads
  "Dashboard, where you left it; again: Home, at its top".

## Where am I

Owner, 2026-10-16, translated: "When you go to the dashboard and walk
through its pages, pages that open in a new window should be marked somehow,
because sometimes it's unclear where you went and where you ended up. And
there must be a button to return to the dashboard." (`lib/pagetitle.ts`,
`components/WhereAmI.tsx`, `lib/externalLinks.ts`.)

- **The page names itself.** Off Home the dashboard's header reads
  *caprock / Pipeline* — the wordmark, then the page (`data-page-title`),
  with what the address says it shows: *Pipeline · caprock* for a
  repository, *Settings · Phone*, *Tasks · Queue*. The browser tab's title
  says the same, *Pipeline · caprock*; Home is *caprock*. The app's window is
  not a tab and keeps its title.
- **One item is current.** The nav pill of the page, filled accent; a
  session's page lights Home, the screen it belongs to. Settings is not in
  the nav, so on its page the *Settings* button is the one drawn filled
  (`aria-current="page"`). The graph and Start work light nothing: their
  name in the header says where you are.
- **← Home, on every page but Home.** First in the header, where a back
  button is looked for: a link to `#/` in the same tab, never a new one. At
  phone width it grows to a 36px touch target and the header wraps around
  it. The in-page *← Home* links on a session's page and on Start work went:
  the header's is the one way back, except on a phone's session, whose own
  bar replaces the header there.
- **← Dashboard, in a tab of its own.** In the app the Pipeline and Mail are
  pinned tabs with no dashboard header (§ Release plan, § Mail), so each
  carries a bar on top (`TabPageBar`): *← Dashboard*, which brings the
  Dashboard tab to the front where it was left (as ⌘0 does), and the page's
  name.
- **↗ means it leaves Caprock.** A link that opens a new browser tab — or,
  in the app, the system browser — draws ↗ after its text and its tooltip
  ends with *Opens in a new tab* (browser) or *Opens in your browser* (app).
  An `<a target="_blank">` draws the mark by itself (`design/tokens.css`); a
  button that calls `openExternal` carries `data-ext` (`extProps`), or
  spells the ↗ in its label. A link that stays inside Caprock uses →:
  *Open Pipeline →*, the cockpit's *Pipeline →*, Home's *Go to session →*
  (both were ↗ and read as leaving). A source guard in
  `WhereAmI.test.tsx` fails when a new-tab link or an `openExternal` button
  is added without saying where it opens.

**The audit** (2026-10-16): no dashboard page opened in a new window or tab.
Every route is a hash change in the same tab; in the app, Pipeline and Mail
open their own pinned tab, by the owner's request (2026-10-14, 2026-10-15),
and keep it. One broken link was found and fixed: the version dialog's
*Turn them on in status* pointed at `#/status`, a route that does not exist,
and fell through to Home; it is *Settings* at `#/settings` now.

| Where you click                      | Browser tab    | Desktop app         |
| ------------------------------------ | -------------- | ------------------- |
| Nav: Home, Work, Cost, Lifetime      | in place       | Dashboard tab       |
| Nav: Week, Memory, Second Brain      | in place       | Dashboard tab       |
| Nav: Tasks, Settings                 | in place       | Dashboard tab       |
| Nav: Pipeline                        | in place       | Pipeline tab        |
| Nav: Mail                            | in place       | Mail tab            |
| Share, Feedback, Get premium         | dialog         | dialog              |
| Feedback's Send                      | new tab ↗      | browser ↗           |
| Premium's buy and info links         | new tab ↗      | browser ↗           |
| Version, What's new                  | dialog         | not shown           |
| Changelog, release page              | new tab ↗      | browser ↗           |
| Home: Open Pipeline →                | in place       | Pipeline tab        |
| Home: a session row                  | in place       | its terminal tab    |
| Sidebar: Open Second Brain           | not shown      | Dashboard tab       |
| Work: a row's title                  | in place       | its tab or Pipeline |
| Cockpit: Pipeline →, Log in Pipeline | not shown      | Pipeline tab        |
| Open repo, PR, run, release, checks  | new tab ↗      | browser ↗           |
| githubstatus.com, footer, star       | new tab ↗      | browser ↗           |
| Settings: token, key, docs links     | new tab ↗      | browser ↗           |
| A link in a mail or a message        | new tab ↗      | browser ↗           |
| Open in Terminal                     | Terminal app ↗ | Terminal app ↗      |
| Mail on its own (`?mail=1`)          | its own window | not shown           |
| ← Home                               | Home, this tab | Home, Dashboard tab |
| ← Dashboard (Pipeline, Mail tabs)    | not shown      | Dashboard tab       |

Mail on its own is the Caprock Mail preview's window (`app/mail-mac`): it has
no dashboard to go back to, so it carries no way back.

## A hidden window does no work

The dashboard, the app window and the menu bar popover all follow one rule:
out of sight, the page does nothing anyone would see, and when it comes back
it shows the current state at once. "Out of sight" is the document's
visibility: a minimized or closed window, one covered by others, another tab
in a browser. The live socket stays open throughout, so notifications, the
tray's *N waiting* and the Dock badge keep up.

- **Polls and clocks stop.** `everyWhileVisible` and `useNow`
  (`ui/src/lib/visible.ts`, `ui/src/lib/useNow.ts`) clear their timers while
  hidden, so nothing wakes the page, and run once on showing when a tick came
  due. A poll that must keep the menu bar current says how slowly it may run
  while hidden (`hiddenIntervalMs`).
- **Live frames are held.** While hidden, `/v1/live` frames are kept and
  applied together every 2 s, in one render (`HIDDEN_FLUSH_MS` in
  `ui/src/lib/live.ts`); a notification, a permission prompt, a loop alert
  or the link's own `hello`/`reset` is applied at once, with everything held
  before it. Showing the page applies what was held. Past 2,000 held frames
  the rest becomes a `reset`, and the screens read again.
- **The debounced tick rests.** `useLiveTick` (one shared timer per pace,
  however many readers) sets no timer while hidden — the workspace's live
  list keeps a 5 s pace for the badge — and catches up on showing; a closed
  popover holds it still.
- **Animations pause.** `<html data-page-hidden>` pauses every CSS
  animation (`design/tokens.css`); infinite ones animate only `transform`
  and `opacity`.
- **Terminals draw nothing.** The tab in front holds its output like a hidden
  tab does and writes it when the window is shown; no cursor blinks.
- **Terminal sockets go** (2026-10-19). Hidden for 2 s
  (`PAGE_HIDDEN_SUSPEND_MS`, `suspendWhileHidden` in `ui/src/lib/visible.ts`),
  every terminal lets its socket go (`TermClient.suspend`), so the daemon
  sends a hidden window no output at all; the daemon keeps it, and showing
  the window writes what was held and reconnects from the last byte the
  terminal has (`since`), so nothing is lost or repainted. A blink of
  occlusion keeps the socket.

Visible, a terminal is handed its output at most every 33 ms
(`DESK_WRITE_MS`, `coalescedWriter` in `ui/src/lib/termwrite.ts`; a phone's
every 100 ms): what arrives meanwhile goes in one write, every byte in
order, and the first write after a quiet spell goes at once, so an echo is
not delayed (2026-10-19, measured in
[docs/app-window-footprint-2026-10-19.md](../docs/app-window-footprint-2026-10-19.md)).

Visible, a screen that follows the live feed takes it a batch at a time —
everything that arrived in the last 250 ms in one render
(`ui/src/lib/framebatch.ts`; the Session timeline, Live activity, the pulse,
the cockpit's events) — and a clock ticks every second only while
something it shows is under a minute old (`useNowFor`): an idle Now or
Session screen redraws every 15 s, the cockpit every 30 s when nothing runs
or waits, and the timeline's older rows (memoised) only when their minute
changes.

### A status moves at once

The owner, 2026-10-14 (translated): an agent still reading *waiting for you*
10–20 s after he had set it going "is not great". Two things held it, both
fixed:

- **The debounce.** Every screen read a session's status on the debounced
  tick: the sidebar, tabs, tray and cockpit a second after the frame, Now
  two. A frame that moves a status is now taken at once by every pace of
  2 s or less (`statusStep` and `STATUS_NOW_MAX_MS` in
  `ui/src/lib/live.ts`): a `turn.user`, `permission.prompt`, `agent.spawn`,
  `session.end`, `session.continue` or `context.clear`; the main thread's
  `agent.stop`; the first event after a dialog (its call running, answered
  in a terminal Caprock does not hold); the first main-thread event after a
  Stop; and a `status` frame. Tool calls keep the debounce, and so do the
  all-time figures (10 s).
- **No event to read.** Answering a permission dialog fires no hook — the
  next one is the call's `PostToolUse`, when the call finishes — so a build
  allowed with `1` read *waiting for approval* for as long as it ran. Input
  into a session Caprock started is now read where it is typed (the daemon's
  nudge, [03-contracts.md § Nudges](03-contracts.md#nudges)): an answered
  dialog reads *approved — running …* (working) or, answered No, *waiting
  for you*; Enter at a waiting prompt reads *starting on your message* for
  up to 5 s (`activity.provisional`), until the prompt's own event arrives
  or, when none does (an empty prompt), back to what the events say. The
  composer, the queue, the card's buttons, the phone and Telegram all type
  through the same path, so each moves the status the same way.

Measured on a preview daemon with the fake `claude` and a headless browser
watching the sidebar row's state word (2026-10-14, one machine, under load):
a prompt's hook to *working*, 1,017–1,048 ms before and 5–7 ms after; a
dialog answered by key or by button, its call running 15 s, 16.0 s before
(the call's `PostToolUse`) and 5–6 ms after; Enter at the prompt with no
hook after it, never before, 4 ms after and back to *waiting* at 5.2 s. A
Codex transcript line reached the daemon in 376–2,874 ms on the 5 s poll and
in 187–605 ms with the reads after input.

### The Now screen refreshes once

Each section of Now (the hero tiles, Plan limits, the live pulse, At a
glance, All time, Projects, the all-time strip) read its own figures on
every live tick. Under a steady stream of hook events (five sessions, ten
events a second, a preview daemon) the open screen asked about 650–770 times
a minute — `/v1/history?range=all` 255 times, five components asking that
one question — and on a copy of the owner's 2.2 GB database the daemon spent
36 s of CPU a minute answering (bench, 2026-10-13). `ui/src/lib/refresh.ts`
makes it one refresh:

- **One pace.** Now provides `LivePace` (`NOW_LIVE_MS`, 2 s); every `useApi`
  under it ticks at the slower of that and its own `liveMs`, and the tick is
  one shared timer per pace, so the sections ask together. All-time figures
  (history, glance, daily) take `SLOW_LIVE_MS`, 10 s. A live tick that asked
  within a hook's interval keeps the interval from asking again.
- **One question, one request.** Hooks asking the same question at about the
  same time share one request (`shared`, keyed by `share`, which defaults to
  the `cache` name); a button's `refresh()` always asks the daemon itself.
- **Nobody looking, nothing asked.** `active: false` holds a hook's ticks and
  intervals and asks once when it turns true. All time (its fold), At a
  glance (its own fold) and Projects pass whether they are open and on
  screen (`usePanelOpen`, `useOnScreen`); a first answer is always asked, as
  it decides whether the panel is there at all. Hidden, nothing is asked
  (above).
- **Seeds read once.** The pulse and the activity feed seed from history
  keyed on the set of sessions, not their order: sorted by the newest event,
  two busy sessions swapped places every few seconds and each swap read every
  track's 2,000 newest events again. A track already seeded follows the live
  feed.
- **No double ask on opening.** A tick store nobody reads is caught up when
  read, and `useApi` refetches only on a tick that moved since it mounted:
  every screen opened after any event used to ask each question twice.

After: about 100 requests a minute (`/v1/history?range=all` 6), and 17 s of
daemon CPU a minute on the same database copy. Most of what is left is the
sessions list, today's summary and the plan-window stop at the 2 s pace.

The sidebar's and tabs' hot-process flame (`HotMark`) is a static mark read
through `useSessionHot`, a string: it draws again only when what it says
changes, where every row's mark used to redraw on each 5 s resources sample.

## Charts

The daily-cost bars on **Cost** and **History** answer on hover and on focus: the bar highlights, its date label brightens, and the panel header swaps the range total for that day's cost, tokens and session count. Reported by an early user as "I expected something to happen and nothing did" — the bars previously carried only a native `title` tooltip, which is a real answer that arrives after a browser delay, in the OS font, detached from the chart. Long enough to read as broken.

Bars are `<button>`s so keyboard users get the same readout via focus, and a zero-cost day still draws a visible sliver rather than vanishing. Implementation: `ui/src/components/BarChart.tsx`, unit-tested.

## Memory (what Claude actually said)

For a large share of sessions the deliverable is not the diff but the conclusion — "this is done, but I could not verify X; check with the team and then we finish it". Caprock always stored that paragraph and never showed it: the timeline rendered a 200-character slice of it on one line, so people copied it into a notepad or lost it to scrollback.

- **Memory** (top nav) searches Claude's prose across every session. It opens with one paragraph saying what it is for — find a conclusion when you remember it but not the session, open the moment it was written, and (with *Memory between sessions* on) the store a new session in the same folder is handed — because a tester could not tell why the tab existed (2026-10-12). Browsing shows substantive answers only, because most rows are one-line asides that would bury the conclusions; a search shows everything, since the user asked for those words. Each result names its repo and links to the session.
- **A session's Answers tab** lists that session's prose, newest first, with the short remarks collapsed behind a toggle. It read from every assistant turn on the machine until 2026-10-04 — the planner preferred the kind index to the session's own — which on the owner's database cost 3.3 s cold for a session of 21 turns; it is 20 ms on the session index. The same day it was found to show one line of a long Russian conversation: the prose had never been stored (see [03-contracts.md](03-contracts.md), parser v4). Prose wraps anywhere (`Prose`, `overflow-wrap: anywhere`): one long URL in a reply used to set the page's width.
- **Drop into Second Brain.** Each answer carries *Drop into Second Brain*, which keeps it in Second Brain (§ Second Brain).
- **The timeline expands into prose.** Expanding a `turn.assistant` or `turn.user` row now shows the full text with its formatting intact, and keeps the raw event behind a `raw event` disclosure rather than as the only thing on offer.

Long answers collapse at 600 characters with an exact character count on the expander (counted in characters, not bytes — the bug that started this counted bytes). Newlines are preserved: the structure of a summary is most of its readability. Subagent sidechains never appear — see [03-contracts.md](03-contracts.md) for why that filter lives in SQL rather than in the caller.

## Cost & Burn

- Burn-rate now ($/hr equivalent, tokens/min) per agent and total.
- **Plan limits**: two signals. (1) The daemon consumes Claude Code's `StopFailure` hook and records each rate-limit/overloaded/billing event into `throttle_observations`; the Cost screen shows the *count* per range — a measured post-hoc fact. (2) The `caprock statusline` command feeds Claude Code's `rate_limits` (5h/7d window `used_percentage` + `resets_at`, Pro/Max only) to the daemon; the Cost screen shows the current window ("5h: 23% · resets 15:40", threshold-coloured), and Now gives it the full-width **Plan limits** panel directly under Today: one ring gauge per window, grouped by agent, the ring moving from green to amber at 60% and to red above 85%, with the reset as a clock and a countdown ("resets 04:15 PM · in 1 h 39 m"). It was once a single cell in the Today row, and before that a panel above it that made two small percentages look like a headline; drawn as gauges it answers "can I keep going" at a glance, which is the question that stops the work. A window whose reset clock cannot be believed makes its percentage old too, so its gauge is drawn grey and says "reset time stale — not a live reading" rather than counting down to a time that is not real and an "at current pace" forecast **only** when the measured slope is rising and would hit the limit before the window resets — otherwise the fact alone, never a guess. When there is one it is a sentence in plain words — "At this pace you'll hit 100% around 19:10 — about 1 h before it resets." — from the daemon's `limit_at`, amber, red only when 100% is under half an hour away, and a thin hairline on the ring's outer edge from the used arc to the end. The rest of the ring is an empty neutral track: the filled arc alone is what is used. It used to draw the forecast as a dashed red arc over the whole remainder, so a window at 9% read as mostly red (owner feedback, 2026-10-04). Under the gauges one line says what these are — "Your plan's limits, per 5 hours and per week — not Caprock's. At 100% the agent pauses until the reset." — with the source and the forecast rule behind **more** and on hover. Since 2026-10-11 each agent is a card — its character, its plan's name, then **5 hours** and **Week** side by side, each a ring with the percentage and two short lines (*resets 07:30 PM*, *in 1 h 1 min*). A window an agent did not report keeps its place as an empty ring saying *not reported by Codex*, so both cards have the same shape (owner: the three-ring layout was "asymmetric", the sentences too long and too small). While no plan is set, a banner under the cards asks *Which plan are you on?*, says why — Caprock can't detect it, and the plan is what Cost weighs the API-price figure against — and opens the plan list in place (owner, 2026-10-11: "always ask with a banner to set your plan, and explain why"). The absolute plan threshold is not emitted by Claude Code and is deliberately not shown. See [03-contracts.md § Statusline](03-contracts.md#statusline).
- **The limits say it in words** (2026-10-04). The owner followed a "95%"
  alert to Cost and could not tell what any of it meant. Each window now reads
  as a sentence beside its ring — "5-hour window: 97% used. Resets 15:20 — in
  9 min. At 100%, Claude Code pauses until then." — with the countdown in
  minutes under an hour and the weekday on a weekly reset. The Claude heading
  names the plan the user picked ("Claude Max 5×"), never one guessed from the
  figures. One line says what these are (Anthropic caps a Pro or Max plan per
  rolling 5-hour window and per week; Anthropic's limits, not Caprock's, read
  from Claude Code's status line), and when a live window passes 85% a note
  says what to do: wait for the reset, or switch to Codex when Codex has room.
  Now and Cost show the same panel (`PlanLimitsPanel`); on Cost it spans the
  grid and carries the anchor the alert links to (`#/cost?section=limits`).
  The alert itself reads "Claude's 5-hour limit: 97% used — resets 15:20, in 9
  min; at 100% Claude Code pauses until then". The Claude desktop app's own
  reading is never shown beside these: it is sampled only while the app runs,
  and a stale 10% next to a live 97% is the confusion this ends. It stays on
  Status, marked as last seen.
- **Codex's plan limits** (`codex_rate_limits`) appear in the same Plan limits panel, under a **Codex** heading below a **Claude Code** one — the two plans share nothing, so one unlabelled pair of rows would read as one limit. The heading carries **"as of"** the time Codex wrote the sample into its transcript, because the figure is not live: with no Codex session running it can be hours old. A `prolite` ChatGPT plan has a weekly window and no 5-hour one; its 5-hour slot is drawn empty, *not reported by Codex*. No forecast, ever; the panel's footnote says so. Without Codex data the panel is unchanged, headings included. A reset more than a day away shows its weekday ("resets Sat 14:03"), for both agents, because a weekly window resetting "at 14:03" does not say which day. On Now the two agents sit side by side, each under its own heading, and stack at phone width. See [03-contracts.md § Rate-limit snapshots](03-contracts.md#rate-limit-snapshots-ddl-migration-0005) and [19-codex.md](19-codex.md#plan-limits).
- **Hermes Agent** (`HermesSpend.tsx`, from the summary's `hermes`), a panel under Totals that appears only when the range has Hermes turns ([ADR-069](08-decisions.md)). Four tiles: *Hermes total* ("inside the total above"), *Provider-reported* (actual: what the provider billed), *Hermes estimate* (from Hermes's own price list), and *Background* — the spend nobody watched as it ran, Hermes's cron jobs, gateway chats and one-shot runs, with the split by source on the sub-line ("cron jobs $0.50 · telegram $0.25") and what counts as background on hover. The corner says *Hermes's own figures*: Caprock never prices Hermes's tokens. A line under the tiles counts turns Hermes could not price (amber; not in the figures) and turns on a subscription route (counted at $0).
- Cache hit-rate, model mix, per-project and per-task cost. This whole screen is Caprock's DNA.
- **What it went on** (`ui/src/components/WorkMix.tsx`) sits beside the model mix and the project list as the third cut of one question — how much, on which model, where, and *on what*. One row per kind of work each turn did, ranked by cost, with the rule on hover. The labels are the design: they must survive "is that true of every turn in this row?", so a turn that called no tool is labelled **"no tool call"** and never "conversation", "thinking" or "planning" — the capture records which tools ran, not what a turn was doing (rule 6). The three panels share one grid and collapse from three columns to two to one as the viewport narrows. When more than 1% of the range's tool calls could not be matched to the turn that paid for them, the panel says so: those calls inflate the "no tool call" row and understate every other. See [03-contracts.md § Work attribution DDL](03-contracts.md#work-attribution-ddl-migration-0014).
- **Every hero cost figure carries its basis on the tile**, from one shared component (`ui/src/components/CostBasis.tsx`) so the four screens that show a large dollar number cannot drift apart. The wording follows the stated plan, because there is no single honest sentence: on a flat plan (Pro/Max/Team seat) the figure is what the same work would have cost through the API and is explicitly **not a bill**; on metered billing (API key, Bedrock, Vertex) it **is** approximately the bill, and calling it an equivalent would be the same error reversed; with no plan stated it names the basis and claims nothing further. Shown to five readers, a bare `$586` was taken for an amount owed by three — and the previous subtitle, `API-equivalent`, is internal jargon that explained nothing to a first-time reader, so it appears nowhere user-facing. The longer form says **"At API list prices"**, not "At Anthropic list prices": Codex, Gemini and DeepSeek turns are priced from their own vendors' lists into the same totals, and the Cost screen's model table showed `gpt-5.6-sol` directly under a subtitle crediting Anthropic. `caprock report` follows the same rule — its caveat names every vendor whose list priced the total ("Anthropic and OpenAI list prices") and its headline says "of coding-agent usage" instead of "of Claude Code" once any priced model is not Claude.

## History (Caprock heritage)

- Cross-session stats: cost per project/day/model, tool usage distribution, avg task duration, success rate (tasks passing verification).
- Exportable; the "wow" screen for blog posts and screenshots.
- Shipped in v0.2.0.
- **Two tiles say precisely what they count**, because the obvious reading of each was wrong. "Active days" counts days on which something happened, not days on which a session was opened — the latter read 21 where 32 days had work in them, since a session that ran twelve days contributed one. "Files touched" is summed per session, so a file edited in three sessions counts three times (1,703 against 1,502 distinct paths on the author's database); the subtitle says so rather than letting the number pass for a count of distinct files.
- **Laid out like the site** (2026-10-04), in its reading style: the money
  first and largest on the left, then **a day** (cost ÷ active days) and **a
  session** (cost ÷ sessions) — both exact divisions of two measured totals
  of the same range, never a forecast — then the cache hit rate, with the
  counts at reading size below. The three money figures are short — whole
  dollars under $1,000, then *$16.8k*, *$168k*, *$1.2M* (`fmtUSDShort`) —
  with the exact sum on hover: cents on $16,817.45 were noise (owner,
  2026-10-11). **Daily cost** follows, with its own period (30d, 90d, 180d,
