  all — calendar days cut from what the range loaded) and a **×2** height
  toggle, both kept per viewer (`caprock-daily-span`, `caprock-daily-tall`);
  its total reads in thousands to three places, *$16.818k* (`fmtUSDk`).
  **Top projects** come next, as a donut beside the exact table; each row
  leads with the donut's colour as a square and its share large, so a row
  is matched to its arc without crossing the table (owner, 2026-10-11). Tool usage and
  the model mix are donuts with the tail grouped as "other" (six tools, five
  models), or tables, by a **Charts | Numbers** switch in the range row,
  remembered per browser (`caprock-lifetime-view`). The full tool list stays
  under the tool donut, because "other" is exactly where a reader wants the
  names.
- **A tool's row opens in place** (`ToolDrill`, 2026-10-04) into what its
  calls were about, for the range above: Bash by command, Read and Edit by
  file, WebFetch by domain, WebSearch by query, an MCP tool by its action
  (`GET /v1/tools/drill`). The groups, their calls and a share bar are free.
  Output, failure rate and an eight-column trend per group, and the hints,
  are Premium: without a licence those columns are blurred placeholder
  glyphs — never a number — the strongest hint is shown in full, and a
  **Premium** chip opens `PremiumModal` with `feature="drill"`. The daemon
  leaves the figures out, so the blur hides nothing that was sent.

## Week

`#/week` (`?start=YYYY-MM-DD` to open a given week). A card of what this
machine's agents did in seven days, made to be posted, in two layouts at their
export sizes: **landscape 1200×675** (X, LinkedIn) and **portrait 1080×1350**
(Instagram, Telegram). Free, like everything that describes one machine; a
card across a team's machines belongs to the team tier and is not built here.

- **The card is the screen.** It is drawn at full size from theme tokens —
  graphite on dark, paper on light — and scaled to fit, and **Download PNG** /
  **Copy image** draw that same node with `html-to-image` (MIT, pinned,
  bundled). The fonts embedded are the bundled faces, read from the same
  origin; nothing is fetched off the machine.
- **The headline is the strongest measured thing, never a zero:** merged pull
  requests, else opened, else commits, else ≈lines, else sessions. The tally
  under it carries the other non-zero counts; the side column leads with
  money (list price, ≈cost per merged PR, share spent re-reading context).
- **Who did what** is drawn with the agent characters
  (`ui/src/components/Characters.tsx`): Claude Code's main threads, its
  subagents, and each other agent with its own sub-agents folded in. One line
  each — CI wait for Claude Code when it was measured, else PRs or sessions.
- **Callouts:** the longest loop (by the detector's rule; "≈" on its context
  cost) and the biggest session's share of the week. Portrait adds the PRs
  opened per day, or cost per day when no PR was opened.
- **Privacy:** no repository, path, prompt or session title is on the card;
  the endpoint does not send them. The footer says where the figures come from
  and what "≈" and "merged" mean.

Weeks are seven local days; the arrows step a week, the date label opens a
date picker, and the default ends today. Contract:
[03-contracts.md § The Week](03-contracts.md#the-week-get-v1week).

## Sharing figures

**One figure, said big** (owner, 2026-10-10: the card was "not noticeable,
not attractive — make it something people want to share", translated). It
used to be drawn to look like the dashboard — eight tiles over two
breakdowns, on the theory that people share a screen they recognise. In a
feed, at the size a card is seen, it read as a spreadsheet: sixteen numbers
are none. The Figures card (`ShareCard.tsx`, a 1200×630 canvas, the size
every social preview crops to) now tells one story at a glance:

- **The hero.** The period's cost at API list prices, whole dollars above
  $100 ("$1,290"), fitted to the width, in the accent. Under it, what it is:
  "of Claude Code this week", naming the one or two agents that did the work
  ("of Claude Code and Codex this week") or "AI coding agents" for three or
  more, and then *at API list prices — not a bill*.
- **The multiple, when it is honest.** With a flat plan set (Settings) and a
  week or a month chosen, the hero is the multiple of the plan's fee for
  that stretch: "28×" over "my $200/mo Max plan", the dollars on the line
  below. Not for a day (a day's share of a monthly fee is not a thing anyone
  pays), not for all time (Caprock does not know how long the subscription
  has run), not below 1.5×, and not on metered billing, where the dollars
  are close to the bill. Never "saved" — the same rule as Plan value.
- **Two or three facts** under a hairline: sessions, tokens, cache hit. A
  fact with nothing measured is left off.
- **The agent mix**: a bar of each agent's share of the cost (subagents
  folded into the agent that ran them, the fifth and later as "other") and a
  legend with each agent's mark (`agentMarkPath`, the same paths as the
  app's glyphs) and percentage. From `GET /v1/glance?range=`.
- **The brand and the call**: the caprock mark and wordmark top left, the
  period and its dates top right ("THIS WEEK · 4 OCT – 10 OCT 2026"; all
  time says its active days), a large faint triangle behind the figure,
  and at the foot *What's yours?* beside `caprock.dev`.

**Two looks, chosen in the sheet.** Dark (graphite) or Paper (caprock.dev's
cream #efe7d6, ink #2b251c, amber #8a4f0c, with the button orange #eb9a3e in
the glow). Fixed values rather than the screen's tokens: a card is a picture
that leaves the machine and must look the same whatever the dashboard's tone.
The default follows the theme (Paper on light), and a choice is remembered
per browser (`caprock-share-look`). The Story card takes the same look
(`.wk-look-dark` / `.wk-look-paper` in `WeekCard.css`).

**Deliberately absent**: the instantaneous burn rate, which read $7.33 one
minute and $33.54 the next while a card lives in a feed for weeks; model
names; and anything that names a project or a session.

**What must never appear on it**: a path separator. Agent names are the only
names on the card, and a slash on an image somebody is about to post is
indistinguishable from a repository path; a test asserts none is drawn.

The caveat — *at API list prices — not a bill* — travels with the figure. A
dollar amount posted without it reads as a bill someone paid. It was lost once
during a rewrite and the test is what caught it.

**Two controls, because there are two jobs.** `ShareCard` is always in the ALL
TIME panel: whether someone's numbers are worth posting is their call, and a
person who wants to share should not wait for permission. `ShareNudge` is the
push, and waits for an occasion it can name in the reader's own numbers — a
round number crossed, the first full week, or a week a fifth clear of every
week before it. That last threshold matters: while usage grows, *highest ever*
is true nearly every week, and a prompt that fires weekly is one people stop
reading. Rate-limited to once a week and dismissible; rules in
`ui/src/lib/shareprompt.ts`. A third entry point sits in the header, because
the panel button is only reachable from Now.

**The dialog offers two things and names where each one goes.** It was three
paragraphs of caveats above a button labelled "Share…", and the owner could not
tell what pressing it would do; an ellipsis is not a destination. The privacy
line sits below the buttons — it answers "is this safe to post", which only
arises once "what happens if I press it" is settled.

**The native share sends the file alone.** `navigator.share({files, text})`
looks like a courtesy, but two payloads leave the receiving app to decide what
they mean and several resolve it as two items — macOS's Copy produced the card
twice. The card already carries every figure and the caveat, so the caption was
never load-bearing.

**Drawing and locking are separate states.** One flag doing both jobs meant
that clearing it early — so the label would stop saying "drawing…" while the OS
share sheet sat open — re-enabled the button underneath that sheet, and a
second press drew a second card. `drawing` labels; `locked` outlasts it.

**Two styles: Figures and Story.** Figures is the card above. Story is the
Week screen's card, its foot *caprock.dev · What's yours?* opposite
*measured locally · ≈ = estimate · API list prices, not a bill* — the headline ("My agents shipped 129 PRs this week."),
the money beside it, who did what with their characters, the longest loop and
the biggest session — at 1200×675 or 1080×1350, for whichever period is
chosen. It reads `GET /v1/week?period=` and speaks in the period's words:
"today", "this week", "this month" (the last 30 days, with the dates on the
card) and "— all time". A day has no bar strip; a month's bars are weeks and
all time's are calendar months, summed, never averaged. Tiles with nothing in
them are left off, and every estimate keeps its "≈". The style and the size
are remembered per browser.

**Opening it shows a card at once.** It used to start from nothing: four
summary requests, the slowest several seconds on a large database, and an empty
box saying "drawing…" until all of them answered. Now the last figures for each
period are kept (in memory, and in localStorage for the next tab;
`ui/src/lib/sharecache.ts`) and drawn the moment the dialog opens, marked "from
14:03 · updating…" while the current ones are fetched behind them. With nothing
kept, the dialog draws the card's outline and ticks off each read as it
answers — the totals, which agents did the work, the plan — with the seconds
elapsed. A Figures card reads only its own period: one summary (history for
all time), one glance and the settings, not the four ranges the eight-tile
card needed. Hovering the Share
button starts the default card's requests, and the preview and the save share
one round of them. **Saving or sending always waits for the current figures**:
a card that leaves the machine never carries an earlier reading.

### The cache hit rate says what it means

The figure was shown alone and coloured amber below 90%, so everything above
that read identically — 99% and 91% looked the same, and neither said
anything, on a measurement that runs from 6% to 99.6% across real sessions
with a median of 93.6%.

One word now sits beside it: **outstanding** at 99% and above, **excellent**
from 98, **very good** from 96, **good** from 90, **ok** from 85, **low** below
that. The top of the range was one step where the eye reads several — 98% and
99% are not the same session and both said "outstanding" — so the two middle
words split the range a reader cares about and `good` moved down to make room.
The bands are set against the observed spread rather than picked for roundness,
and everything from `good` up shares one colour: the word carries the
distinction, not the hue.

**A caveat for whoever touches these next.** The distribution has moved a long
way since the bands were first set. Measured when the middle words were added,
the median hit rate is **99.98%** and **59% of sessions** land in the top band —
where the original intent was about one session in nine. So `excellent` and
`very good` describe roughly 3% of sessions each while `outstanding` describes
the majority. The bands are honest about what they measure; whether 99%+ still
earns a word is a separate question, left open rather than decided here. The
alternatives considered were moving the top band to 99.9% (where the mass
actually sits) or showing no word at all above 99%, on the grounds that a
number that high speaks for itself.

Three rules hold it in place.

- **It describes a state, not a performance.** The cache is Claude Code's
  doing and Caprock only reads it, so nothing here congratulates anyone.
- **`low` is a fact, not a verdict.** A short session has little to reuse; a
  low rate is the honest result of the work, not a fault, which is why it is
  not called *bad* or coloured red.
- **Nothing moves.** The dashboard sits open all day, and a flash that
  celebrates a good number is charming once and an irritation by the
  afternoon. Motion means live data on this dashboard and nothing else.

The middle band is deliberately uncoloured: colour means something here, and
an ordinary reading is not something. Bands live in `ui/src/lib/cachelevel.ts`
and are rendered by `CacheStat` — one component for the three screens that
show the figure, because three copies of a product decision drift.

## Visual direction (v0 tokens)

Utility-first "mission control" aesthetic — the opposite pole from Munder Difflin's toy-office charm; the personality comes from density and precision, not decoration.

- **Theme:** nine palettes, dark by default (ops-room; Dark Pastel since 2026-10-13) — see § Themes below. Every one is the same semantic tokens in `ui/src/design/tokens.css` redefined under its own selector, so every screen follows along. Dark background near-black, panels one step lighter, 1px borders, no gradients.
- **Color is semantic only:** green = working/verified, amber = idle/waiting, red = loop/error/over-budget, blue = info/links, neutral gray for everything else. Brand accent (single hue, pick once at T7) used only for interactive elements. No decorative color anywhere — if a color doesn't encode state, it's gray.
- **Type:** UI in a neutral sans (Inter or system stack); **all numbers, costs, IDs, and terminal content in monospace with tabular numerals** — columns of costs must align.
- **Density:** compact rows, generous data-per-screen; sparklines over big charts on Now; big charts live on Cost/History only.
- **Motion:** none except live-data updates (value ticks, new-event flash ≤150ms). No spinners longer than 300ms — show last-known data with a staleness dot instead.

These tokens live in `ui/src/design/tokens.css` as the single source of truth; every component derives from them (same discipline Munder Difflin's DESIGN.md enforces, different taste).

## Themes

Nine palettes (owner, 2026-10-11: plain names, and more of them; Dark Pastel added 2026-10-12, Sunlight 2026-10-15). One table
in `ui/src/lib/themes.ts` (`THEMES`) holds each one's id, name, side, the
value of its sub-attribute, its panel colour, its picker swatch, the app
terminal it takes under Match app and the dark terminal the dashboard takes;
the picker, the cycle, `<meta name="theme-color">`, the terminal and the
pre-paint script in `ui/index.html` all read it. A palette is `data-theme`
(light or dark — every light/dark rule keys on it) plus `data-dark` (dark) or
`data-tone` (light); `data-glow` marks Dark Side and Light Side.

| Palette     | id            | Attributes           | Panel     | Accent    | Was      |
| ----------- | ------------- | -------------------- | --------- | --------- | -------- |
| Dark Pastel | `dark-pastel` | `data-dark=pastel`   | `#212221` | `#a3b6c9` | —        |
| Dark Warm   | `dark-warm`   | `data-dark=graphite` | `#211f1d` | `#feb157` | Graphite |
| Dark Cold   | `dark-cold`   | `data-dark=slate`    | `#1f2023` | `#86a9dc` | Slate    |
| Dark Side   | `dark-side`   | `data-dark=side`     | `#15111d` | `#b45cff` | —        |
| Light       | `light`       | `data-tone=white`    | `#ffffff` | `#99600a` | White    |
| Light Cold  | `light-cold`  | `data-tone=cold`     | `#f5f8fc` | `#0057e0` | —        |
| Book        | `book`        | `data-tone=paper`    | `#f4ecdd` | `#8a4f0c` | Paper    |
| Light Side  | `light-side`  | `data-tone=side`     | `#f9f6ef` | `#1763cc` | —        |
| Sunlight    | `sunlight`    | `data-tone=sun`      | `#ffffff` | `#0038b8` | —        |

- **Dark Pastel is the default dark palette** (owner, 2026-10-13: "it came
  out gorgeous", translated), and first in the picker and the cycle; Book
  stays the default light one. `DEFAULT_DARK` in `lib/themes.ts` names it,
  the pre-paint script mirrors it, and `tokens.css` paints its block for a
  dark `<html>` with no or an unknown `data-dark` (a `:where()` fallback
  that excludes the other dark subs) — so the first paint, the phone, the
  tray popover and the app's fallback page are pastel before any script
  runs. Dark Warm stays the `@theme` base the other palettes override. The
  PWA manifest and `<meta name="theme-color">` carry its colours; the
  screenshot scripts shoot it for the dark pass.
- **Dark Cold and Light Cold have no orange anywhere.** Warn is a cool yellow
  (`#d9c76a`) in Dark Cold and an olive (`#7a6800`) in Light Cold. Light
  Cold is a blue-grey ground (`#edf1f7`) with blue-grey borders and an
  electric blue accent.
- **Dark Pastel** is a neutral graphite (`#1c1d1c`) with a dusty blue accent
  (`#a3b6c9`) and low-chroma status colours — sage ok, sand warn, dusty rose
  danger, lavender premium — and a terminal in the same muted hues. Two
  testers picked Dark Cold as the calmer dark and still found it "a lot of
  colour", one asking for "neutral and pastel" (2026-10-12, translated). Its
  body ink is 11:1 on panel-2, muted 6.5:1, faint 4.8:1, the accent 6.9:1
  and every mark over 6:1; `theme.test.ts` asserts body ≥ 7:1, muted, faint
  and accent ≥ 4.5:1 and marks ≥ 3:1 on all three surfaces.
- **Selected text** keeps its own colour on a 20% wash of the accent:
  `--color-selection` per palette in `tokens.css`, written as rgba (not a
  `color-mix()` of the accent) and read by the one `::selection` rule. Body
  ink on it is 8:1 or more in every palette (worst Dark Pastel 8.0:1, Dark
  Warm 8.5:1), muted ink 4.3:1 or more (worst Dark Warm 4.29:1), on bg and
  panel alike. It was 35% until 2026-10-14, where Dark Pastel's body ink was
  5.7:1 and its muted ink 3.4:1.
- **Dark Cold's accent is one step softer** since the same day: `#6ea8fe` →
  `#86a9dc` (strong `#9cc4ff` → `#a9c3e6`), 6.2:1 on panel-2.
- **Sunlight** is for working in bright light (owner, 2026-10-15,
  translated: "a screen mode for when you sit in the sun"). Glare washes out
  whatever sits near the ground, so it has no faint greys. Black ink on pure
  white (bg and panel `#ffffff`, panel-2 `#f0f0f0`), muted `#1f1f1f` and faint
  `#3b3b3b`. Every text token is 7:1 or more on all three surfaces, measured
  2026-10-15: body 21:1 (18.4 on panel-2), faint 11.2 (9.8), the accent
  `#0038b8` 9.3 (8.2) and as a filled button's label 9.3, and the status
  colours, all saturated and apart from each other: ok `#005c19` 8.3 (7.2,
  the lowest), warn `#6b3d00` 9.2 (8.0), danger `#9e0010` 8.5 (7.5), merged
  `#6c1ab0` 8.7 (7.6), premium `#3d16c4` 10.0 (8.7). Borders are `#767676`
  (4.5:1) and `#3b3b3b`. The panel shadow is a 1px outline, since a shadow is
  lost in glare. The text is one weight heavier (500, the variable faces take
  it) with macOS's thinning antialiasing off. In the app the hairlines are
  the same dark greys and the sidebar is opaque, so the desktop does not
  wash through it. Its terminal (`SUNLIGHT_TERMINAL_THEME`) is black on the
  white panel. The eight normal ANSI colours are 7:1 or more — the yellow a
  dark ochre `#755000` 7.2, the cyan a deep teal `#00616b` 7.2, the lowest
  red 7.18 — and the bright eight 5.3:1 or more (lowest bright white
  `#6b6b6b`, 5.33). On a light ground "white" is a text colour too, so it is
  a grey. Selected text is 13.9:1 in the terminal and on the page.
  `theme.test.ts` and `termthemes.test.ts` measure every one of these
  numbers. The dashboard terminal stays dark (Dark Cold's).
- **Light Side** is a pale sand ground (`#f2eee5`) with a blue accent and a
  green for success (`#137a3f`); its swatch shows both.
- **Dark Side** is near-black with a hint of violet (`#0d0b14`) and a neon
  purple accent (`#b45cff`), with neon cyan (`#38d4ff`) for premium (owner,
  2026-10-12: the first version's crimson was "very red"; "neon, like the
  purple sabers", translated). Danger is its own red (`#ff4d5e`), apart
  from the accent; warn stays amber
  (`#e8a03c`), and a filled destructive button is drawn there as an outline
  while an accent one is filled.
- **The glow** (`data-glow`, Dark Side and Light Side) is a soft neon on three
  things only: the focused field (`.input:focus`), the filled accent button
  and the sidebar's primary action, and the top edge of the tab in front.
  Never on text.
- **Stored as** `caprock-theme-mode` (the id) beside `caprock-theme` (light
  or dark, still written: the tray window and the screenshot scripts read
  it). `resolveMode` takes the mode while it agrees with `caprock-theme`;
  otherwise the keys from before the rename decide — `caprock-dark-tone`
  `slate` is Dark Cold, else the default; `caprock-light-tone` `white` is
  Light, else Book — and with nothing kept the OS preference picks the side
  (Dark Pastel or Book). Only `caprock-theme-mode` counts as a choice: the
  old `graphite` tone was the default, written whether or not anyone picked
  it, so it moves to Dark Pastel with the default (2026-10-13); `slate` was
  always picked and stays Dark Cold; a Dark Warm chosen in the picker has
  the mode key and stays. `index.html` mirrors it before first paint;
  `theme.test.ts` runs that script against `resolveMode` for every case.
- **Contrast** (WCAG, measured 2026-10-11 against bg, panel and panel-2):
  body text, faint text included, is 4.5:1 or more in every palette; marks
  (ok, warn, danger, premium) are 3:1 or more. Lowest text ratio per palette:
  Dark Warm 4.56 (faint on panel-2), Dark Cold 4.55 (faint on panel-2), Dark
  Side 4.90 (faint on panel-2), Light 4.58 (accent on panel-2), Light Cold
  5.00 (faint on panel-2), Book 5.03 (faint on panel-2), Light Side 4.53
  (accent on panel-2). Lowest mark: Light's ok on panel-2, 3.01. To get
  there the pass lifted Dark Warm's faint ink (`#837f78` → `#8f8b84`) and
  Dark Cold's (`#7d8088` → `#8b8e96`) and premium (`#5a68f6`), and
  darkened Light's faint ink (`#8a8479` → `#6c675e`) and accent (`#b8730d`
  → `#99600a`). One mark is under 3:1 and stays: Book's filled button
  (`#eb9a3e`) against its panel is 1.94:1 — it is the site's button, and its
  dark label is 7.44:1.

## Narration map (T7)

The narration is a pure function `(tool_name, tool_input, recent history) → phrase`. Baseline phrases:

| Tool                     | Phrase                                      |
| ------------------------ | ------------------------------------------- |
| `Read`                   | reading `<basename>`                        |
| `Edit` / `Write`         | editing `<basename>` / writing `<basename>` |
| `Bash`                   | running `<first token(s) of command>`       |
| `Grep` / `Glob`          | searching for `<pattern>`                   |
| `WebFetch` / `WebSearch` | fetching `<host>` / searching the web       |
| `TodoWrite`              | planning — N/M done                         |
| `Task` / `Agent`         | delegating to a subagent                    |
| `mcp__<server>__<tool>`  | `<tool>` via `<server>`                     |
| unknown                  | using `<tool_name>`                         |

Repeats append an ordinal ("— 2nd attempt") when the same tool+input recurs; ≥ K repeats flip the health badge to `looping?`.

### Mail folder cleanup

Spam offers **Clean spam → Trash** and Trash offers **Clean Trash…** in the list header, outside search results. Each opens an inline review for the selected account or all accounts. Age presets are any age and older than 7, 30 or 90 days; Trash defaults to 30 days. Age refers to message date, not time in Trash. Optional spam-score thresholds are 50, 75 and 90, explicitly described as Caprock's score rather than a probability; provider-only and AI-only classifications do not satisfy that filter.

The review counts every matching synced message, including unloaded list pages, before enabling **Move N to Trash** or **Delete N forever**. Trash warns that deletion is permanent on the mail server. Changing a filter invalidates the previous count; execution uses only the reviewed snapshot. Errors refresh the message list and require a fresh preview before retrying. See the cleanup endpoints in [03-contracts.md](03-contracts.md).
